2.15
:

\begin{split}{\bm{{\mu}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}}&={\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{y}|{}\hat{x}}^{-1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}-{\bm{{\mathit{c}}}}}}\right)+{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}^{-1}}{\bm{{\mu}}_{{}\hat{x}}}}}\right]\\
&=\bm{\mathbf{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}-{\bm{{\mathit{c}}}}}}\right)+{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}^{-1}}}}\right){\bm{{\mu}}_{{}\hat{x}}}\\
&=\bm{\mathbf{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}-{\bm{{\mathit{c}}}}}}\right)+{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{y}|{}\hat{x}}^{-1}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{y}|{}\hat{x}}^{-1}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}+{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}^{-1}}}}\right){\bm{{\mu}}_{{}\hat{x}}}\\
&=\bm{\mathbf{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}-{\bm{{\mathit{c}}}}}}\right)-\bm{\mathbf{K}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{{\mu}}_{{}\hat{x}}}+{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{y}|{}\hat{x}}^{-1}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}+{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}^{-1}}}}\right){\bm{{\mu}}_{{}\hat{x}}}\\
&=\bm{\mathbf{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}-{\bm{{\mathit{c}}}}}}\right)-\bm{\mathbf{K}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{{\mu}}_{{}\hat{x}}}+{\bm{{\mu}}_{{}\hat{x}}}\\
&={\bm{{\mu}}_{{}\hat{x}}}+\bm{\mathbf{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{{\mu}}_{{}\hat{x}}}+{\bm{{\mathit{c}}}}}}\right)}}\right].\end{split}

(2.24)

Intuitively, the recognition mean is the source mean, adjusted by a “correction factor.”
The correction measures the difference between the emission as observed (
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}
) and as predicted by the source mean (
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{{\mu}}_{{}\hat{x}}}+{\bm{{\mathit{c}}}}
), weighted by the precision of the emission (
\bm{\mathbf{K}}
).

Despite appearances, matrix inversions are still required in Eq.
2.24
; they occur in the calculation of
\bm{\mathbf{K}}
.
Consulting its definition, Eq.
2.15
, we see one inversion of the emission covariance, and another in the computation of the recognition covariance, albeit in the space of emissions via Eq.
2.23
.
With a little work, one of these inversions can be eliminated:

\begin{split}\bm{\mathbf{K}}={\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{y}|{}\hat{x}}^{-1}}&=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}-{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}+{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{y}|{}\hat{x}}}}}\right)^{-1}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{y}|{}\hat{x}}^{-1}}\\
&={\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{\mathbf{I}}-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}+{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{y}|{}\hat{x}}}}}\right)^{-1}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{y}|{}\hat{x}}^{-1}}\\
&={\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}+{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{y}|{}\hat{x}}})^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}+{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{y}|{}\hat{x}}}}}\right)-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{y}|{}\hat{x}}^{-1}}\\
&={\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}+{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{y}|{}\hat{x}}})^{-1}.\end{split}

(2.25)

Thus in fact only one matrix need be inverted in computing the entire recognition distribution, namely
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}+{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{y}|{}\hat{x}}}
—which, by Eq.
2.17
, is just the marginal covariance,
{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{y}}}
.
So we can also write

\bm{\mathbf{K}}={\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{y}}^{-1}}.

(2.26)

To make the complete calculation more perspicuous, we collect here the marginal cumulants from Eqs.
2.16
and
2.17
:

\begin{split}{\bm{{\mu}}_{{}\hat{y}}}&=\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{{\mu}}_{{}\hat{x}}}+{\bm{{\mathit{c}}}},\\
{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{y}}}&=\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}+{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{y}|{}\hat{x}}},\end{split}

(2.27)

along with this alternative (“Woodburied”) computation of the recognition cumulants:

\begin{split}\bm{\mathbf{K}}&={\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{y}}^{-1}}\\
{\bm{{\mu}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}}&={\bm{{\mu}}_{{}\hat{x}}}+\bm{\mathbf{K}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}-{\bm{{\mu}}_{{}\hat{y}}}}}\right)\\
{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}}&={\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}}-\bm{\mathbf{K}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}}\\
&={\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}}-\bm{\mathbf{K}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{y}}}\bm{\mathbf{K}}^{\text{T}},\end{split}

(2.28)

where on the last line we have rewritten the recognition covariance yet again using Eq.
2.26
.

2.1.3
Sparse coding

To motivate “sparse” codes, we consider more complicated marginal distributions of data, focusing in particular on the distribution of natural images, for which mixture models and factor analyzers are inadequate models.
To see this, we (following Hinton and Ghahramani
[
13
]
) cast these two model types as poles of a continuum of models, from sparsest to densest.
As we saw above, one possible formulation of mixture models interprets the “source” variable,
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}
, as a one-hot vector of length
\mathit{{K}}
(as opposed to a scalar with support in the set
\{1,\ldots,\mathit{{K}}\}
).
We can even think of each element
\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{k}
of
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}
as a separate “unit” in a neural network.
Indeed, the winner-take-all neural networks of the 1980s and ’90s
[
10
, Chapter 9]
can be thought of as (perhaps approximate) recognition models for generative models that are mixtures.
From this perspective, then, the term “mixture” is potentially misleading: the marginal distribution of observations
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}
is indeed a mixture, whence the name, but each individual example
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}}
is
not
mixed.
That is, each
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}}
is generated by just one element of
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}
, and the code for generating it is maximally
sparse
.

At the other extreme, in factor analyzers, all of the “hidden units” (elements of
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}
) typically will have non-zero values, since they are independent and normally distributed.

All
elements of a sample
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}
therefore potentially contribute to a single emission
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}}
.
This is a
dense
code.

To make matters even more concrete, consider a GMM whose emission covariance is fixed across classes, so that the activity of the source variables determines only the mean of the emission.
Then the emissions for the GMM and factor analyzer can be written identically, as a normal distribution about
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}
(cf. Eq.
2.5
and Eq.
2.20
).
That is, both models generate data as a “combination” of basis vectors, the columns of
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}
, but differ in how many columns are allowed to contribute to a single observation.

The statistics of natural scenes.

Both types of model are problematic for complex distributions, like that of natural images.
Gaussian mixture models can indeed approximate any marginal density over
\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}
(Eq.
2.10
) to arbitrary accuracy
[
28
]
: intuitively, a distribution of any shape can be constructed by placing little blobs of probability mass at various locations.
This is essential for the distribution of images, where the presence of (e.g.) lines makes the marginal distribution of (say) triplets of adjacent pixels inexplicable by their mean values or even pairwise correlations alone.
But each such bit of structure must be “explained” with a separate basis vector!
There is no way to explain a letter “X” as a superimposition of two lines; rather, the “X” and the two lines into which it might be decomposed would each require its own basis vector.
More generally, every image (or image patch) that could not be expressed as a Gaussian disturbance of some other image would require another column in
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}
.
Thus the ability to model structure comes at the price of a very large number of mixture components.
It must be emphasized that we believe this price to be too high precisely because we believe the structure in images to be “componential,” explicable in terms of combinations of simpler basis elements.

A factor analyzer, in contrast, can avail itself of all basis vectors at once in attempting to explain or generate an observation, and consequently can in theory recognize an image in terms of its constituent parts.
By the same token, it needs no more hidden units than there are pixels in the images:
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}
can be square, i.e. a complete basis.
However, the marginal distribution of observations under a factor analyzer is just another normal distribution (recall Eq.
2.21
)—a fact that looked helpful from the perspective of calculating with the model, but is fatal for modeling natural images.
This is a consequence of the higher-order structure in images alluded to above.

The existence of such higher-order structure as seen in the figure is not perhaps obvious
a priori
.
After all, the size of the covariance matrix for the model marginal,
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
, scales quadratically in the number of pixels—a lot of representational power to work with!
For example, the covariance matrix of even modestly sized, (256
\times
256)-pixel, images has
256^{2}(256^{2}-1)/2\approx

2\text{\times}{10}^{9}
independent entries.
It would seem that we could represent any distribution with this many parameters.
But in fact, by taking certain symmetries into account, we reduce the number of covariance entries to a linear function of the number of pixels.
The statistics of natural images are, for the most part
6
6

6

Many natural images, as seen by land-dwelling animals, have sky in the upper portion and ground below, which does introduce statistical regularities that depend on absolute position.
Overrepresentation of vertical and (especially) horizontal lines would likewise introduce assymmetries.

, stationary: the correlation between any two pairs of pixels depends on the distance between them but not their absolute locations.
In our example, there are only
256^{2}
distances—from, say, the upper left pixel to any pixel in the image, including itself.
So the covariance cannot encode so much information after all.

Covariance matrices arising from shift-invariant data will in general have this “symmetric Toeplitz” structure.
More specifically, when the shift-invariant data are two-dimensional, like images, the covariance matrix will be block-Toeplitz with Toeplitz blocks
[
8
]
.
Furthermore, if we assume that the covariance drops to zero at some distance within the image, and we ignore edge effects, the covariance matrix can be approximated as block-circulant with circulant blocks, in which case its eigenvectors are the (two-dimensional) discrete Fourier modes, and its eigenvalues constitute the power spectrum
[
8
]
.
7
7

7

Since the eigenvectors of the covariance matrix are the
principal components
of the data (see also Section
6.4.3
), this likewise implies that the Fourier modes are the principal components of shift-invariant data.

In natural images, the power spectrum appears to fall off with frequency
f
at a rate of very nearly
1/f^{2}

[
24
]
.

Thus, natural images can be decorrelated by projection onto the Fourier modes, and then “whitened” by normalizing by the power spectrum.
Yet it is easily seen that this process leaves most of the structure intact
[
24
]
.
Or again, coming from the other direction, restoring the (approximately)
1/f^{2}
power spectrum to white noise does not produce a natural image.
Factor analyzers thus have no means of capturing the structure in natural images that is lies beyond the mean and the power spectrum.

Moderately sparse generative models.

We have established, then, that neither factor analyzers nor GMMs are good models for natural images, albeit for roughly opposite reasons.
Furthermore, the inadequacy of the multivariate Gaussian as a marginal distribution for natural images is relevant to a wider range of models than the factor analyzer.
When the source variables are densely, even though non-normally, distributed, the model marginal
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
may very well end up approximately normal anyway, for central-limit-like reasons: the marginal distribution is an average of many independent, “random” basis vectors.
Averaging can wash out higher-order structure in these vectors.
This is (I take it) another way of expressing Hinton’s conjecture that the most useful codes will be componential, in order to represent the properties—pose, deformation, etc.—of the objects in the image; but also non-dense, in order to accommodate multiple objects in the same image
[
13
]
.

Between the “extremes” of the GMM and the factor analyzer lie models in which the “source” variables are sparsely distributed: some small subset are “on” for any given image.
For example, they could be distributed according to a product over Bernoulli distributions with small means.
Or again, we could interpret “on” more liberally and assign fat-tailed (leptokurtotic) distributions to the source variables, so that they usually take on values close to zero but can take on extreme probabilities more often than normally distributed variables.
Common choices are the Laplace and Cauchy distributions
[
20
,
17
]
.
In this case it is convenient to write the source distribution in the form of a product of Boltzmann distributions,

\displaystyle{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}=\frac{1}{Z({\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}\prod_{{\mathit{k}}=1}^{\mathit{{K}}}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{-E_{{\mathit{k}}}(\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}\right\},

\displaystyle{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}=\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}},\frac{1}{\lambda}\bm{\mathbf{I}}}}\right),

(2.29)

with the energy functions
E_{{\mathit{k}}}
chosen so as to enforce some form of sparseness.
For example,

E_{{\mathit{k}}}(\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}):=\begin{cases}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\beta_{{\mathit{k}}}^{2}+\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}^{2}}}\right)&\text{Cauchy distribution}\\
\alpha_{{\mathit{k}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}\right\rvert&\text{Laplace distribution}\\
\frac{\alpha_{{\mathit{k}}}}{\beta}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\cosh(\beta\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}})}}\right)&\text{hyperbolic-cosine distribution}.\end{cases}

(2.30)

The last is not a standard distribution, but for large
\beta
it approximates the Laplace distribution while retaining differentiability at 0
[
18
]
.
It will serve as our default example in the sparse-coding model that follows.
The emissions, for their part, are once again normally distributed about a linear function of the source variables, and for simplicity we have given them identical variance,
1/\lambda
.
Eq.
2.29
describes the complete sparse-coding model, a graphical model for which is shown in Fig.
2.4A
.

\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}={\frac{1}{Z}\prod_{k=1}^{K}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{-G(\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{k})}}\right\}}
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}={\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}},\frac{1}{\lambda}\bm{\mathbf{I}}}}\right)}

N

(A)

\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{1}
\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{2}
\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{3}
\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{4}
\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{5}
\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}_{1}
\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}_{1}
\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}_{3}

N

(B)

Figure 2.4:
A sparse-coding model.
(
) The most abstract version of the model (cf. the GMM in Fig.
2.1
and the factor analyzer in Fig.
2.3A
).
The energy function
G
enforces sparseness; e.g.,

G(\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{k})=\alpha_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{k}}}\right\rvert
,
or

G(\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{k})=\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\beta^{2}+\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{k}^{2}}}\right)

(
) The independence statements and overcompleteness made explicit.

Since we are aiming at economy of expression (few descriptors per image), we must concede prodigality in vocabulary (many descriptors)
[
23
]
—i.e., an overcomplete set of basis vectors.
This is made explicit in Fig.
2.4B
, along with the independence statements implied by our choices of source and emission distributions.
A factor of 2 overcompleteness is common
[
17
]
.

…

Inference and marginalization in a sparse-coding model: Laplace’s method.

There is a reason that factor analyzers and GMMs remain popular despite their limitations: the model recognition and marginal distributions are computable in closed form.
This computability is due to the source distributions being conjugate (or pseudo-conjugate) to the emission “likelihoods.”
When they are not, the marginal and recognition distributions can typically be computed only approximately.
When the source variables are Laplace-distributed, e.g., it is not clear that the recognition distribution can be computed (although see
[
22
]
).

Perhaps the simplest approach is to make a Taylor approximation.
8
8

8

Another, more powerful class of techniques for approximate inference is based on (iterative)
optimization
of the recognition distribution.
However, this endows it with the character of learning, and accordingly we shall defer discussing these techniques until Chapter
6
.

In particular, consider approximating the energy,
E({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}})
, of a distribution near its mode,
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}^{0}}
, with the first three terms of the Taylor series.
It is easy to see that, for
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}^{0}}
to be a mode of the distribution, it must satisfy the following conditions:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{E}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}^{0}})=0,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}{E}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}^{0}})\succ 0;

i.e., at the mode, the energy’s gradient must be zero and its Hessian must be positive definite.
For a generic energy, the second-order Taylor approximation at the mode is therefore

\begin{split}E(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}})&\approx E({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}^{0}})+\frac{\partial{E}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}^{0}})\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}-{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}^{0}}}}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}-{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}^{0}}}}\right)^{\text{T}}\frac{\partial^{2}{E}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}^{0}})\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}-{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}^{0}}}}\right)\\
&=E({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}^{0}})+\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}-{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}^{0}}}}\right)^{\text{T}}\frac{\partial^{2}{E}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}^{0}})\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}-{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}^{0}}}}\right).\end{split}

(2.31)

The only remaining
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}
-dependent term should look familiar: it is the energy of a normal distribution with mean at the mode and inverse covariance equal to the Hessian of the energy (evaluated at the mode).
The first term,
E({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}^{0}})
, on the other hand, is constant in the random variable
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}
and can therefore be absorbed into the normalizer.
Hence we can approximate the recognition distribution as

{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}=\frac{1}{Z}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{-E_{\text{recog}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}\right\}\approx\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}^{0}},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\partial^{2}{E_{\text{recog}}}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}^{0}},\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}})}}\right)^{-1}}}\right).

(2.32)

Note that this approximation is valid at realizations of
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}
even “moderately” close to
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}^{0}}
, since the neglected higher-order terms need only be less than unity in order for the exponentiation to drive their contribution to the distribution down to much smaller values.
On the other hand, it is obviously inappropriate for distributions having multiple modes, or over discrete random variables.

So much for approximating the recognition distribution; we now move on to the model marginal.
Using the definition of conditional probability with the approximate recognition distribution, Eq.
2.32
, and then evaluating at the mode, we see that

\begin{split}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}&={{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}\\
&\approx\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}^{0}},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\partial^{2}{E_{\text{recog}}}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}^{0}},\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}})}}\right)^{-1}}}\right){{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}\\
\implies{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}^{0}},\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}&\approx\tau^{-{\mathit{k}}/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\frac{\partial^{2}{E_{\text{recog}}}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}^{0}},\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}})}}\right\rvert^{1/2}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}\\
\implies{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}&={{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}^{0}},\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}\tau^{{\mathit{k}}/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\frac{\partial^{2}{E_{\text{recog}}}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}^{0}},\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}})}}\right\rvert^{-1/2}.\end{split}

(2.33)

This is the approximate model marginal under Laplace’s method.

Let us specialize to an isotropic Gaussian emission and a factorial source distribution, Eq.
2.29
.
Start with the Hessian:

\begin{split}\frac{\partial^{2}{}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}E_{\text{recog}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})&=\frac{\partial^{2}{}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}E_{\text{joint}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})\\
&=\frac{\partial^{2}{}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{{\mathit{k}}=1}^{\mathit{{K}}}E_{{\mathit{k}}}(\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})+\frac{\lambda}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}}}\right\rVert^{2}}}\right)\\
&=\text{diag}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\partial^{2}\!{E_{1}}}{\partial{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}_{1}}}^{2}}(\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{1},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}),\cdots,\frac{\partial^{2}\!{E_{\mathit{{K}}}}}{\partial{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}_{\mathit{{K}}}}}^{2}}(\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{\mathit{{K}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}\right]^{\text{T}}}}\right)+\lambda\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}},\\
\end{split}

where
\text{diag}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{a}}}}\right)
is a diagonal matrix with the vector
\bm{{a}}
as its diagonal.
For concreteness, let us specialize further to Laplace-distributed source variables, as in Eq.
2.30
.
Unfortunately, the curvature of this energy function is undefined at the origin, and (equally bad) zero everywhere else.
Lewicki and colleagues
[
17
,
18
]
therefore suggest approximating this energy—in the Hessian only—with the hyperbolic-cosine energy (see again Eq.
2.30
), which approaches the Laplace energy in the limit of large
\beta
.
The second derivative of this energy is

\begin{split}\frac{\partial^{2}\!{E_{{\mathit{k}}}}}{\partial{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}^{2}}(\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})=\frac{\partial^{2}\!{}}{\partial{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}^{2}}\frac{\alpha_{{\mathit{k}}}}{\beta}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\cosh(\beta\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}})}}\right)=\alpha_{{\mathit{k}}}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}\tanh(\beta\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}})=\alpha_{{\mathit{k}}}\beta\sech^{2}(\beta\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}).\end{split}

Hence, from Eq.
2.32
, an approximate recognition distribution for the sparse-coding model is

{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}\approx\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{0}},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\lambda\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}+\text{diag}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\alpha}}\circ\beta\sech^{2}(\beta{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{0}})}}\right)}}\right)^{-1}}}\right),

(2.34)

where
\circ
is the element-wise product and
\sech(\cdot)
is supposed to act element-wise.
Notice that although this may appear to be independent of the observations
{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}
, they enter through the mode, which obviously varies as a function of
{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}
.

To specialize the marginal distribution to the Laplace prior, we use Eq.
2.33
, substituting the Laplace energies into the joint, but the hyperbolic-cosine energies into the Hessian (to maintain differentiability):

{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}\approx\frac{\tau^{{\mathit{k}}/2}}{Z_{\text{joint}}}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\bm{{\alpha}}^{\text{T}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{0}}}}\right\rvert-\frac{\lambda}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{0}}}}\right\rVert^{2}}}\right\}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\lambda\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}+\text{diag}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\alpha}}\circ\beta\sech^{2}(\beta{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{0}})}}\right)}}\right\rvert^{-1/2}.

(2.35)

…

2.2
Dynamical models

Consider the graphical model in Fig.
2.5
.
Here we finally encounter some interesting statistical-independence structure among the variables.
One interpretation is that we have dropped the i.i.d. assumption: the plates that we saw in Figs.
2.1
and
2.3
have been replaced with explicit representation of the source-emission replicates, because now “adjacent” sources are dependent on each other.
The joint distribution still factorizes, but in a more complicated way, with individual factors linking together pairs of random variables:

{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{1},\ldots,\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{{{T}}},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)}=\prod_{t=1}^{{{T}}}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}}}\right)}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}}}\right)}.

(2.36)

(A few simple remarks on the notation:
(1) There is no
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{0}
, but we define

{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{1}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{\ignorespaces 0}}}\right)}:={{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{1}}}\right)}
, the initial source distribution.
This allows for a more compact expression of the joint.
(2) The joint has been written as a function of the instantiations of
{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}
to reflect the assumption that they have been observed.
(3) The dependence on parameters has been suppressed to reduce clutter.)

Alternatively, we can interpret Fig.
2.5
as showing structure
internal
to each of
N
source and emission variables, in which case the entire graph could be wrapped in a plate, and Eq.
2.36
would represent just one sample sequence of many.
Within each sequence, we assume that the data were “emitted” by some process with Markov dynamics.
We will prefer the second interpretation because it allows for multiple, i.i.d. observation sequences, a scenario typically encountered.

\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}_{1}
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{1}
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}_{2}
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{2}
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}_{3}
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{3}
\cdots
\cdots
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}_{\!{{T}}\!-\!2\!\!}
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{\!{{T}}\!-\!2\!\!}
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}_{\!{{T}}\!-\!1\!\!}
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{\!{{T}}\!-\!1\!\!}
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}_{{{T}}}
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{{{T}}}

Figure 2.5:
The hidden Markov Model.
Generally, this name is used only if the latent state is discrete, but the same architecture underlies state-space models with real-valued hidden states.

Note the tree structure in Fig.
2.5
.
It implies that inference—although substantially more complicated than that for the models encountered previously, which was essentially a single application of Bayes’s rule—is still only a straightforward application of the sum-product algorithm.
Making that connection precise is, however, left as an exercise for the reader.
Here we derive, and show the connections between, the classical inference algorithms for the two main models for which this graph structure holds: the “forward-backward” algorithm for the hidden Markov model; and the Kalman filter and Rausch-Tung-Striebel smoother for the state-space model (SSM).

We begin without specializing to either of these models.
The general strategy will be to try to derive recursive procedures.
More specifically, we will compute the
filter distribution
,

{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}}}\right)}
,
i.e. the distribution over hidden state
t
given all the observations up to and including point
t
, in a
forward
pass.
Then in a backward pass starting from sample
{{T}}
, we will compute the
smoother distributions
,
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1},\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)}
and
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)}
: the distribution over hidden states given
all
the observations
sans phrase
.
We derive the smoother for pairs of adjacent bstates as well as single states in anticipation of their usefulness in
learning
.
In particular, it is intuitively clear that it would be impossible to learn state-transition probabilities with only distributions over single states.

These are not the only possible forward and backward recursions, and indeed the classical version of the forward-backward algorithm for HMMs computes variations on both of these quantities.
We start with the filter and smoother, however, to establish the correspondence with Kalman filtering and RTS smoothing in the case of the SSM.
Furthermore, the filter distribution may be useful
per se
, as (e.g.) for temporal data (
t
indexing the time of arrival) that need to be processed on-line.
Finally, for compactness, all marginalizations in the development below are written as sums (as for the HMM) rather than integrals (as for the SSM).

Filtering.

We proceed by induction.
In particular, we assume that we start with the filter distribution at
t-
1,
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}
, and show how to update it to
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}}}\right)}
.
We will handle the base case at the end.

To derive the recursion, we work with the two graphical-model fragments shown in Fig.
2.6A
and Fig.
2.6B
.
The distributions we have chosen to parameterize these fragments are those associated with the graphical model in Fig.
2.5
; that is, they are either factors in the parameterization of the joint distribution in Eq.
2.36
, or derivable (we hope) from it.
In any case, they provide perfectly legitimate parameterizations for the directed graphical models in Fig.
2.6
, although why we have chosen precisely these may not yet be clear.

In particular, the filtering process can be thought of as repeated application of two stages, corresponding to operations on these two model fragments: (a) the time update (marginalization in Fig.
2.6A
) and (b) the measurement update (“Bayes inversion” in Fig.
2.6B
).
Thus the time update is:

TIME UPDATE

{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)},\rightarrow{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}

Marginalize

\begin{pmatrix}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}\\
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}}}\right)}\end{pmatrix}

\begin{split}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}&=\sum_{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t-1}}{}}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t-1}}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t-1}},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}\\
&=\sum_{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t-1}}{}}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t-1}}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}\\
\end{split}

(2.37)

The second equality follows from properties of the graph in Fig.
2.5
: given the previous state, the current state is independent of all preceding observations.
Thus we have transformed a (filter) distribution over the previous state,
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}
, into a distribution over the current state,
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}
(in both cases conditioned on observations up
t-
1)—hence the “time update.”

\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}}}\right)}

(A)

\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}_{t}
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}}}\right)}

(B)

\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)}
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)}

(C)

Figure 2.6:
Three graphical model fragments for HMM inference problems.

The various inference problems for HMM-like models can be expressed in terms of recursive application to these three fragments two operations: marginalizing out the source variable (
marginalization
) and computing the recognition distribution (
inversion
).
(
)
Marginalization: the time update.
Inversion: future conditioning.
(
)
Marginalization: the recursion for the data marginal.
Inversion: the measurement update.
(
)
Marginalization: the backward step.
Inversion: the recursion for the recognition distribution over sequences.
The distributions have been written in terms of a particular instantiation of the observations (i.e., lowercase letters) to emphasize their use in
inference
.

In the
measurement
update, we will add another measurement to the filter distribution; that is, we will transform a distribution over
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}
by adding the current observation,
{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}
, to the set of conditioning variables.
In particular, using Bayes’s rule on the graph in Fig.
2.6B
to compute the recognition distribution over
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}
:

MEASUREMENT UPDATE

{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}\rightarrow{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}}}\right)}

Bayes-Invert

\begin{pmatrix}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}\\
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}}}\right)}\end{pmatrix}

\begin{split}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}}}\right)}&=\frac{{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}}{\sum_{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t}}{}}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t}}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t}},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}}\\
&=\frac{{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}}}\right)}}{\sum_{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t}}{}}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t}}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t}}}}\right)}}.\end{split}

(2.38)

Here the second equality again follows from independence statements asserted by the graph in Fig.
2.5
: given the current state, the current observation is independent of all past observations.

Thus Eqs.
2.37
and
2.38
together transform the filter distribution at step
t-
1 into the filter distribution at step
t
.
The question is how difficult the operations in these equations are to carry out.
We precise them to the case of the HMM and SSM below.
All that remains is the base case, then, and it is indeed obvious that at step
t=1
, the measurement update is simply initialized at
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{1}}}\right)}
, the source distribution over the initial states.

Smoothing.

Again we break the recursion into two stages, corresponding to Fig.
2.6B
and Fig.
2.6C
, although here we reverse the order of “Bayes inversion” and marginalization.
This time we assume that we start with a smoother distribution at step
t
and show how to get the smoother distribution at step
t-
1, i.e. a backwards recursion.

In the first stage, we return to the graphical model fragment used in the the time update, Fig.
2.6A
, only this time we compute a recognition distribution rather than merely marginalizing.
Of course, this “inversion” requires computing the marginal along the way, so we may be able to reuse here some of the computations from the time update.
We revisit this point below.

Faute de mieux
, we call this stage “future conditioning”:

FUTURE CONDITIONING

{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}\rightarrow{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}

Bayes-Invert

\begin{pmatrix}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}\\
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}}}\right)}\\
\end{pmatrix}

\begin{split}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)}&={{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}\\
&=\frac{{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}}{\sum_{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t-1}}{}}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t-1}}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t-1}},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}}\\
&=\frac{{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}}}\right)}}{\sum_{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t-1}}{}}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t-1}}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}}.\end{split}

(2.39)

The final equality follows, as in the time update, from the fact that
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}
is independent of all past observations, conditioned on
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}
.
The first equality follows from the mirror-image independence statement: conditioned on the next state, the current state is independent of all
future
observations.

The second and final stage, which we call the “backward step,” marginalizes out
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}
from the graph fragment in Fig.
2.6C
:

BACKWARD STEP

{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)}\rightarrow{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1},\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)}

{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)}\rightarrow{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)}

Marginalize

\begin{pmatrix}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)}\\
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)}\\
\end{pmatrix}

\begin{split}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)}&=\sum_{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t}}{}}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1},{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t}}|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)}\\
&=\sum_{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t}}{}}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t}}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t}},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)},\end{split}

(2.40)

yielding the smoother distributions at the previous step (
t-
1).
Notice that the distribution over pairs of states is simply the joint distribution in the penultimate line prior to marginalization.

One interesting fact about the smoother is that it does not invoke
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}}}\right)}
; that is, it does not need to (re-)access the data!
Instead, all relevant information about the observations is contained in the filter distribution.

So much for the recursion; what about the initialization?
The answer is trivial: at step
{{T}}
, the smoother distribution is identical to the filter distribution,
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{{{T}}}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)}
.

Marginalizing out the states.

The joint distribution
{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)
can also be computed with a recursion.
By the chain rule of probability,

{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}}}\right)=\prod_{t=1}^{{{T}}}{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right).

(2.41)

And in fact, we are already required to calculate the factors on the right-hand side for the measurement updates, Eq.
2.38
:
We saw that each measurement update is an “inversion” of Fig.
2.6B
, which requires along the way computing the marginal distribution over
{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}
in this graph fragment.
It turns out that these marginals are the factors in Eq.
2.41
:

\sum_{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t}}{}}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t}}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t}},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}={}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right).

Inference over sequences.

This observation suggests an interesting symmetry.
Marginalizing and Bayes inverting in all three of the graphical-model fragments in Fig.
2.6
yields six total operations, five of which we have now made use of:
The time update and future conditioning correspond, respectively, to marginalization and inversion in Fig.
2.6A
.
The recursion for the data marginal and the measurement update correspond respectively, as we have just seen, to marginalization and inversion in Fig.
2.6B
.
On the remaining graph fragment, Fig.
2.6C
, the backward step corresponds to marginalization.
That leaves inversion on this fragment—what does it compute?

\begin{split}\frac{{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)}}{\sum_{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t}}{}}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t}}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t}},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)}}&={{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)}\\
&={{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)}.\end{split}

Now consider the following recursion for computing the recognition distribution over
an entire sequence
, starting again from the chain rule of probability and then applying conditional-independence statements asserted by the graph:

\begin{split}{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{1},\ldots,\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{{{T}}}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)&=\prod_{t=1}^{{T}}{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{1},\ldots,\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)\\
&=\prod_{t=1}^{{T}}{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right).\end{split}

So we see that each of the six combinations of marginalization and Bayes inversion on each of the three unique graphical model fragements of Fig.
2.6
is a useful computation in its own right.

2.2.1
The hidden Markov Model and the
\alpha
-
\gamma
algorithm

We have not as yet specified the conditional distributions parameterizing the graph in Fig.
2.5
or Eq.
2.36
.
Let us specify that the states are discrete and therefore categorically distributed:

{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}}}\right)}=\begin{cases}\text{Categ}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{\pi}}}}}}\right)&t=1\\
\text{Categ}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}}}\right)&t>1\end{cases}

where the vector
{\bm{{\mathit{\pi}}}}
and matrix
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}
are parameters.
Note that we have assumed a
homogeneous
Markov chain, i.e.,
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}
is independent of
t
.

The most important consequence of this choice for the states of the “backbone” is that the filter and smoother distributions are both again categorical distributions, no matter what the form of the emission densities.
This is a result of the “pseudo-conjugacy” of the categorical distribution (see above).

Filtering and smoothing.

Now for concreteness, let us consider in turn each of the four half-updates, referring to the corresponding graphical model, as they apply to the HMM.
Since the filter distribution is categorical, the joint distribution represented by Fig.
2.6A
is a(nother) mixture model.
Marginalizing out the class identities in this model—the
time update
—therefore amounts to an application of Eq.
2.10
.
Similarly, the joint distribution of the model in Fig.
2.6B
is another mixture model.
The
measurement update
amounts to applying Bayes’ theorem to this mixture model—Eq.
2.6
.

The smoother follows the same template.
The joint distributions in both Fig.
2.6A
(as we have just seen) and Fig.
2.6C
are mixture models, and the “future conditioning” and “backward step” correspond, respectively, to “Bayes inversion” (Eq.
2.6
) and marginalization (Eq.
2.10
) in these two models.

The
\alpha
-
\gamma
algorithm.

What if we are not actually interested in the filter distribution, that is, if all we want at the end of the day are the smoother distributions,
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)}
and
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1},\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)}
(at all steps
t
)?
Then it can be wasteful and unnecessary to keep renormalizing in the measurement update, Eq.
2.38
.
We shall see below that it is
not
wasteful for Gaussian random variables, for which the recognition (cumulants) can be calculated without any explicit calculation of the normalizer.
But for discrete random variables, we are nearly doubling the number of operations performed.
On the other hand, normalization has agreeable numerical consequences, preventing the numerical underflow that would results from repeated multiplication of numbers less than one.
Therefore, we present the following mostly to connect with the historical literature.

Consider, then, calculating only the numerator in Eq.
2.38
:

\begin{split}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}&={{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}}}\right)}\\
&=\sum_{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t-1}}{}}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t-1}}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}}}\right)}\\
\implies{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}}}\right)}&=\sum_{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t-1}}{}}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t-1}},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}}}\right)}\\
\end{split}

where on the second line we have substituted in the time update, Eq.
2.37
, and on the third multiplied through by
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}
.
This can be written more compactly still as

\bm{{\alpha}}
-Recursion

\implies\alpha_{t}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t})=\sum_{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t-1}}{}}\alpha_{t-1}({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t-1}}){{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}}}\right)}

(2.42)

with the aid of the definition

\alpha_{t}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}):={{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}}}\right)}.

(2.43)

This is the well known
\alpha
forward recursion.

It remains to show that the smoother algorithm can be adjusted to work with
\alpha_{t}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t})
rather than the filter distribution.
This is trivial: simply multiplying the numerator and denominator in the final line of the “future conditioning” stage, Eq.
2.39
, by
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}
converts the filter densities into
\alpha
’s.
That is to say, the smoother recursion does not need to be altered; it works the same if the filter densities are replaced by
\alpha
’s.
To make this explicit and invoke the classical terminology, we assign the Greek letter
\gamma
to the smoother distribution,

\gamma_{t}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}):={{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)}

(2.44)

and combine the “future conditioning” and “backward step” into a single recursion.
We break it into two pieces to show where the smoother distribution over pairs of states is calculated:

\bm{{\gamma}}
-Recursion

\begin{split}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1},\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)}&=\gamma_{t}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t})\frac{\alpha_{t-1}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}){{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}}}\right)}}{\sum_{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t-1}}{}}\alpha_{t-1}({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t-1}}){{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}}\\
&=\gamma_{t}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t})\frac{\alpha_{t-1}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}){{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}}}\right)}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}}}\right)}}{\alpha_{t}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t})}\\
\gamma_{t-1}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1})&=\sum_{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t}}{}}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1},{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t}}|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)}\\
\end{split}

(2.45)

On the second line we have simply substituted in the formula for the alpha recursion, Eq.
2.42
.
This avoids recalculating the normalizer, as noted above—but at the price of reintroducing a direct dependency on the observations through
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}}}\right)}
.

Computational complexity of HMM filtering and smoothing.

2.2.2
State-space models, the Kalman filter, and the RTS smoother

If the graphical model of the previous section is parameterized with a jointly Gaussian distribution over
all
the variables, a different, but equally tractable, and equally popular, model results: the state-space model.
More specifically, the model can be interpreted as a discrete-time, linear, time-invariant (LTI) system, with additive Gaussian noise on the state transitions and observations:

\displaystyle{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}}}\right)}=\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}+\bm{\mathbf{B}}{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t-1}}+{\bm{{\mathit{a}}}},{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}}}}\right),

\displaystyle{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}_{t}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}}}\right)}=\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}+{\bm{{\mathit{c}}}},{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{y}|{}\hat{x}}}}}\right).

To connect with the historical literature, we have allowed the time evolution of the latent state to depend on some inputs or controls
{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}}
.
But notice that the controls are not random variables, since they are assumed to be observed (in theory, we issue them).
We could therefore treat all of
\bm{\mathbf{B}}{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t-1}}+{\bm{{\mathit{a}}}}
as a single vector of (time-varying) “parameters.”
In this derivation, however, we will be explicit and maintain separate identities for these quantities throughout.

As in the hidden Markov model, inference in the state-space model requires a forward sweep, followed by a backward sweep, through ordered samples (space or time).
Since we have presented the
\alpha
-
\gamma
version of the forward-backward algorithm for the HMM—i.e., forward filtering followed by backward smoothing—the SSM algorithms are identical at this level of description: Eqs.
2.37
,
2.38
,
2.39
, and
2.40
.

Differences arise only when we make precise how these steps are to be implemented.
That is, in the case of the SSM, applying these four equations amounts to a series of marginalizations and “Bayes inversions” with
jointly Gaussian random variables
.
Fortunately, as we have seen in Section
2.1.2
, marginalization and Bayes inversion for jointly Gaussian random variables yield elegant closed-form expressions.
Moreover, since the set of jointly Gaussian random variables is closed under these operations,
all random variables of interest remain Gaussian throughout
.
Thus keeping track of filter and smoother distributions amounts (merely) to keeping track of mean vectors and covariance matrices; and inference amounts to recursive application of certain matrix-vector operations: for marginal cumulants, Eq.
2.27
; for recognition cumulants, Eq.
2.28
; and for the cross-covariance matrix, Eq.
2.19
.
We discuss their computational complexity below.
These algorithms go by the famous names of the
Kalman filter
and the
Rauch-Tung-Striebel smoother
.
9
9

9

The term “Kalman smoother” is sometimes encountered in the literature, but is technically a misattribution.

Filtering.

Recall again that the goal is to derive the “filtering” equation,
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}}}\right)}
.
Here we translate the time update, Eq.
2.37
, and the measurement update, Eq.
2.38
, into operations on Gaussian random variables.
We have lately noted that this will required keeping track only of recognition mean vectors and covariance matrices, so to facilitate that process we begin by assigning shorthand symbols to the filter cumulants,

\displaystyle{\bm{{\bar{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t|t}}:=\mathbb{E}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}}}\right]},

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathbf{{\upsilon}}}_{t|t}}:=\text{Cov}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}}}\right]};

(2.46)

and likewise to the recognition mean and covariance given the observations only up to the previous step:

\displaystyle{\bm{{\bar{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t|t-1}}:=\mathbb{E}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right]},

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathbf{{\upsilon}}}_{t|t-1}}:=\text{Cov}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right]}.

(2.47)

We hope that these are the only parameters we ever need to keep track of.
To show that they are, we proceed by induction.

We begin with the induction step, which amounts to a measurement update followed by a time update.
Assume that the one-step prediction distribution at step
t-
1 is a normal distribution over
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}
,
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}=\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\bar{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t|t-1}},{\bm{\mathbf{{\upsilon}}}_{t|t-1}}}}\right)
.
Then, since the emission
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}_{t}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}}}\right)}
is likewise a normal distribution, whose mean is an affine function of
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}
, we can simply apply our equations for the recognition cumulants, Eq.
2.28
:

Measurement Update

\begin{split}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}}}\right)}&=\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\bar{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t|t}},{\bm{\mathbf{{\upsilon}}}_{t|t}}}}\right),\\
\bm{\mathbf{K}}_{t}&={\bm{\mathbf{{\upsilon}}}_{t|t-1}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\upsilon}}}_{t|t-1}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}+{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{y}|{}\hat{x}}}}}\right]}^{-1}\\
{\bm{{\bar{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t|t}}&={\bm{{\bar{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t|t-1}}+\bm{\mathbf{K}}_{t}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{{\bar{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t|t-1}}+{\bm{{\mathit{c}}}}}}\right)}}\right]\\
{\bm{\mathbf{{\upsilon}}}_{t|t}}&={\bm{\mathbf{{\upsilon}}}_{t|t-1}}-\bm{\mathbf{K}}_{t}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\upsilon}}}_{t|t-1}}.\end{split}

(2.48)

Let us interpret the cumulants.
The update to our existing estimate of the current hidden state,
{\bm{{\bar{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t|t-1}}
, is a product of a precision,
\bm{\mathbf{K}}_{t}
, and an accuracy,
{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{{\bar{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t|t-1}}+{\bm{{\mathit{c}}}}}}\right)
.
In particular, the more accurately
{\bm{{\bar{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t|t-1}}
predicts the current observation (
{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t}}
), the less it needs to be updated.
On the other hand, we need not be troubled by an inaccurate prediction if the observation is not particularly informative, to wit, when the emission is imprecise relative to the original distribution,
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}
.
This relative precision is measured by the Kalman gain,
\bm{\mathbf{K}}_{t}
, as we saw in Eq.
2.15
.

We expect the covariance, for its part, to shrink when we add new information.
The more precisely the emission reflects the hidden state (
\bm{\mathbf{K}}_{t}
), the larger the fraction of the existing covariance (
{\bm{\mathbf{{\upsilon}}}_{t|t-1}}
) we expect to remove.

We turn to the time update, which moves our prediction from
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}
to
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}
, without considering any new evidence.
It does so by considering the state transition, in particular multiplying the filter distribution
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}
by
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}}}\right)}
and marginalizing out
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}
(Eq.
2.37
).
The state transitions are again normally distributed about a mean that is an affine function of
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}
, which as we have just seen is itself normally distributed under the filter distribution.
Therefore we can compute the marginal distribution over
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}
simply by applying Eq.
2.27
:

Time Update

\begin{split}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}&=\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\bar{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t|t-1}},{\bm{\mathbf{{\upsilon}}}_{t|t-1}}}}\right),\\
{\bm{{\bar{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t|t-1}}&=\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}{\bm{{\bar{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t-1|t-1}}+\bm{\mathbf{B}}{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t-1}}+{\bm{{\mathit{a}}}}\\
{\bm{\mathbf{{\upsilon}}}_{t|t-1}}&=\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\upsilon}}}_{t-1|t-1}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}^{\text{T}}+{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}}.\end{split}

(2.49)

Thus, our best estimate of the next state (
{\bm{{\bar{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t|t-1}}
) is simply our estimate of the current state (
{\bm{{\bar{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t-1|t-1}}
) passed through the state-transition function.
The uncertainty (covariance) compounds: it is our uncertainty about the previous state, as transformed by the state-transition matrix, plus the state-transition uncertainty.

We cannot quite say, however, that the uncertainty grows, even though we are not incorporating any new observations and the state transition is noisy:
If the underlying dynamical system is strictly stable, i.e. all its eigenvalues are within the unit circle, then the covariance
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\upsilon}}}_{t-1|t-1}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}^{\text{T}}
will be smaller than
{\bm{\mathbf{{\upsilon}}}_{t-1|t-1}}
; and it could in theory be sufficiently smaller even to account for the additional transition noise (
{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}}
).
Such systems, however, are heading rapidly toward an equilibrium point, and are (perhaps) less common targets for tracking than marginally stable systems, with eigenvalues on the unit circle.
For such systems, the uncertainty increases with the time update.

To complete the induction, we need to show that the one-step prediction distribution at step
1
is a normal distribution over
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{1}
.
And indeed, setting this distribution to be the source distribution over
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{1}
,
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{1}}}\right)}
, achieves that goal.
For compactness, then, we define

\displaystyle{\bm{{\bar{{}\hat{x}}}}_{1|0}}:={\bm{{\mu}}_{1}},

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathbf{{\upsilon}}}_{1|0}}:={\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{1}},

(2.50)

so that Eq.
2.48
gives the measurement update for
all
time steps.
Now the time update, Eq.
2.49
, and the measurement update, Eq.
2.48
, completely define the filter.

Smoothing.

Recall the goal: to get, for all
t
,
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)}
, where we are conditioning on
all
the observations.
Once again we shall show that this distribution is normal, and thus we need only keep track of its first two cumulants:

\displaystyle{\bm{{\bar{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t|{{T}}}}:=\mathbb{E}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right]},

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathbf{{\upsilon}}}_{t|{{T}}}}:=\text{Cov}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right]}.

(2.51)

In deriving our algorithm, it will also be helpful to keep track of the “future-conditioned smoother,”

{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)}
, so we give names to its cumulants, as well:

\displaystyle\bm{{{\bar{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1|t,{{T}}}

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right]}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right]},

(2.52)

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathbf{{\upsilon}}}_{t-1|t,{{T}}}}

\displaystyle:=\text{Cov}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right]}

\displaystyle=\text{Cov}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right]}.

(2.53)

Notice that the “future-conditioned” mean is a random variable, because it depends on the random variable
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}
.
(The future-conditioned covariance, although it also seems to depend on a random variable, will turn out to be non-random.)
This raises a minor question of how to represent and store this mean, an issue which did not arise for the HMM.
We shall revisit this after the derivation of the smoother.

Since the Kalman filter leaves us with
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{{{T}}}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)}
at the last step, we start there and recurse backwards.
This filter distribution is normal, which takes care of the base case of the induction.
The induction step, then, assumes that at step
t
we have
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)}
, and concludes by showing that we can get
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)}
from it.

Beginning with “future conditioning,” Eq.
2.39
, we “Bayes invert” the filter distribution,
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{t-1}}}}\right)}
, and the state-transition distribution,
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}}}\right)}
.
Once again,
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}
is normally distributed under the filter distribution (as we showed in the derivation of the Kalman filter), and
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}}}\right)}
is normally distributed about an affine function of
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}
.
So we apply Eq.
2.28
and find:

Future Conditioning

\begin{split}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{{T}}}}}}\right)}&=\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{{\bar{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1|t,{{T}}},{\bm{\mathbf{{\upsilon}}}_{t-1|t,{{T}}}}}}\right),\\
\bm{\mathbf{J}}_{t-1}&={\bm{\mathbf{{\upsilon}}}_{t-1|t-1}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}^{\text{T}}(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\upsilon}}}_{t-1|t-1}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}^{\text{T}}+{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}})^{-1}\\
&={\bm{\mathbf{{\upsilon}}}_{t-1|t-1}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}^{\text{T}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\upsilon}}}_{t|t-1}^{-1}}\\
\bm{{{\bar{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t-1|t,{{T}}}&={\bm{{\bar{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t-1|t-1}}+\bm{\mathbf{J}}_{t-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}{\bm{{\bar{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t-1|t-1}}+\bm{\mathbf{B}}{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t-1}}+{\bm{{\mathit{a}}}}}}\right)}}\right]\\
&={\bm{{\bar{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t-1|t-1}}+\bm{\mathbf{J}}_{t-1}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{t}-{\bm{{\bar{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t|t-1}})\\
{\bm{\mathbf{{\upsilon}}}_{t-1|t,{{T}}}}&={\bm{\mathbf{{\upsilon}}}_{t-1|t-1}}-\bm{\mathbf{J}}_{t-1}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\upsilon}}}_{t-1|t-1}}\\
&={\bm{\mathbf{{\upsilon}}}_{t-1|t-1}}-\bm{\mathbf{J}}_{t-1}{\bm{\mathbf{{\upsilon}}}_{t|t-1}}\bm{\mathbf{J}}_{t-1}^{\text{T}}.\end{split}

(2.54)

For each quantity, we have provided an alternative expression involving the cumulants of the time-updated filter distribution, Eq.
2.49
.
