\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

(4.2)

Intuitively, by making the “odds more even,” I have made the problem harder; and indeed, even though you tailor your guessing strategy to these new odds, you must on average ask more questions to locate the marble:

\sum_{{\bm{{\mathit{z}}}}{}}-{q\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{z}}}}}}\right)}\log_{2}{q\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{z}}}}}}\right)}=\frac{1}{4}\log_{2}\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{4}\log_{2}\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{4}\log_{2}\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{4}\log_{2}\frac{1}{4}\\
=2.0.

Information entropy.

It should be intuitive that, by hiding the marble “more randomly,” I have increased the number of yes/no questions you must ask to locate it.
In a rather different context, Shannon famously sought a mathematical expression for the “randomness” or “uncertainty” of a random variable,
\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}
, in terms of its probability distribution, that captured two essential notions: that broader distributions should be more uncertain, and that the uncertainty of two independent random variables should be additive (as opposed to sub- or super-additive)
[
15
]
.
Remarkably, he showed that this expression must be (equivalent to) the number of yes/no questions asked in a guessing game like ours.
More precisely, he showed that any expression satisfying his desiderata must be, up to a scale factor,

\sum_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}{}}-{p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}\right)}\log{p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}\right)}=:\text{H}_{p}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right]},

(4.3)

a quantity he called
entropy
†
†
margin:

information entropy

†
†
margin:

information entropy

for its resemblance to the quantity of that name in statistical physics, and for which accordingly we use the symbol
H
(i.e., capital “eta” for
e
ntropy).
Because the scaling is irrelevant, a logarithm of any base will do equally well.
The natural logarithm is most mathematically convenient so we default to it throughout; but base 2 yields the convenient intepretation of our guessing game, and allows entropy to be denominated in the familiar quantity of bits.

We return to that game now but suppose this time that you do
not
know my hiding probabilities.
In particular, suppose I hide the marble according to the first scheme, Eq.
4.1
, but you guess according to the second scheme, Eq.
4.2
.
Then the number of questions you will need to ask, on average, is

\sum_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}{}}-{p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}\right)}\log_{2}{q\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}\right)}=\frac{1}{2}\log_{2}\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{4}\log_{2}\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{8}\log_{2}\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{8}\log_{2}\frac{1}{4}\\
=2.0.

It is no coincidence that more guesses will be required on average under the “wrong” distribution (2.0) than under the right one (1.75).
Lest one suspect that this has to do with the entropies of
p
and
q
, notice that the result still holds when the distributions are reversed:
If I hide the marble according to the uniform distribution (
q
, Eq.
4.2
) and you guess according to the non-uniform distribution (
p
, Eq.
4.1
), then you will likewise have to ask more questions than if you had guessed according to my distribution:

\sum_{{\bm{{\mathit{z}}}}{}}-{q\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{z}}}}}}\right)}\log_{2}{p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{z}}}}}}\right)}=\frac{1}{4}\log_{2}\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{4}\log_{2}\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{4}\log_{2}\frac{1}{8}+\frac{1}{4}\log_{2}\frac{1}{8}\\
=2.25,

rather than 2.0.

In general, we call the average number of questions required under the strategy derived from
q
, when the marble is hidden according to
p
, the
cross entropy
†
†
margin:

cross entropy

†
†
margin:

cross entropy

between
p
and
q
:

\sum_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}{}}-{p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}\right)}\log{q\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}\right)}=:\text{H}_{pq}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right]}.

(4.4)

That the cross entropy
\text{H}_{pq}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right]}
is always greater than the entropy (
Gibbs’s inequality
†
†
margin:

Gibbs’s inequality

†
†
margin:

Gibbs’s inequality

) follows from Jensen’s inequality:

\displaystyle\text{H}_{pq}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right]}-\text{H}_{p}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right]}

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}{}}-{p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}\right)}\log{q\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}\right)}+\sum_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}{}}{p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}\right)}\log{p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}\right)}

Eqs.
4.3
and
4.4

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log\frac{{q\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}\right)}}{{p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}\right)}}}}\right]}

\displaystyle\geq-\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{{q\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}\right)}}{{p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}\right)}}}}\right]}}}\right)

Jensen’s

\displaystyle=-\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1}}\right)

\displaystyle=0,

with equality only when
p=q
(again by Jensen’s inequality).
Since
{q\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Z}}}}{}}}\right)}
could be any distribution, this also shows that the guessing strategy proposed at the outset is the optimal one: no other strategy can yield fewer questions on average.
The quantity on the left-hand side is, then, the number of
extra
questions you have to ask in virtue of having used the wrong strategy.
This quantity also has a name, the
relative entropy
†
†
margin:

relative entropy

†
†
margin:

relative entropy

, or Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence:

\KL\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\right)}\middle|\middle|{q\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)}}}\right\}:=\text{H}_{pq}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right]}-\text{H}_{p}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right]}.

(4.5)

Efficient coding.

The guessing-game interpretation of entropy maps straightfowardly onto the classical coding problem.
Under an efficient code, more frequent data
{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}
are assigned shorter code words.
And indeed, assigning 1 and 0 (resp.) to the answers “yes” and “no” in our guessing game yields the following binary codes for the four letters:

\displaystyle\text{A}=1

\displaystyle\text{B}=01

\displaystyle\text{C}=001

\displaystyle\text{D}=000.

This is a prefix-free code—no codeword has another codeword as a prefix—so any string of binary numbers, like
10011000101
, has an unambiguous interpretation (ACADAB).
The average codeword length is the entropy of the data—e.g., 1.75 bits under Eq.
4.1
.
As with the guessing game, designing the code (guessing strategy) according to the wrong distribution costs an extra number of bits (questions) given by the relative entropy of the correct to the incorrect distributions, Eq.
4.5
.
For example, Eq.
4.2
suggests partitioning the space first into (A or B) vs. (C or D), and thence into individual letters, which is equivalent to the coding scheme

\displaystyle\text{A}=11

\displaystyle\text{B}=10

\displaystyle\text{C}=01

\displaystyle\text{D}=00.

This is also a prefix-free code, but as we have seen, under Eq.
4.2
it costs 2.0 bits on average.

Thus the relative entropy measures how much less efficient it is to use
q
rather than
p
to encode some data
{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}
drawn from
{p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\right)}
.
Since the relative entropy is non-negative and zero only when
p=q
, we have at last operationalized the notion of one distribution being or becoming “more like” another:
We make a distribution
q
more like a distribution
p
when we decrease the number of extra bits required to encode, according to
q
, data drawn from
{p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\right)}
.

Minimizing relative entropy.

Most (but not all) of the losses in this book, then, whether for discriminative or generative models, supervised or unsupervised learning, can be written as relative entropies, with the model distribution
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
in place of
q
.
The generic optimization problem is

\displaystyle\argmin_{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\KL\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)}\middle|\middle|{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right\}}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\argmin_{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\text{H}_{p{}\hat{p}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right]}}-{\text{H}_{p}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right]}}}}\right\}

\displaystyle\text{by def'n, Eq.\ \ref{eqn:relativeEntropy}}

(4.6)

\displaystyle=\argmin_{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\text{H}_{p{}\hat{p}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right]}}}}\right\}

\displaystyle\text{entropy is parameter-free}

\displaystyle\approx\argmin_{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}}}\right\}

\displaystyle\text{approx.\ w/sample average}

\displaystyle=\argmax_{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\argmax_{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\log\prod_{n}^{N}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}_{n};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\argmax_{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\prod_{n}^{N}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}_{n};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right\}

\displaystyle\log\text{ is monotonic}.

Thus we see that minimizing relative entropy is equivalent to minimizing cross entropy, or again to
maximizing the likelihood of the parameters
†
†
margin:

maximum likelihood

†
†
margin:

maximum likelihood

, thus connecting our optimization to the classical objective for fitting statistical models.

Maximum-likelihood estimates (MLEs) have long enjoyed widespread use in statistics for their asymptotic properties: Suppose the data were “actually” generated from a parameterized distribution within the family of model distributions,
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
.
Then as the sample size approaches infinity, the MLE converges (in probability) to the true underlying parameter (“consistency”) and achieves the mininum mean squared error among all consistent estimators (“efficiency”).
The parameter estimates of models fit by minimizing relative entropy inherit these properties.

[………….]

[[Forward and reverse KL]]

[[continuous RVs]]

[[well known losses like squared error and the “binary cross entropy”]]

Chapter 5
Learning in Discriminative Models

The essential feature of discriminative models is that they do not attempt to model the distribution of all of the available data.
Instead, they attempt to model only the distribution of one set of data conditioned on another set,
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
.
No attempt is made to model the distribution of
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}
.
Consequently, the variables
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}
and
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}
are often referred to as the ‘‘inputs’’ and ‘‘outputs,’’ respectively.
1
1

1

With some reservations, I have reversed the standard convention of using
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}
for outputs and
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}
for inputs.
The point is to emphasize that discriminative models are the Bayesian inverses of generative models.
But why should the generative models use
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}
for their “source” variables and
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}
for the emissions?
This in turn is to match the standard conventions from control theory for, e.g., linear dynamical system; see for example Section
2.2
.
This tension is clearly felt in the machine-learning literature, where generative models typically introduce yet another symbol,
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}}
, for their latent variables!

Since we are focused on parameteric models, the critical questions are:

1.

What parametric family of distributions shall we use for the conditional? and

2.

What family of functions shall we use for the map from the “inputs,”
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}
, to the parameters of that distribution?

5.1
Supervised Learning

In the classical notion of a discriminative model, the inputs and outputs are completely observed.
That is, classically, learning in discriminative models is supervised.
We shall nevertheless have occasion to explore unsupervised learning in discriminative models—in the next section.
Here, we describe supervised learning as an instance of the general approach laid out in Section
4.2
, to wit, minimizing relative or cross entropy.
Since are only modeling the conditional distribution, this means the conditional entropies.
But remember that an expectation is still taken under
{p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\right)}
:

\begin{split}{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}^{*}&=\argmin_{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\KL\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\right)}\middle|\middle|{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right\}}}\right\}\\
&=\argmin_{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right]}}}\right\}.\end{split}

(5.1)

How we proceed from here depends on our answers to the two questions posed at the outset.

5.1.1
Linear regression

Here we begin our account of supervised learning with the model that gives the simplest answers: the (multivariate) normal distribution, whose mean depends linearly on the inputs:

{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}=\tau^{-\mathit{{K}}/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}}}}\right\rvert^{-1/2}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{-\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}}}\right)^{\text{T}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}^{-1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}}}\right)}}\right\}.

Note that this could easily be extended to an affine function,
{\bm{{\mu}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}}=\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}+{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}}
, but the notation is simpler if we assume that both the inputs and outputs,
\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}
and
\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}
, have been centered, in which case
{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}}
is otiose.
Alternatively, a fixed value of 1 can be appended to the vector
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}
, and
{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}}
appended as a final column on
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}
—after all, we will make no assumptions about the distribution of
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}
.

To find
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}
, we differentiate the loss in Eq.
5.1
:

\begin{split}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right]}&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}}}\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)^{\text{T}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}^{-1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)}}\right]}\\
&=-{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}^{-1}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}0\\
\implies\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]}^{-1}\\
&\approx{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}^{-1}},\end{split}

(5.2)

where the move to a sample average in the final line reflects the fact that we have only samples from the data distribution.
The final line is famous enough to have earned its own name, the
normal equations
†
†
margin:

normal equations

†
†
margin:

normal equations

.
Acquiring
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}
through the normal equations is known as
linear regression
†
†
margin:

linear regression

†
†
margin:

linear regression

.
In fact, our optimization is not the only route to the normal equations, and to gain more intuition about linear regression we shall examine several of these.
But first we investigate a variation on the optimization just derived.

Regularization.

The inverted term
{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}
is a sum of outer products.
Therefore the resulting matrix, although obviously square, is not invertible if there are fewer samples than dimensions of
\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}
, in which case a unique solution for
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}
does not exist.
In that case a pseudo-inverse can be used to solve Eq.
5.2
.
However, as we shall see, the standard pseudo-inverse is merely a special case of a more general and elegant solution to the problem of
underdetermined
†
†
margin:

underdetermined

†
†
margin:

underdetermined

normal equations.

For notational simplicity, consider the special case of a scalar output—for example
\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}
, entry
{\mathit{k}}
in the vector of outputs
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}
.
And now let us treat the corresponding row of
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}
as a
random
vector,
\bm{{\mathit{{G}}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}^{\text{T}}
, independent of
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}
and with its own (generic) prior distribution:
2
2

2

Recall that random vectors use bold italic capitals, whereas matrices are assigned bold Roman capitals.

{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{G}}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{}}}\right)}={{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{G}}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}\right)}=\frac{1}{Z_{g}}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{-E_{{\mathit{k}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{G}}}}_{{\mathit{k}}})}}\right\}.

(5.3)

Then in place of original conditional distribution,
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathit{{X}}_{{\mathit{k}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
, we use the augmented conditional
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathit{{X}}_{{\mathit{k}}},\bm{{\mathit{{G}}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{{\mathit{k}}}}}\right)}
, a kind of posterior distribution over
\bm{{\mathit{{G}}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}
:

\begin{split}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}^{\text{T}}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathit{{X}}_{{\mathit{k}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathit{{X}}_{{\mathit{k}}},{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)}}}\right]}&=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}^{\text{T}}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathit{{X}}_{{\mathit{k}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathit{{X}}_{{\mathit{k}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}\right)}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}\right)}}}\right)}}\right]}\\
&=-{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}^{-1}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathit{{X}}_{{\mathit{k}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathit{{X}}_{{\mathit{k}}}-{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]}+\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{E_{{\mathit{k}}}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}^{\text{T}}}({\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}0\end{split}

For example, consider an isotropic, zero-mean, Gaussian prior distribution over
\bm{{\mathit{{G}}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}
.
Intuitively, this encodes our belief that the parameters are
most likely to be zero
, with the penalty for being non-zero growing quadratically with distance.
No parameter is considered
a priori
any more important than, or correlated with, any other.
Furthermore, the energy and its gradient for the isotropic, zero-mean, Gaussian are

\displaystyle E_{{\mathit{k}}}({\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}^{\text{T}}{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}},

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{E_{{\mathit{k}}}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}^{\text{T}}}({\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}})

\displaystyle={\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}^{\text{T}},

(5.4)

so we can solve for
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}
in closed-form:

\begin{split}0&=-{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}^{-1}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathit{{X}}_{{\mathit{k}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathit{{X}}_{{\mathit{k}}}-{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]}+{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}^{\text{T}}\\
\implies 0&=-{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}^{-1}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]}+\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}\\
\implies\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}+{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}}}}\right)^{-1}.\end{split}

(5.5)

[[Since the energy in Eq.
5.4
represents an
L^{2}
norm, this is known as
Tikhonov, ridge, or
L^{2}
-regularized regression
†
†
margin:

Tikhonov, ridge, or
L^{2}
-regularized regression

†
†
margin:

Tikhonov, ridge, or
L^{2}
-regularized regression

.]]
As long as the rank of
{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}}
is full, so is the rank of the inverted term (adding positive definite matrices cannot reduce rank), so the normal equations are now solvable even for rank-deficient
{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}
.

Newton-Raphson.

The least-squares penalty can be solved in one step because the resulting cost function is quadratic in
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}
.
Still, for costs that are not quadratic, but nevertheless convex, the (celebrated) Newton-Raphson algorithm is guaranteed to find the unique global solution.
In anticipation of encountering such costs (Section
5.1.2
), we apply this method to linear regression.

The basic Newton-Raphson method aims to find the roots (zeros) of a scalar function of a scalar input,
g({\mathit{\theta}})
, as follows.
From a given point in parameter space,
{\mathit{\theta}}^{(i)}
, move along the local line tangent to
g({\mathit{\theta}}^{(i)})
to the point where it intercepts the
{\mathit{\theta}}
axis; call this point
{\mathit{\theta}}^{(i+1)}
and iterate.
For appropriately smooth functions, each root
{\mathit{\theta}}^{*}
, at which
g({\mathit{\theta}}^{*})=0
, is guaranteed to be the convergent value of this procedure when intialized from some surrounding neighborhood.
Mathematically, the algorithm amounts to enforcing

\begin{split}\text{slope}=\frac{\text{rise}}{\text{run}}\implies g^{\prime}({\mathit{\theta}}^{(i)})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}\frac{g({\mathit{\theta}}^{(i)})-0}{{\mathit{\theta}}^{(i)}-{\mathit{\theta}}^{(i+1)}}\implies{\mathit{\theta}}^{(i+1)}={\mathit{\theta}}^{(i)}-g({\mathit{\theta}}^{(i)})/g^{\prime}({\mathit{\theta}}^{(i)}).\end{split}

The procedure is easily extended to vector-valued functions of vector inputs, as long as the vectors have the same length (otherwise the extension is more complicated).
This is especially germane to our problem of finding extrema of a function
f({\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})
, since the roots of the function
\bm{{g}}:=f^{\prime}
are extrema of
f
.
Replacing the scalar-valued functions above with their vector and matrix counterparts yields:

{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}^{(i+1)}}^{\text{T}}={{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}^{(i)}}^{\text{T}}-\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}^{\text{T}}}({\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}^{(i)}){\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\partial^{2}{f}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}^{\text{T}}}({\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}^{(i)})}}\right)}^{-1}.

(5.6)

In our case, the parameters are in the form of a matrix,
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}
, which would make the Hessian a tensor.
To avoid this ugliness, let us work with one row of the matrix,
{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{k}}^{\text{T}}
, at a time.
Then from Eq.
5.2
, we see that the gradient and Hessian of the cross entropy
H
are

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{H}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{k}}}}({\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{k}})

\displaystyle=-{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}^{-1}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathit{{X}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathit{{X}}_{{\mathit{k}}}-{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{k}}^{\text{T}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right]},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}{H}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{k}}}\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{k}}}^{\text{T}}}({\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{k}})

\displaystyle={\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}^{-1}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]}.

Consequently, the Newton-Raphson update becomes

\begin{split}{{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{k}}^{(i+1)}}^{\text{T}}&={{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{k}}^{(i)}}^{\text{T}}+\mathbb{E}_{\mathit{{X}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathit{{X}}_{{\mathit{k}}}-{{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{k}}^{(i)}}^{\text{T}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]}^{-1}\\
&={{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{k}}^{(i)}}^{\text{T}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mathit{{X}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathit{{X}}_{{\mathit{k}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]}-{{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{k}}^{(i)}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]}}}\right)\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]}^{-1}\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{\mathit{{X}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathit{{X}}_{{\mathit{k}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]}^{-1}\\
\implies\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]}^{-1},\end{split}

where on the last line we have simply collected up the updates for all rows.
We see that for any starting point, the solution (which doesn’t depend on that point) is reached in one step, as expected.

Moment matching under additive noise.

Linear regression is so ubiquitous that the assumption of Gaussian noise, although frequently justifiable by reference to the central limit theorem, may feel overly restrictive.
Let us therefore retain the assumption of a linear map with additive, independent, zero-mean noise,

\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}=\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}+\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}},

(5.7)

but no longer require the noise,
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}}
, to be normally distributed.
Now, without a probability model, it is no longer possible to minimize the relative entropy between data and model distributions.
Instead, we will match their first two moments.
More precisely, we shall require that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}}}\right]}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}{}}}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}}}\right]}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}.

Notice that we do not need to specify the expected outer product of the outputs.
Using the fact that the noise is independent of the inputs, we see that

\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]}=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}+\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}})(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}+\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}})^{\text{T}}}}\right]}=\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}^{\text{T}}+\bm{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{{}\hat{z}}.

So assuming
{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}{}}}
is “bigger” than
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}^{\text{T}}
, the noise covariance
\bm{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{{}\hat{z}}
can always make up the difference.

Furthermore, as lately noted, equality of means can be achieved simply by centering the data.
That leaves
{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}
and
{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}
.
Applying our “model” (Eq.
5.7
), using the fact that the means are zero, and then setting the model expectations equal to the data expectations, yields:

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]}&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}+\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}})\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{}^{\text{T}}}}\right]}=\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]}\\
\implies\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}&\approx{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}^{-1}},\end{split}

the normal equations.

In fine, assuming that the additive noise is Gaussian has the same net effect as fitting a kind of second-order approximation to the joint distribution.
This should not be surprising, since the normal distribution is the most “agnostic” of all distributions that specify the first two moments.

The emission covariance.

Let us pause briefly to consider the meaning of, and whether we should be surprised by, the fact that
{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}}
disappears from Eq.
5.2
.
It implies that use of the normal equations makes no assumptions about the “shape” (covariance) of the noise about points in the output space.
For example, some outputs could be “more important” (lower-variance) than, or correlated with, others.
These correspond respectively to differing values on the diagonal, and non-zero values in the off-diagonals, of the covariance matrix.
The reason this makes no difference to the solution is that we have at our disposal a separate row in
{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}}
for every output,
\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}
: we can safely adjust weights for one output dimension without affecting any other.

†
†
margin:

B
ut not under regularization; explain this.

†
†
margin:

But not under regularization; explain this.

What we do not have is a separate set of parameters for every
pair of samples
,
{\bm{{\mathit{x}}}}_{n},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}_{n}
.
This caused no complications because the samples were assumed to be i.i.d.
Nevertheless, the assumption of i.i.d. samples is not always appropriate.
For example….
The data are then
heteroscedastic
†
†
margin:

heteroscedastic

†
†
margin:

heteroscedastic

(differently dispersed) rather than
homoscedastic
†
†
margin:

homoscedastic

†
†
margin:

homoscedastic

(identically dispersed).
Still, if the dependence between samples is only second-order—i.e., in the first-order correlations—and known, the linear regression has an only slightly more complicated complete solution.
It is most most elegantly derived when the heteroscedastic data samples are represented explicitly.

Linear regression without probability distributions

Accordingly, let us assemble all the samples into two matrices:
3
3

3

These are the transposes of the matrices used in most developments of linear regression, but they are consistent with all the other standard conventions used throughout this book.

\displaystyle\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{X}}}}}}

\displaystyle:=\begin{bmatrix}{\bm{{\mathit{x}}}_{1}}&\cdots&{\bm{{\mathit{x}}}_{{N}}}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}

\displaystyle:=\begin{bmatrix}{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{1}}&\cdots&{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}_{{N}}}\end{bmatrix}.

In place of the preceding models, let us simply look for a solution to the linear equation

\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{X}}}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}.

(5.8)

The equation has a unique solution if and only if
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}
is square—that is, there are precisely as many samples (
{N}
) as dimensions (
\mathit{{M}}
)—and full-rank.
When
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}
is “tall” (more dimensions than samples,
\mathit{{M}}>{N}
), the solution is underdetermined.
We have postponed this variant of the problem and will continue to do so for now.
When
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}
is “fat” (more samples than dimensions,
{N}>\mathit{{M}}
), the solution is overdetermined:
no
matrix
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}
satisfies Eq.
5.8
, and a notion of “best fit” must be imposed in order to select just one of the many approximately satisfactory matrices.

Still, let us proceed somewhat naïvely and simply look for an obvious linear-algebraic solution.
If
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}
is full-rank in addition to being tall, then the Gram matrix (in sample space)
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}^{\text{T}}
is square (
\mathit{{M}}\times\mathit{{M}}
) and full-rank, and therefore invertible.
Hence, starting from Eq.
5.8
,

\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{X}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}^{\text{T}}=\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}^{\text{T}}\implies\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{X}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}^{\text{T}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right)^{-1}=\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}.

Again we have recovered the normal equations, this time apparently with even fewer assumptions.
Lest the normal equations seem inevitable, let us recall that the choice to right multiply by the matrix
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}^{\text{T}}
was, although perhaps obvious, also arbitrary.
Note in particular that, under our assumption that
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}
is tall, choosing
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}
according to the normal equations
does not actually satisfy Eq.
5.8
—indeed, no choice of
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}
could, because the problem is overdetermined.
What notion of “best fit” have we tacitly imposed in order to select one value for
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}
?

The answer can be read off the derivation in Eq.
5.2
above:
The normal equations arise from
squared-error
penalties, and accordingly the procedure is sometimes called the
method of least squares
†
†
margin:

method of least squares

†
†
margin:

method of least squares

.
Evidently the assumption of normally distributed errors and the least-squares penalty are two sides of the same coin.
In the case of a linear map, when
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}
is full rank, the least-squares solution exists.
And since the quadratic is convex, the solution is the unique; that is, the normal equations yield the global minimizer of the least-squares penalty.
As a “sanity check,” we can also reassure ourselve that when Eq.
5.8
really does hold, i.e. when
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}
is invertible, the normal equations reduce to the unique solution,
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}=\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{X}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}^{-1}
.

Heteroscedastic data: weighted least-squares.

Now that we have examined linear regression thoroughly from the perspective of the data matrices
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{X}}}}}}
and
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}
, let us complicate the problem along the lines suggested above.
Suppose that the samples in these matrices are not i.i.d.
In particular, let
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\tilde{X}}}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}
and
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\tilde{Y}}}}}_{{\mathit{m}}}
be random vectors of all samples, for dimensions
{\mathit{k}}
and
{\mathit{m}}
of the output and input (resp.), and suppose
\text{Cov}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\tilde{X}}}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\tilde{Y}}}}}_{{\mathit{m}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\tilde{X}}}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\tilde{Y}}}}}_{{\mathit{m}}}}}\right]}=\bm{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}
(fixed for all
{\mathit{k}}
and
{\mathit{m}}
).
Then we can decorrelate
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\tilde{X}}}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}
simply by multiplying by
\bm{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}^{-1/2}
.
In terms of the data matrix
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{X}}}}}}
, in which each
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\tilde{x}}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}
is a
row
, not a column, this amounts to right multiplication.
To maintain the linear relationship in Eq.
5.8
, we multiply
both
sides by
\bm{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}^{-1/2}
.
Replacing

\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{X}}}}}}
with
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{X}}}}}}\bm{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}^{-1/2}
and

\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}
with
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}\bm{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}^{-1/2}

turns the normal equations into

\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}=\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{X}}}}}}\bm{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}^{-1}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}^{\text{T}}{(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}\bm{\mathbf{\Upsilon}}^{-1}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}^{\text{T}})}^{-1}.

(5.9)

This variation is known as
weighted least squares
†
†
margin:

weighted least squares

†
†
margin:

weighted least squares

.

Geometric arguments.
