5.1.2
Generalized linear models

Having explored rather thoroughly the simplest model, let us revisit the methodological choices posed at the beginning of the chapter.
In particular, suppose we relax the assumption that the conditional be Gaussian, but retain the assumption that the data are combined linearly across dimensions.
[[Some discussion of finite sufficient statistics to motivate exponential families….]]
This class includes the multivariate Gaussian, but also many other familiar distirbutions—Poisson, multinomial, Gamma, Dirichlet, etc.—as special cases.
On the one hand, for none of these other distribution is the cross entropy quadratic in the parameters (recall Eq.
5.2
), so closed-form minimizations are not typically possible.
On the other hand, the cross-entropy loss does retain convexity for any exponential-family distribution, so the Newton-Raphson algorithm lately derived is guaranteed to find the global optimum.
As we shall see, each step of the algorithm can be rewritten as solving a
weighted
least-squares problem, as in Eq.
5.9
, except that the weights change at every iteration.

Exponential families

Marginal distribution:

{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\eta}}}}}}\right)}=h(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}})\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\bm{{\mathit{\eta}}}}^{\text{T}}{\bm{{\mathit{t}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}})-A({\bm{{\mathit{\eta}}}})}}\right\}

(5.10)

\begin{split}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\eta}}}}}^{\text{T}}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\eta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right]}&=-\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\bm{{\mathit{t}}}}^{\text{T}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}})-\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{A}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\eta}}}}}^{\text{T}}}({\bm{{\mathit{\eta}}}})}}\right]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}0\\
\implies\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\bm{{\mathit{t}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}})}}\right]}&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\bm{{\mathit{t}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}})}}\right]}.\end{split}

The optimal moment parameters occur when the gradient with respect to the natural parameters is zero….

Generalized linear model, assuming canonical response function:

{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}}}\right)}=h(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}})\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}^{\text{T}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}^{\text{T}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}-A\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}}}\right)}{\phi}}}\right\}

(5.11)

Gradient:

\begin{split}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}}}\right)}}}\right]}&=-\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{1}{\phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}-\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{A}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\eta}}}}}}}}\right)\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]}\\
&=-\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{1}{\phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}{}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}\middle|{}}}\right]}-\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}\middle|{}}}\right]}}}\right)\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]}\\
\end{split}

(5.12)

Hessian, for a scalar output:

\begin{split}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}^{2}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}}}^{\text{T}}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathit{{X}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathit{{X}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}}}}\right)}}}\right]}&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{1}{\phi}\text{Var}_{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}{}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}\middle|{}}}\right]}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]}\\
\end{split}

(5.13)

IRLS update:

\begin{split}{{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}}^{(i+1)}}^{\text{T}}&={{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}}^{(i)}}^{\text{T}}+\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mathit{{X}}{}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathit{{X}}\middle|{}}}\right]}-\mathbb{E}_{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}{}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}\middle|{}}}\right]}}}\right)\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\text{Var}_{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}{}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}\middle|{}}}\right]}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]}^{-1}\\
&={{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}}^{(i)}}^{\text{T}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{x}}}}-{\bm{{\mu}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}^{(i)}}}}\right)^{\text{T}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}^{\text{T}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}\bm{\mathbf{V}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right)^{-1}\\
&=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}}^{(i)}}^{\text{T}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}\bm{\mathbf{V}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}^{\text{T}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{x}}}}-{\bm{{\mu}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}^{(i)}}}}\right)^{\text{T}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}\bm{\mathbf{V}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right)^{-1}\\
&=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}}^{(i)}}^{\text{T}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{x}}}}-{\bm{{\mu}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}^{(i)}}}}\right)^{\text{T}}\bm{\mathbf{V}}^{-1}}}\right)\bm{\mathbf{V}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}^{\text{T}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}\bm{\mathbf{V}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right)^{-1}\\
&=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{\eta}}}}^{(i)}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{x}}}}-{\bm{{\mu}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}^{(i)}}}}\right)^{\text{T}}\bm{\mathbf{V}}^{-1}}}\right)\bm{\mathbf{V}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}^{\text{T}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}\bm{\mathbf{V}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{Y}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right)^{-1}.\end{split}

(5.14)

This is a weighted least-squares problem—cf. Eq.
5.9
—but with the parenthetical quantity, rather than
{\bm{{\mathit{x}}}}
, as the output variable.
Notice, however, that
\bm{\mathbf{V}}^{-1}=\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\eta}}}}^{(i)}}/\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mu}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}^{(i)}}}
.
So the parenthetical quantity is the Taylor-series expansion of the natural parameters
{\bm{{\mathit{\eta}}}}^{(i)}
in the vicinity of the mean,
{\bm{{\mu}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}^{(i)}}
.

Iteratively reweighted least squares

IRLS for the Bernoulli distribution: Logistic regression

IRLS for the Poisson Distribution

Having derived IRLS, we get a feel for the algorithm by applying it to a few cases, beginnning with
Poisson
noise.
For simplicity, we consider a single, scalar “output” or dependent variable; and the
canonical
link function, the natural logarithm.
This makes the mean of the distribution an exponentiated (inverse canonical link) linear function of the “input” or independent variables,
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{}
, i.e.
\mathbb{E}_{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}{}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\right]}=\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}}^{\text{T}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}}}\right\}
.
Recall that for Poisson random variables, the variance is equal to the mean.
Inserting these values into the first line of Eq.
5.14
, yields

{{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}}^{(i+1)}}^{\text{T}}={{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}}^{(i)}}^{\text{T}}+\mathbb{E}_{\mathit{{X}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathit{{X}}-\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}}^{(i)}}^{\text{T}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right\}}}\right)\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}}^{(i)}}^{\text{T}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right\}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]}^{-1}.

IRLS for the Gamma Distribution

†
†
margin:

U
pdate to new notation and perhaps rewrite.

†
†
margin:

Update to new notation and perhaps rewrite.

We consider the case where the
scale
parameter is known, but the
shape
parameter is not.
The reverse is much more commonly considered (it is the “gamma” GLiM built into
Matlab
’s
glmfit
, for example), probably because the sufficient statistic for the shape parameter is just the sum over independent output samples,
\sum_{n}{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}_{n}}
.
For simplicity, we consider outputs of length one (
\mathit{{K}}=1
) only, which makes the parameters a (row) vector,
{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}^{\text{T}}
.
Now, the log-likelihood of the parameters under the gamma distribution is:

\begin{split}{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}^{*}&=\argmax_{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\log\prod_{n}^{N}q({\mathit{{}\hat{x}}_{n}}|{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{n}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}\right\}\\
&=\argmax_{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\sum_{n}^{N}\log q({\mathit{{}\hat{x}}_{n}}|{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{n}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}\right\}\\
&=\argmax_{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\sum_{n}^{N}\bigg(\begin{bmatrix}\log{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}_{n}}\\
{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}_{n}}\end{bmatrix}^{\text{T}}\begin{bmatrix}{\mathit{\eta}^{n}_{k}}({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{n}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})\\
{\mathit{\eta}^{n}_{\theta}}\end{bmatrix}-\log\Gamma({\mathit{\eta}^{n}_{k}}({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{n}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})+1)+({\mathit{\eta}^{n}_{k}}({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{n}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})+1)\log(-{\mathit{\eta}^{n}_{\theta}})\bigg)}}\right\}\\
&=\argmax_{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\sum_{n}^{N}\bigg(\begin{bmatrix}\log{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}_{n}}\\
{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}_{n}}\end{bmatrix}^{\text{T}}\begin{bmatrix}k_{n}({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{n}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})-1\\
-1/\theta\end{bmatrix}-\log\Gamma(k_{n}({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{n}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}))-k_{n}({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{n}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})\log\theta\bigg)}}\right\}\\
\end{split}

Here we have made explicit that the only natural parameters depending on the inputs—and the parameters—are those associated with the shape parameter:
{\mathit{\eta}^{n}_{k}}=k_{n}-1
.
The scale parameter is assumed constant across all samples.
Since
k_{n}
must be positive, we define the link function as:

{\mathit{\eta}^{n}_{k}}=\exp\big\{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}^{\text{T}}{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{n}}\big\}-1.

The trailing 1 only clutters the derivation, so we work instead with
k_{n}
, starting with its gradient with respect to the parameters:

k_{n}=\exp\{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}^{\text{T}}{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{n}}\}\implies\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{k_{n}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}^{\text{T}}}=\exp\{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}^{\text{T}}{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{n}}\}{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{n}}^{\text{T}}=k_{n}{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{n}}^{\text{T}}.

We shall also need the first and second the derivatives of the log-partition function with respect to
{\mathit{\eta}^{n}_{k}}
, i.e., the expected value and variance of
\log{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}_{n}}
:

\begin{split}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{A}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\mathit{\eta}^{n}_{k}}}}&=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{A}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{k_{n}}}=\psi^{(0)}(k_{n})+\log\theta=:\mu_{n},\\
\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu_{n}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\mathit{\eta}^{n}_{k}}}}&=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu_{n}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{k_{n}}}=\psi^{(1)}(k_{n})=:\sigma^{2}_{n},\end{split}

(5.15)

with
\psi^{(i)}(\cdot)
the
i^{\text{th}}
-order polygamma function.
Putting these pieces together, the gradient (with respect to the parameters) of the entire cost function above is

\begin{split}\frac{\partial{L}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}^{\text{T}}}&=\sum_{n}^{N}(\log{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}_{n}}-\mu_{n})k_{n}{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{n}}^{\text{T}}\\
&=\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{}}}}}}\bm{{v}}\end{split}

where
v_{n}=(\log{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}_{n}}-\mu_{n})k_{n}
.

The Newton-Raphson algorithm also requires the Hessian.
Differentiating a second time we find

\begin{split}\frac{\partial^{2}{L}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}^{\text{T}}}&=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}^{\text{T}}}\sum_{n}^{N}{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{n}}(\log{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}_{n}}-\mu_{n})k_{n}\\
&=\sum_{n}^{N}{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{n}}\bigg(-\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu_{n}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{k_{n}}^{\text{T}}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{k_{n}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}^{\text{T}}}k_{n}+(\log{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}_{n}}-\mu_{n})\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{k_{n}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}^{\text{T}}}\bigg)\\
&=\sum_{n}^{N}{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{n}}\big(-\sigma^{2}_{n}k_{n}+\log{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}_{n}}-\mu_{n}\big)k_{n}{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{n}}^{\text{T}}\\
&=\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{}}}}}}\bm{\mathbf{D}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{}}}}}},\end{split}

with
\bm{\mathbf{D}}
a diagonal matrix with entries
d_{nn}=-k_{n}^{2}\sigma^{2}_{n}+k_{n}\log{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}_{n}}-k_{n}\mu_{n}
.
Notice, however, that if we use the
expected
Hessian, the term
\log{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}_{n}}-\mu_{n}
vanishes, leaving

d_{nn}=-k_{n}^{2}\sigma^{2}_{n}
.

The name “regression.”

5.1.3
Artificial neural networks

Backpropagation

†
†
margin:

U
pdate to new notation and begin with statistical formulation.

†
†
margin:

Update to new notation and begin with statistical formulation.

How do we train multi-layer neural networks to map certain inputs (
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}}
) into certain outputs (
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}
)?
This is the classical supervised-learning problem for neural networks; and the classical answer, still ubiquitous today, is “backprop.”
The procedure assigns blame to weights (synaptic strengths) in the network by propagating error derivatives backwards through the network, from the output to the input, and then changes the weights in proportion to their blameworthiness—an instance of gradient descent.
The derivation is essentially just an application of the chain rule of differentiation from multivariate calculus to a rather complicated function, which necessitates some punctilious bookkeeping.
In our discussion, we consider only feedforward neural nets with a single output layer at the end of the network; but more general variants are more or less easily derived from the equations presented here.
The idea here is to introduce the concepts and (perhaps especially) the machinery for deriving the update rules.
With a flexible matrix calculus in hand (see Section
A.1
), the derivations are straightforward, economical, and elegant.

Backprop is in fact the answer to a slightly more general question than that posed at the outset: How should the weights of an
N
-layer neural network be changed so as to reduce a loss,
L(\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{N},{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}})
, evaluated on the output layer,
\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{N}
?
(Note that these outputs,
\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{N}
, are deterministic functions of the network’s inputs,
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}}
, so the loss is actually a function of both inputs and outputs).
When the goal is to make the network produce certain output “targets,”
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}
, the loss function is often defined to be
L:=\frac{1}{2}(\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{N}-{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}})^{\text{T}}(\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{N}-{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}})
; but really any differentiable function of
\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{N}
will do.

Derivation.

Let each layer in the network perform the same operation, up to some parameters
\bm{\mathbf{W}}
,
\bm{{b}}
, on its inputs: an affine function of those inputs followed by a(n element-wise) nonlinear operation.
In symbols, the output of the
n^{\text{th}}
layer,
\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{n}
, is produced by:

\begin{split}\bm{{\mathit{v}}}_{n}&=\bm{\mathbf{W}}_{n}\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{n-1}+\bm{{b}}_{n},\\
\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{n}&=\mathbf{f}_{n}(\bm{{\mathit{v}}}_{n}),\end{split}

(5.16)

where
\mathbf{f}_{n}
is the nonlinearity.
We index each “weight matrix”
\bm{\mathbf{W}}_{n}
by the layer it
terminates on
.

We start our derivation, with a little bit of foresight, by defining the backwards recursion:

{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{N}}=\bm{\mathbf{J}}_{N}^{\text{T}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{L}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{N}}},\hskip 28.90755pt{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{n-1}}:=\bm{\mathbf{J}}_{n-1}^{\text{T}}\bm{\mathbf{W}}_{n}^{\text{T}}{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{n}},\>\>\text{for }n=N,N-1,\ldots,1.

(5.17)

Here,
\bm{\mathbf{J}}_{n}:=\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{n}}/\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{{\mathit{v}}}_{n}^{\text{T}}}=\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{f}_{n}}/\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{{\mathit{v}}}_{n}^{\text{T}}}
, the Jacobian of the nonlinearity.
With the loss function given above for output targets,
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{L}/\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{N}}=\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{N}-{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}
.
Now we take derivatives, making frequent use of the matrix-calculus results reviewed in Section
A.1
.
Beginning with the output layer:

\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{L}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mathbf{W}}_{N}}}=\bm{\mathbf{J}}_{N}^{\text{T}}(\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{N}-{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}})\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{N-1}^{\text{T}}={\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{N}}\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{N-1}^{\text{T}}.

Proceeding to the second layer, we find:

\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{L}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mathbf{W}}_{N-1}}}=\bm{\mathbf{J}}_{N-1}^{\text{T}}\bm{\mathbf{W}}_{N}^{\text{T}}{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{N}}\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{N-2}^{\text{T}}={\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{N-1}}\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{N-2}^{\text{T}}.

Proceeding backwards to earlier layers continues the pattern.
The derivatives with respect to the biases are a simple variation: substituting
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{{\mathit{v}}}_{n}}/\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{{b}}_{n}}=I
for
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{{\mathit{v}}}_{n}}/\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mathbf{W}}_{n}}=\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{n}^{\text{T}}
, we see that:

\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{L}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{{b}}_{n}}}={\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{n}}.

Weight changes are made proportional to the
average
error derivatives under the distribution of input/output pairs,
p({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}
), so the entire procedure can be summarized as:

\Delta\bm{\mathbf{W}}_{n}\propto{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{n}}\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{n-1}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{p}},\hskip 72.26999pt\Delta\bm{{b}}_{n}\propto{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}_{n}}}}}\right\rangle_{p}},

(5.18)

along with Eq.
5.17
and the definition of
\bm{\mathbf{J}}_{n}
.
Notice, in this connection, that if
\mathbf{f}_{n}
is taken (as usual) to operate element-wise,

\mathbf{f}_{n}(\bm{{\mathit{v}}}_{n})=[\mathrm{f}_{1}({\mathit{v}^{1}_{n}}),\mathrm{f}_{2}({\mathit{v}^{2}_{n}}),\ldots,\mathrm{f}_{k}({\mathit{v}^{k}_{n}})]^{\text{T}}
,
then
\bm{\mathbf{J}}_{n}
is diagonal, which simplifies the calculation in Eq.
5.17
(it can be performed with a Hadamard product rather than a full matrix multiply).

A statistical view.

Although this neural network is entirely deterministic, the learning procedure can be given a statistical interpretation by translating the loss into a probability distribution.
A useful (and standard) way to do this is to identify the loss with a(n average) negative log probability; for example, for the usual neural-network loss function,
L=\frac{1}{2}(\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{N}-{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}})^{\text{T}}\bm{\mathbf{\Sigma}}^{-1}(\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{N}-{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}})
, we have:

\begin{split}q({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}|{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})&\propto\exp\bigg\{-\frac{1}{2}(\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{N}({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}})-{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}})^{\text{T}}\bm{\mathbf{\Sigma}}^{-1}(\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{N}({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}})-{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}})\bigg\}\\
\implies q({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}|{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})&=\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{N}({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}}),\bm{\mathbf{\Sigma}}}}\right),\end{split}

where we have made explicit the dependence of network’s output layer on its inputs.
The parameters
{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}
are the weights (
\bm{\mathbf{W}}_{n}
) and biases (
\bm{{b}}_{n}
).
Although we have introduced a covariance matrix (
\bm{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
) into the equation, it plays no role in the minimization, as can be seen by setting the derivative of the negative log probability to zero.
As usual, we exploit the fact that the logarithm is monotonically increasing (its derivative is strictly positive) to see that maximizing
q({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}|{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})
is equivalent to minimizing the negative log probability, i.e., the loss function.
More precisely, we attempt to maximize the
average
of
q({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}|{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})
(or
\log q({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}|{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})
) under the “data” distribution of inputs and outputs.
This maximization can also be re-described as follows:

\begin{split}\argmax_{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{q({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}|{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}}\right\rangle_{p({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}})}}}}\right\}&=\argmin_{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{-\log q({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}|{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}}\right\rangle_{p({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}})}}}}\right\}\\
&=\argmin_{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\log p({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}|{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}})}}}\right\rangle_{p({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}})}}-{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\log q({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}|{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}}\right\rangle_{p({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}})}}}}\right\}\\
&\approx\argmin_{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathbb{E}_{p({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}})}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\log p({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}|{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}})}}\right]}-\mathbb{E}_{p({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}})}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\log q({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}|{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}\right]}}}\right\}\\
&=\argmin_{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\KL\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{p({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}|{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}})\middle|\middle|q({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}|{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}\right\}}}\right\},\end{split}

where the quantity being minimized in the final line is the
conditional
KL divergence (or “conditional relative entropy”).

So we see that backprop admits of a few, closely related statistical interpretations.
It maximizes the average (under the data distribution,
p({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}})
) conditional probability (
q({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}|{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})
) of the network’s outputs.
This is the same as maximizing the average log probability—or what is usually called the “expected log likelihood,” with
q({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}|{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})
interpreted as a likelihood function for the
parameters
,
{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}
.
Or again, it minimizes the (approximate) cross entropy,
{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{-\log q({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}|{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}}\right\rangle_{p({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}})}}
.
Finally, this is equivalent to implementing a form of conditional density estimation, making the “model conditional density,”
q({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}|{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})
, more like the “data conditional density,”
p({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}|{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}})
, on average across the distribution
p({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}})
of inputs and outputs.

Note that the model—the neural network—is only probabilistic at the very end, where data are generated normally about its deterministically computed outputs,
\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{N}
.
Different choices for this output distribution correspond to different loss functions.
Alternatively, we can view this noise as allowing for mismatch between the neural network and the true distribution of input/output pairs.

Some examples.

Backpropagation through time (BPTT)

Suppose we have reason to believe that some of the structure of our network is “conserved” or repeated.
For instance, we might imagine segments of the network to correspond to slices of time, in which case it is reasonable to treat every time slice as identical in structure, even though the inputs and outputs change over time.
Or we might have reason to believe that certain spatial structures are repeated.
In either case, the concrete meaning of the assumption is that the same parameters show up in multiple places in the network.
The network is then called “recurrent.”
The upshot for backprop is that a little care must be taken in distinguishing partial from total derivatives, after which the algorithm goes through as in the previous section.

We consider a generic, deterministic, recurrent neural network (RNN) with inputs
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}_{1},\ldots,\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}_{{{T}}}\sim p({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{{{T}}}})
and “hidden”-unit activities
{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}}
recursively computed as:

{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}}=\mathbf{f}({\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t-1}},{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{t}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}).

(5.19)

For simplicity, we let the recurrent layer also be the output layer—i.e., the layer at which the loss function is evaluated—in which case the most general loss can be expressed as
L({\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{0}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{T}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})
.
Notice that we let it depend on the hidden units at every time step, as well as directly on the parameters.
To compute parameter changes, we take the (total) derivative of the loss function with respect to those parameters:

\begin{split}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{L}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}^{\text{T}}}&=\frac{\partial{L}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}^{\text{T}}}+\sum_{t=0}^{T}\frac{\partial{L}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}}}^{\text{T}}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}^{\text{T}}}\\
&=\frac{\partial{L}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}^{\text{T}}}+\sum_{t=0}^{T}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{L}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}}}^{\text{T}}}\frac{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}}}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}^{\text{T}}}.\end{split}

(5.20)

The first equality is just standard calculus; the second follows because
{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}
only occurs in the final terms in the chains of derivatives.
Thus, one can either compute the total effect of
{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}
on each
{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}}
, and then compute the direct effects of the latter on
L
; or one can compute the
direct
effect of
{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}
on each
{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}}
, and then compute the
total
effect of the latter on
L
.

Because of Eq.
5.19
, the effect on
L
of
{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}}
, the current hidden state, is its direct effect, plus the effects caused by its influence on
future
hidden states,
{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t+k}},k>0
.
This can be expressed recursively as:

\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{L}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}}}^{\text{T}}}=\frac{\partial{L}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}}}^{\text{T}}}+\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{L}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t+1}}}^{\text{T}}}\frac{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t+1}}}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}}}^{\text{T}}}.

(5.21)

The recursion is initialized at
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{L}/\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{T}}^{\text{T}}}=\partial L/\partial{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{T}}^{\text{T}}
and run backwards in time.

The backprop-through time algorithm consists of Eqs.
5.20
and
5.21
.
Applying it to a particular model requires only working out three partial derivatives,
\partial{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t+1}}/\partial{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}}^{\text{T}}
,
\partial L/\partial{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}}^{\text{T}}
, and
\partial L/\partial{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}^{\text{T}}
, for a particular choice of recurrent function and loss function.
Using the standard recurrent function for a neural network (cf. Eq.
5.16
):

{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}}=\mathbf{f}(\bm{\mathbf{W}}_{uu}{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t-1}}+\bm{\mathbf{W}}_{{}\hat{y}{}\hat{x}}{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{t}}+\bm{{b}}_{u}),

and applying Eqs.
5.20
and
5.21
yields:

\begin{split}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{L}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}}}^{\text{T}}}&=\frac{\partial{L}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}}}^{\text{T}}}+\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{L}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t+1}}}^{\text{T}}}\bm{\mathbf{J}}_{t+1}\bm{\mathbf{W}}_{uu};\\
\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{L}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mathbf{W}}_{uu}}}&=\frac{\partial{L}}{\partial{\bm{\mathbf{W}}_{uu}}}+\sum_{t=0}^{T}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{L}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}}}^{\text{T}}}\frac{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}}}}{\partial{\bm{\mathbf{W}}_{uu}}}=\frac{\partial{L}}{\partial{\bm{\mathbf{W}}_{uu}}}+\sum_{t=0}^{T}\bm{\mathbf{J}}_{t}^{\text{T}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{L}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}}}}{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t-1}}^{\text{T}},\\
\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{L}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mathbf{W}}_{{}\hat{y}{}\hat{x}}}}&=\frac{\partial{L}}{\partial{\bm{\mathbf{W}}_{{}\hat{y}{}\hat{x}}}}+\sum_{t=0}^{T}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{L}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}}}^{\text{T}}}\frac{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}}}}{\partial{\bm{\mathbf{W}}_{{}\hat{y}{}\hat{x}}}}=\frac{\partial{L}}{\partial{\bm{\mathbf{W}}_{{}\hat{y}{}\hat{x}}}}+\sum_{t=0}^{T}\bm{\mathbf{J}}_{t}^{\text{T}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{L}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}}}}{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{t}}^{\text{T}},\\
\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{L}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{{b}}_{u}}^{\text{T}}}&=\frac{\partial{L}}{\partial{\bm{{b}}_{u}}^{\text{T}}}+\sum_{t=0}^{T}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{L}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}}}^{\text{T}}}\frac{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}}}}{\partial{\bm{{b}}_{u}}^{\text{T}}}=\frac{\partial{L}}{\partial{\bm{{b}}_{u}}^{\text{T}}}+\sum_{t=0}^{T}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{L}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}}}^{\text{T}}}\bm{\mathbf{J}}_{t}.\\
\end{split}

(5.22)

Note that the subscript on the Jacobian of the nonlinearity,
\bm{\mathbf{J}}_{t}=f^{\prime}
, indicates the time index of its
output
.

Applying backprop-through-time to the RTRBM.

To get a better feel for the algorithm, we consider the recurrent temporal restricted Boltzmann machine (RTRBM), introduced in Section
3.4.1
.
Only part of the update equations will be derivable with the tools we have seen so far; we shall revisit the remainder once we have seen the methods of unsupervised learning (Sections
6.8.1
and
6.8.2
).
This complexity is due to the fact that the RTRBM’s loss function is not, as we saw in Section
3.4.1
, simply a squared-error penalty on outputs—but, as we have lately observed, backprop can be applied (at least in principle) to any differentiable function of the outputs.
The RTRBM’s loss, in particular, is defined to be:

L=\sum_{t=0}^{T}L_{t}({\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}),\hskip 18.06749ptL_{T}:=-\log q({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{0}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})\hskip 18.06749ptL_{t}:=-\log q({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{t+1}}|{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})\>\>\>\text{for all other $t$},

where for notational compactness, we (confusingly) assigned the first term to the
last
loss, since this allows us to write
[
27
]
:

\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\KL\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{p({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{{{T}}}})\middle|\middle|q({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{{{T}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}\right\}=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\sum_{t=0}^{T}L_{t}({\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}}\right\rangle_{p({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{{{T}}}})}}.

Thus, improving (decreasing) the loss on the right is equivalent to making the RTRBM a good generative model for the data.
And the resulting partial derivatives in Eq.
5.22
decouple over time:

\frac{\partial{L}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}^{\text{T}}}=\sum_{t=0}^{T}\frac{\partial{L_{t}}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}^{\text{T}}}.

(5.23)

Now, under this (unusual) loss function, it is not perhaps obvious how to compute the terms
\partial L_{t}/\partial\bm{\mathbf{W}}_{uu}
,
\partial L_{t}/\partial\bm{\mathbf{W}}_{{}\hat{y}{}\hat{x}}
, and
\partial L_{t}/\partial\bm{{b}}_{u}
required by Eq.
5.22
.
In particular, we recall that
q({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{t+1}}|{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})
is the marginal distribution over the visible units of an RBM (or EFH), and that each RBM in the RTRBM is defined via the conditional distribution
q({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{t+1}},{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t+1}}|{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})
.
Thus, computing the average of these loss-function gradients under the data distribution,
p({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{{{T}}}})
, is equivalent to making each RBM a good model for these data, but—in contrast to the problems solved by backprop—without knowing the values of certain variables, sc., the “latent” variables of the RBMs,
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t+1}}
.
Instead, one has only a model of the joint distribution over
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{t+1}},{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t+1}}
, in this case the conditional
q({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{t+1}},{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}_{t+1}}|{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})
.
We shall consider this type of learning—“latent-variable density estimation”—in the subsequent sections.

In fact, it turns out that the last unspecified derivative,
\partial L_{t}/\partial{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}}^{\text{T}}
(see Eq.
5.21
) will also require unsupervised learning, so we defer its calculation until a later time.
At present, for economy of expression, we merely define:

\begin{split}\bm{{g}}_{t}^{\text{T}}:=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{L}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}}}^{\text{T}}}\bm{\mathbf{J}}_{t}&=\bigg(\frac{\partial{L}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}}}^{\text{T}}}+\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{L}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t+1}}}^{\text{T}}}\frac{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t+1}}}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}}}^{\text{T}}}\bigg)\bm{\mathbf{J}}_{t}\\
&=\bigg(\frac{\partial{L}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}}}^{\text{T}}}+\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{L}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t+1}}}^{\text{T}}}\bm{\mathbf{J}}_{t+1}\bm{\mathbf{W}}_{uu}\bigg)\bm{\mathbf{J}}_{t}\\
&=\bigg(\frac{\partial{L}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}}}^{\text{T}}}+\bm{{g}}_{t+1}^{\text{T}}\bm{\mathbf{W}}_{uu}\bigg)\bm{\mathbf{J}}_{t}\\
\end{split}

where the second equality follows from Eq.
5.21
.
This constitutes the backward recursion.
The gradients, according to which weight changes will be made, can then be written:

\begin{split}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{L}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mathbf{W}}_{uu}}}&=\sum_{t=0}^{T}\bigg(-\frac{\partial{\log q({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{t+1}}|{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}{\partial{\bm{\mathbf{W}}_{uu}}}+\bm{{g}}_{t}{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t-1}}^{\text{T}}\bigg),\\
\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{L}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mathbf{W}}_{{}\hat{y}{}\hat{x}}}}&=\sum_{t=0}^{T}\bigg(-\frac{\partial{\log q({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{t+1}}|{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}{\partial{\bm{\mathbf{W}}_{{}\hat{y}{}\hat{x}}}}+\bm{{g}}_{t}{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{t}}^{\text{T}}\bigg),\\
\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{L}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{{b}}_{u}}^{\text{T}}}&=\sum_{t=0}^{T}\bigg(-\frac{\partial{\log q({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{t+1}}|{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}{\partial{\bm{{b}}_{u}}}+\bm{{g}}_{t}^{\text{T}}\bigg),\\
\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{L}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{{b}}_{{}\hat{y}}}^{\text{T}}}&=\sum_{t=0}^{T}-\frac{\partial{\log q({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}_{t+1}}|{\bm{{\mathit{u}}}_{t}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}{\partial{\bm{{b}}_{{}\hat{y}}}^{\text{T}}}.\end{split}

(5.24)

We shall return to these equations in Section
6.8.2
.

5.2
Unsupervised learning

So far we have considered discriminative models in which the “outputs,”
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}
, have been observed.
Indeed, it might seem rather quixotic to try to learn a discriminative model purely from its inputs,
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}
.
Nevertheless, let us consider the intuitive objective of maximizing information transmission
[
3
]
, and see where it leads us.

5.2.1
“InfoMax” in deterministic, invertible models

To motivate the objective, consider the deterministic input-output relationship

\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}}={\bm{{\mathit{f}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}).

(5.25)

(Notice that we have introduced a new symbol for the outputs; this will be justified shortly.)
For example, when there are fewer outputs than inputs,
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}}}}\right\rvert<\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right\rvert
, changing
{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}
so as to maximize mutual information between inputs and outputs will yield a function
{\bm{{\mathit{f}}}}(\cdot,{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})
that recodes its inputs more efficiently.
However, we begin with a seemingly more näive, but mathematically tractable, alternative:
Let the number of outputs equal the number inputs,
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}}}}\right\rvert=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right\rvert
, and furthermore let
{\bm{{\mathit{f}}}}
be invertible (in its first argument), so that
\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}={\bm{{\mathit{f}}}}^{-1}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})
.
This may seem somewhat perverse, since invertible transformations are automatically information preserving.
However, recall that mutual information nevertheless depends on
{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}
:

\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}}}}\right)=\text{H}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right]}-\text{H}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right]}=\text{H}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right]}.

(5.26)

That is, although the conditional entropy is zero for all values of
{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}
(under the assumption that
{\bm{{\mathit{f}}}}(\cdot,{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})
doesn’t lose its invertibility), the output entropy still depends on
{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}
.
In fine, for invertible
{\bm{{\mathit{f}}}}(\cdot,{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})
, maximizing mutual information amounts to maximizing output entropy.
4
4

4

If there
were
noise in the map from
\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}
to
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}}
, this would add an extra term to the gradient, and the goal of maximizing output entropy would have to be balanced against noise-proofing the transmission.

Since a vector random variable
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}}
is maximally entropic only if its components are statistically independent, this suggests that the “InfoMax” criterion can underwrite a form of
independent-components analysis
†
†
margin:

independent-components analysis

†
†
margin:

independent-components analysis

.
That is, maximizing input-output mutual information or output entropy will “unmix” the inputs
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}
into their independent components.

Without yet specifying the form of
{\bm{{\mathit{f}}}}
, let us try to re-express this objective, Eq.
5.26
.
More precisely, we consider its negation,
-\text{H}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right]}
, for consistency with the standard objectives of this book, which are losses.
To re-express this quantity we note that, although we have not specified a distribution for
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}}
,
{{}\hat{p}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}}}}\right)}
is inherited directly from the data distribution
{p_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\right)}
via the deterministic relationship in Eq.
5.25
.
In particular, since the relationship is (by assumption) invertible, the two distributions are related by the standard change-of-variables formula, which we apply on the third line:

\begin{split}-\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}}}}\right)=-\text{H}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}}}}\right]}&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\log{{}\hat{p}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}}}}\right)}}}\right]}\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\log{{}\hat{p}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{f}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}\right)}}}\right]}\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\log\frac{{p_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{f}}}}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}\right\rvert}}}\right]}\\
&=\KL\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{p_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)}\middle|\middle|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{f}}}}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}\right\rvert}}\right\}.\end{split}

(5.27)

Evidently, maximizing mutual information through an invertible transformation is identical to minimizing our standard loss, the relative entropy between data and model distributions, with the latter equal to the Jacobian determinant of that transformation:

{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{f}}}}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}\right\rvert.

(5.28)

The assimilation to density estimation can be completed by reinterpreting Eq.
5.25
as defining (via its inverse) the emission density of a
generative
model.
We defer this reinterpretion until Chapter
6
, when we take up learning in generative models in earnest.

InfoMax independent-components analysis.

One interesting special case of the unsupervised, discriminative learning problem just described is so-called InfoMax ICA
[
3
]
.
Here, the transformation of Eq.
5.25
is conceived as a neural network (see Fig.
5.1
), and therefore consisting of two steps: linear transformation followed by elementwise nonlinearity:

\displaystyle\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}

\displaystyle=\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}},

\displaystyle\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}

\displaystyle={\mathit{\psi}_{{\mathit{k}}}}(\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}),\text{ for all }{\mathit{k}}.

(5.29)

We leave the output nonlinearity undefined for now, except to insist that it be invertible.
It could, for example, be the classic logistic (sigmoid) function.
For convenience we also assign the symbol
{\bm{{\mathit{\psi}}}}
to the vector-valued “stack” of
{\mathit{\psi}_{{\mathit{k}}}}(\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}})
for all
{\mathit{k}}
:

\{{\bm{{\mathit{\psi}}}}(\bm{{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}}})\}_{{\mathit{k}}}:={\mathit{\psi}_{{\mathit{k}}}}(\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}).

(5.30)

The gradient, whether of the mutual information, output entropy, relative input entropy, or input cross entropy, is then

\begin{split}-\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}}}\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}}}}\right)=-\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}}}\text{H}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}}}}\right]}=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}}}\KL\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{p_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)}\middle|\middle|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{f}}}}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}})}}\right\rvert}}\right\}&=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{f}}}}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}})}}\right\rvert}}\right]}\\
&=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\psi}}}}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}})\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}}}\right\rvert}}\right]}\\
&=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\psi}}}}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}})}}\right\rvert}}\right]}-{\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}}^{-\text{T}}.\end{split}

(5.31)

Chapter 6
Learning in Generative Models

6.1
Introduction

[[
We are concerned with learning in generative models under two circumstances, “supervised” and “unsupervised.”
The paradigm case of each is the illustrated in the Fig. 6.1.
In Fig. 6.1a, each datum (
{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}
) comes with a class label—but despite the “missing” labels in Fig. 6.1b, class structure is still perspicuous.
Both sets of data can be fit with a Gaussian mixture model (Chapter
2
), but in the case of Fig. 6.1b, the “source” variables
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}
are “latent” or unobserved.
The learning algorithms for the two cases are consequently different.
Nevertheless, for the GMM, the supervised-learning algorithm can be written as a special case of the unsupervised-learning algorithm, and indeed this is often the case.
So we shall concentrate on the unsupervised problems, deriving the supervised solutions along the way.
]]

Density estimation.

Let us begin with an even simpler data set to model.
The data look to be distributed normally, so it would be sensible simply to let
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
be
\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{m}},\bm{\mathbf{S}}}}\right)
, with
\bm{{m}}
and
\bm{\mathbf{S}}
the sample mean and sample covariance.
But let us proceed somewhat naïvely according to the generic procedure introduced in Section
4.2
.
The procedure enjoins us to minimize the relative entropy; or, equivalently, since the entropy doesn’t depend on the parameters, the cross entropy:

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right]}\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tau^{-M/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}}}}\right|^{-1/2}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{-\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}-{\bm{{\mu}}})}}\right)^{\text{T}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}^{-1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}-{\bm{{\mu}}})}}\right)}}\right\}}}\right]}}\right]}\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[M\log\tau+\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}}}}\right|+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}-{\bm{{\mu}}}}}\right)^{\text{T}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}^{-1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}-{\bm{{\mu}}}}}\right)}}\right]}}\right]}.\end{split}

Differentiating with respect to
{\bm{{\mu}}}
indeed indicates that
{\bm{{\mu}}}
should be set equal to the sample average:

\begin{split}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{L}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mu}}}}}&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}^{-1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}-{\bm{{\mu}}}}}\right)}}\right]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}0\\
\implies{\bm{{\mu}}}&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right]}\approx{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}},\end{split}

where in the final line we approximate the expectation under the (unavailable) data distribution with an average under (available) samples from it.
Likewise, differentiating with respect to
{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}^{-1}}
, we find (after consulting Section
A.1
)

\begin{split}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{L}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}^{-1}}}}&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}}-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}-{\bm{{\mu}}}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}-{\bm{{\mu}}}}}\right)^{\text{T}}}}\right]}}\right]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}0\\
\implies{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}}&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}-{\bm{{\mu}}}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}-{\bm{{\mu}}}}}\right)^{\text{T}}}}\right]}\approx{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}-{\bm{{\mu}}}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}-{\bm{{\mu}}}}}\right)^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}.\end{split}

So far, so good.
We now proceed to the dataset shown in Fig. 6.1b.
Here by all appearances is a mixture of Gaussians.
In Section
2.1.1
we derived the marginal distribution for the GMM, Eq.
2.10
, so it seems that perhaps we can use the same procedure as for the single Gaussian.
The cross-entropy loss is

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right]}\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{{\mathit{k}}=1}^{\mathit{{K}}}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{}\middle|\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{k}=1;{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}{\mathit{\pi}_{k}}}}\right)}}\right]}\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{{\mathit{k}}=1}^{\mathit{{K}}}\tau^{-M/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}\right|^{-1/2}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{-\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}-{\bm{{\mu}}_{{\mathit{k}}}})}}\right)^{\text{T}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}^{-1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}-{\bm{{\mu}}_{{\mathit{k}}}})}}\right)}}\right\}{\mathit{\pi}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}\right)}}\right]}.\end{split}

(6.1)

We have encountered a problem.
The summation (across classes) inside the logarithm couples the parameters of the
K
Gaussians together.
In particular, differentiating with respect to
{\bm{{\mu}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}
or
{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}
will yield an expression involving
all
the means and covariances.
Solving these coupled equations (after setting the gradients to zero) is not straightforward.

6.2
Latent-variable density estimation

Now notice that the root of the problem, the summation sign in Eq.
6.1
, was introduced by the marginalization.
Indeed, the joint distribution, the product of Eqs.
2.3
and
2.4
, is

{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}=\prod_{{\mathit{k}}}^{\mathit{{K}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mu}}_{{\mathit{k}}}},{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}\right){\mathit{\pi}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}\right]^{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}.

(6.2)

The log of this distribution evidently does decouple into a sum of
\mathit{{K}}
terms, each involving only
{\bm{{\mu}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}
,
{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}
, and
{\mathit{\pi}_{{\mathit{k}}}}

So perhaps we should try to re-express the marginal cross entropy, or anyway its gradient, in terms of the joint cross entropy.

Introducing the joint distribution.

Starting with the gradient of a generic marginal cross entropy, we move the derivative into the expection, “anti-marginalize” to restore the latent variables, and then rearrange terms:

\begin{split}{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right]}&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\frac{1}{{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right]}\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\frac{1}{{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}{{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}}\sum_{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}{}}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right]}\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\frac{1}{{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}\sum_{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}{}}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right]}\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\sum_{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}{}}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right]}\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\right]}}}\right]}\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right]},\end{split}

(6.3)

where in the final line we have combined the data distribution and the model recognition distribution into a single
hybrid joint distribution
†
†
margin:

hybrid joint distribution

†
†
margin:

hybrid joint distribution

,

{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}{p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\right)}
.
This looks promising.
It
almost
says that the gradient of the marginal (or “incomplete”) cross entropy is the same as the gradient of a joint (or “complete”) cross entropy.
But the derivative cannot pass outside the expectation, because the hybrid joint distribution depends, like the model distribution, on the parameters
{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}
.

To see the implications of this dependence on the parameters, we return to our workhorse example, the Gaussian mixture model.
Inserting Eq.
6.2
into the final line of Eq.
6.3
shows that

\begin{split}{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right]}&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right]}\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}\log\prod_{{\mathit{k}}}^{\mathit{{K}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mu}}_{{\mathit{k}}}},{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}\right){\mathit{\pi}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}\right]^{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}\right]}\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{1}{2}{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}\sum_{{\mathit{k}}}^{\mathit{{K}}}\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M\log\tau+\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}\right|+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}-{\bm{{\mu}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}\right)^{\text{T}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}^{-1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}-{\bm{{\mu}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}\right)+\log{\mathit{\pi}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}\right)}}\right]}.\end{split}

The gradient with respect to (e.g.)
{\bm{{\mu}}_{k}}
is therefore

\begin{split}{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mu}}_{k}}}}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right]}&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{1}{2}\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}^{-1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}-{\bm{{\mu}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}\right)}}\right]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}0\\
\implies{\bm{{\mu}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}&=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right]}}{\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}\right]}}.\end{split}

(6.4)

This formula looks elegant only if we forget that
{\bm{{\mu}}_{k}}
is on both sides.
The expectations under the model recognition distribution,
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
, involve
{\bm{{\mu}}_{k}}
—indeed, they involve all of the parameters (recall Eq.
2.6
)!
This is reminiscent of the problem with the direct optimization of the marginal cross entropy, Eq.
6.1
, so it seems perhaps we have made no progress.

6.3
Expectation-Maximization

Approximating the recognition distribution.

This isn’t quite true.
Working with the joint did allow the parameters
inside
the expectation to decouple.
The present problem is a consequence of the parameters in the expectation itself, in particular the model recognition distribution,
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
.
This does not preclude gradient descent, and we shall return to it and Eq.
6.3
in Section
6.8.1
.

But here we will not yet give up hope of a cleaner solution.
Consider this seemingly artless proposal: simply take the expectation under a different distribution,
{{}\check{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\right)}
, one that doesn’t depend on these parameters.
1
1

1

Note the new latent variables
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}
, as opposed to
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}
, that have necessarily been introduced with this proxy recognition distribution.
This distinction is not always noted in the literature.

Doing so will certainly ruin the equality in Eq.
6.3
; that is,

\begin{split}{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right]}&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right]}\\
&\neq\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right]}={\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right]},\end{split}

where in the bottom lines the expectation is under this
proxy recognition distribution
†
†
margin:

proxy recognition distribution

†
†
margin:

proxy recognition distribution

.
But, as indicated by the final equality, and in contrast to the correct gradient, this incorrect gradient is indeed the gradient of a joint (“complete”) cross entropy: the derivative can pass outside the expectation that no longer depends on
{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}
.

The central intuition behind the algorithms that follow is to fit marginal densities by minimizing the joint or complete cross entropy,

\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right]}

in lieu of the marginal or incomplete cross entropy,

\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right]}
.
The discrepancy between these cross entropies can be finessed (we claim) by simultaneously minimizing the discrepancy between our proxy recognition distribution and the true (model) recognition distribution.

Relative entropy, cross entropy, and free energy.

Let us prove this last claim.
We begin by noting the equivalence of the following optimizations:

\begin{split}\argmin_{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\KL\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{{}\check{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)}{p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)}\middle|\middle|{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right\}}}\right\}&=\argmin_{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{}\check{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)}{p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)}}}\right)}-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right]}}}\right\}\\
&=\argmin_{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\log{{}\check{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)}-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right]}}}\right\}\\
&=\argmin_{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right]}}}\right\}.\end{split}

(6.5)

Although minimized by the same settings of the parameters, the three optimized quantities are all different:

•

The first optimized quantity is a relative entropy, in this case between a “hybrid” joint distribution—the product of the proxy recognition distribution and the data distribution—and the model joint.
Thus our optimization can be expressed in terms of the fundamental loss function proposed in Section
4.2
.

•

The second optimized quantity we call the
free energy
†
†
margin:

free energy

†
†
margin:

free energy

,
\mathcal{F}
, after its counterpart in statistical physics.
It differs from the relative entropy only by the entropy of the data.
The proof is most elegantly expressed in terms of the free energy and accordingly will be presented that way.

•

The quantity in the third line is the joint cross entropy that we lately proposed to optimize in lieu of the marginal cross entropy.
We will construct our optimization algorithms in terms of the joint cross entropy.
Notice that the
\argmin
of the cross entropy would not be equal to the other two if the expectation were taken under the true recognition distribution, because the entropy of the recognition distribution would then depend on the parameters
{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}
.

We start our proof by rearranging the free energy:

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}({\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}},{}\check{p})&:=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\log{{}\check{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)}-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right]}\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right]}+\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\log{{}\check{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)}-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right]}\\
&={\text{H}_{p{}\hat{p}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right]}}+\KL\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{{}\check{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)}\middle|\middle|{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right\}.\end{split}

(6.6)

The first term is the marginal cross entropy—the quantity that we actually want to minimize.
The second term is non-negative, because it is a relative entropy (see Section
4.2
).
So the free energy is an upper bound on the marginal cross entropy.
Given the equivalence of the optimizations in Eq.
6.5
, this shows that minimizing the joint cross entropy (via
{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}
) lowers an upper bound on the marginal cross entropy.
And the bound can be tightened by optimizing the free energy with respect to the recognition distribution, i.e., decreasing the relative entropy term.

Let us make the procedure explicit.
We minimize the free energy with respect to its two arguments:

EM Algorithm

\bullet\>

(E) Discriminative optimization
:

{{}\check{p}_{t+1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\right)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}\argmin_{{}\check{p}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathcal{F}({\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{t},{}\check{p})}}\right\}

\bullet\>

(M) Generative optimization
:

{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{t+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}\argmin_{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathcal{F}({\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}},{}\check{p}_{t+1})}}\right\}
.

The parameters can be initialized randomly,
{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{0}
, although there are better alternatives (see below).
The two optimizations may be executed alternately or simultaneously—we shall see examples of both below—until convergence, at which point
{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{\text{final}}
is our solution.

What remains to be specified now is a form for the proxy recognition distribution and (in turn) a method for optimizing it.
These choices lead to different algorithms.
In this text, we refer to all such algorithms as
expectation-maximization
†
†
margin:

expectation-maximization

†
†
margin:

expectation-maximization

(EM).
In fact, only one special case (discussed next) corresponds to the original EM algorithm of Dempster and Laird
[
1
]
, but it was later generalized to the procedure just described under the same name, by Neal and Hinton
[
19
]
.

What’s in a name?

The E and M in the “EM algorithm” originally stood for the two optimization steps that we have called “discriminative” and “generative.”
Historically, the discriminative optimization was known as the “E step” because it referred to
taking expectations under
, rather than finding via optimization, the proxy posterior distribution.
This is the expectation in the final line of Eq.
6.5
(ignoring the additional average under the data distribution).
Indeed, as we shall see shortly, the process of computing the expectations can be rather involved, since it requires running an inference algorithm (recall, for example, the smoothers of Section
2.2
).
Nevertheless, the “E step” has come to refer to the optimization, as this plays a larger role in modern variants of EM—and this allows us to see EM as a form of coordinate descent in the free energy.

Since EM was originally written in terms of expected log-likelihood rather than cross entropy, the “M step” originally referred to a maximization, but it will equally well refer to a minimization as in our setup.

6.4
Learning with exact inference

We saw in Chapter
2
that computing the recognition distribution is frequently impossible, because computing the normalizer
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
requires either an intractable integral or a sum over an exponential number of terms (i.e., exponential in the number of configurations of the variables to be marginalized out,
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}
).
Nevertheless, we begin with models for which exact inference is tractable, where the algorithm is particularly elegant.
Such models are sometimes described as
invertible
†
†
margin:

invertible generative models

†
†
margin:

invertible generative models

.

In this classical version of EM, the two free-energy optimizations are carried out in consecutive steps.
At discriminative step
t
, the optimization is trivial:

\begin{split}{{}\check{p}_{t+1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\right)}&=\argmin_{{}\check{p}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\text{H}_{p{}\hat{p}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{t}}}\right]}}+\KL\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{{}\check{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)}\middle|\middle|{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{t}}}\right)}}}\right\}}}\right\}\\
&={{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{t}}}\right)}.\end{split}

The conclusion follows because the first term (the marginal cross entropy) doesn’t depend on the proxy recognition distribution; because the second term, the relative entropy, is minimal (0) when its arguments are equal; and because we have assumed the model recognition distribution is computable, and therefore an available choice for the proxy recognition distribution.
Thus the algorithm becomes:

EM Algorithm under exact inference

\bullet\>

E step
:

{{}\check{p}_{t+1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\right)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{t}}}\right)}

\bullet\>

M step
:

{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{t+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}\argmin_{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathcal{F}({\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}},{}\check{p}_{t+1})}}\right\}
.

That is, we will carry out the optimization in Eq.
6.5
with an iterative process, at each iteration of which we take expectations under the model recognition distribution from the previous iteration.
For example, to update our estimate of the mean of the GMM, we will indeed use Eq.
6.4
, except that the recognition distribution under which the expectations are taken will be evaluated at the parameters from the previous iteration.
This eliminates the dependence on
{\bm{{\mu}}_{k}}
from the right-hand side of the equation.

As we saw in the informal proof in the previous section, this procedure is guaranteed to decrease (or, more precisely, not to increase) an upper bound on the loss we actually care about, the marginal cross entropy.
In this version of the algorithm, we can say more.
At each E step, the relative-entropy term in Eq.
6.6
is not merely reduced but eliminated.
So at the start of every M step, the bound is tight.
That means that any decrease in free energy at this point entails a decrease in marginal cross entropy—a better model for the data.
Typically this decrease in free energy will also be accompanied by an increase in the relative-entropy term.
But so far from being a bad thing, this corresponds to an even larger decrease in marginal cross entropy than the decrease in free energy.
And at the next step E step, the relative entropy is again eliminated—the bound is again made tight.

In the examples we consider next, the M step is carried out in closed form, so every parameter update either decreases the marginal cross entropy or does nothing at all.
In contrast, for models in which the M step is carried out by gradient descent, a decrease in free energy need not correspond to a decrease in marginal cross entropy:
As the free energy decreases across the course of the M step, the relative entropy can open back up—the bound can loosen—and therefore further decreases can correspond to the bound tightening again, rather than the marginal cross entropy decreasing.
Indeed, the marginal cross entropy can
increase
during this period of bound tightening.
But it can never increase above its value at at the beginning of the M step, when the bound was tight.

Applying EM.

The EM “algorithm” is a in fact a kind of meta-algorithm or recipe for estimating densities with latent variables.
To derive actual algorithms we need to apply EM to specific graphical models.
In the following sections we apply EM to some of the most classic latent-variable models.

As usual, we attempt to minimize cross entropies.
However, to keep the derivation general, we will avoid specifying the averaging distribution until as late as possible, simply calling it “
{}\check{p}
.”
Thus,
the following derivations apply equally well to fully observed models
, in which case
