{{}\check{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\right)}={p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\right)},

as they do to a single step in EM, in which case

{{}\check{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\right)}={{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}^{\text{old}}}}\right)}{p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\right)}.

6.4.1
The Gaussian mixture model and
K
-means

We return once again to the GMM, but this time, armed with the EM algorithm, we shall finally derive the learning rules.
The complete cross entropy for a Gaussian mixture model is

\begin{split}\text{H}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right]}&\approx{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{-\log\prod_{{\mathit{k}}=1}^{\mathit{{K}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mu}}_{{\mathit{k}}}},{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}\right){\mathit{\pi}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}\right]^{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{-\sum_{{\mathit{k}}=1}^{\mathit{{K}}}\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{1}{2}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}^{-1}}}\right\rvert-\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mu}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right){\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mu}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)+\log{\mathit{\pi}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}\right]}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}+C.\end{split}

To enforce the fact that the source probabilities sum to one, we can augment the loss with a Lagrangian term:

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}({\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})&=\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{{\mathit{k}}=1}^{\mathit{{K}}}{\mathit{\pi}_{{\mathit{k}}}}-1}}\right)+\text{H}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right]}\\
&=\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{{\mathit{k}}=1}^{\mathit{{K}}}{\mathit{\pi}_{{\mathit{k}}}}-1}}\right)+{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{-\sum_{{\mathit{k}}=1}^{\mathit{{K}}}\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{1}{2}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}^{-1}}}\right\rvert-\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mu}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right){\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mu}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)+\log{\mathit{\pi}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}\right]}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}+C.\end{split}

We take the derivatives in turn.
First the mixing proportions:

0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{L}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\mathit{\pi}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}=\lambda-\frac{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}}{{\mathit{\pi}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}\implies\sum_{{\mathit{k}}=1}^{\mathit{{K}}}{\mathit{\pi}_{{\mathit{k}}}}\lambda=\sum_{{\mathit{k}}=1}^{\mathit{{K}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\implies\lambda=1\implies{\mathit{\pi}_{{\mathit{k}}}}={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}};

then the class-conditional means:

0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}\frac{\partial{\mathcal{L}}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mu}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}^{\text{T}}}={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mu}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)^{\text{T}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}^{-1}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\implies{\bm{{\mu}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}=\frac{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}}{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}};

and the class-conditional covariances:

\begin{split}0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{L}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}^{-1}}}={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{-\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{1}{2}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mu}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mu}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)^{\text{T}}}}\right]}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\implies{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}&=\frac{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mu}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mu}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}}{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}}\\
&=\frac{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}}{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}}-{\bm{{\mu}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}{\bm{{\mu}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}^{\text{T}}.\end{split}

Whether in EM or under a fully observed model, the optimal parameters are intuitive.
The optimal mixing proportion, emission mean, and emission covariance for class
{\mathit{k}}
are their sample counterparts, i.e. the sample proportion, sample mean, and sample covariance (resp.); or, to put it yet another way, the average number of occurrences of class
{\mathit{k}}
, the average value of the samples
\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}
from class
{\mathit{k}}
, and the average covariance of the samples
\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}
from class
{\mathit{k}}
.
The difference between learning (in EM) with latent, rather than fully observed, classes is that these are
weighted
, rather than unweighted, averages.
In particular, class assignments are soft: each class takes some continuous-valued
responsibility
†
†
margin:

class responsibilities

†
†
margin:

class responsibilities

for each datum
{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}_{n}
, namely

\mathbb{E}_{{}\hat{p}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}_{n}}}\right]}={{}\check{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}_{n}}}\right)}
, the probability of that class under the (previous) recognition distribution.

For example, when the class labels are observed, the denominator in the equation for the optimal mean becomes just the number of times class
{\mathit{k}}
occurred, and the numerator picks out just those samples
{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}_{n}
associated with class
{\mathit{k}}
.
This is the sample average of the
{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}_{n}
in class
{\mathit{k}}
.
But when the class
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}
is latent, the denominator is the average
soft
assignment or responsibility of class
{\mathit{k}}
, and the numerator is the (soft) average of
all
observations
{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}_{n}
, each weighted by the responsibility that class
{\mathit{k}}
takes for it.

Evidently, the expected sufficient statistics are

{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}
,

{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}
, and

{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}
.
During EM, i.e. when the averaging distribution is….

K
-means

In Section
2.1.1
, we saw what happens to the recognition distribution of the GMM when all classes use the same covariance matrix,
{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}}
.
The class boundaries become lines, and the responsibility of class
j
for observation
{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}
becomes

\begin{split}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{j}=1\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}&=\frac{\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{-\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}-{\bm{{\mu}}_{j}}}}\right)^{\text{T}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}^{-1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}-{\bm{{\mu}}_{j}}}}\right)}}\right\}{\mathit{\pi}_{j}}}{\sum_{{\mathit{k}}=1}^{\mathit{{K}}}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{-\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}-{\bm{{\mu}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}\right)^{\text{T}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}^{-1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}-{\bm{{\mu}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}\right)}}\right\}{\mathit{\pi}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}.\end{split}

(6.7)

It is not hard to see that in the limit of infinite precision, this quantity goes to zero unless
{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}
is closer to
{\bm{{\mu}}_{j}}
than any other mean, in which case it goes to 1.
The “source” probabilities
{\bm{{\mathit{\pi}}}}
have become irrelevant.
Then the algorithm becomes

K
-Means

\bullet\>

E step
:

{{}\check{p}_{t+1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{j}=1\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}\right)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}\begin{cases}1,&\text{if }j=\argmin_{{\mathit{k}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}-{\bm{{\mu}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}\right\rVert\\
0,&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\bullet\>

M step
:

{\bm{{\mu}}_{j}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}\frac{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{{}\check{p}_{t+1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{j}=1\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}}{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{{}\check{p}_{t+1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{j}=1\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}}

This algorithm, which pre-existed EM for the GMM, is known as
K
-means
†
†
margin:

K
-means

†
†
margin:

K
-means

.

6.4.2
Hidden Markov model

The average is across all sequences…. Here we use
superscripts
for the “time variable” (
t
), i.e. to denote a random variable’s order in the sequence, so that subscripts can continue to pick out a single entry (
{\mathit{k}}
) in the random vector
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}
.
Bare random variables, i.e. without super- or subscripts, denote the complete set of observations.
The elements of the state-transition matrix
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}
are referred to as
{\mathit{a}_{ij}}
, with
i
and
j
indicating row and column, respectively.
We also use the abbreviation

{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}^{1}}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}^{\ignorespaces 0}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}:={{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}^{1}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}=\text{Categ}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{\pi}}}}}}\right)
.

\begin{split}\text{H}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right]}&\approx{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{-\log\prod_{t=1}^{{T}}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{t}}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{-\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{t=2}^{{T}}\prod_{i=1}^{\mathit{{K}}}\prod_{j=1}^{\mathit{{K}}}{\mathit{a}_{ij}}^{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}^{t}_{i}}{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}^{t-1}_{j}}}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{{\mathit{k}}=1}^{\mathit{{K}}}{\mathit{\pi}_{{\mathit{k}}}}^{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}^{1}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{t=1}^{{T}}\prod_{{\mathit{k}}=1}^{\mathit{{K}}}\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mu}}_{{\mathit{k}}}},{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}\right)^{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}^{t}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}}\right)}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{-\sum_{t=2}^{{T}}\sum_{i=1}^{\mathit{{K}}}\sum_{j=1}^{\mathit{{K}}}{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}^{t}_{i}}{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}^{t-1}_{j}}\log{\mathit{a}_{ij}}-\sum_{{\mathit{k}}=1}^{\mathit{{K}}}{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}^{1}_{{\mathit{k}}}}\log{\mathit{\pi}_{{\mathit{k}}}}-\sum_{t=1}^{{T}}\sum_{{\mathit{k}}=1}^{\mathit{{K}}}{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}^{t}_{{\mathit{k}}}}\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mu}}_{{\mathit{k}}}},{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}\right)}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}.\end{split}

Again we need to enforce probabilities summing to one, in this case both the source probability
{\bm{{\mathit{\pi}}}}
and all
\mathit{{K}}
columns of the state-transition matrix
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}
.
The Lagrangian becomes

\mathcal{L}({\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})=\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{{\mathit{k}}=1}^{\mathit{{K}}}{\mathit{\pi}_{{\mathit{k}}}}-1}}\right)+\sum_{j=1}^{\mathit{{K}}}\eta_{j}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\mathit{{K}}}{\mathit{a}_{ij}}-1}}\right)+\text{H}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right]}.

The derivative with respect to
{\mathit{\pi}_{{\mathit{k}}}}
is exactly as above, just confined to the very first sample (
t=1
), and so the optimal source probability is

{\mathit{\pi}_{{\mathit{k}}}}={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}^{1}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}.

Likewise for the class-conditional means and covariances, although here we have to remember to sum across all samples:

\begin{split}{\bm{{\mu}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}=\frac{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\sum_{t=1}^{{{T}}}{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}^{t}_{{\mathit{k}}}}{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{t}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}}{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\sum_{t=1}^{{{T}}}{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}^{t}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}},\qquad{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}&=\frac{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\sum_{t=1}^{{{T}}}{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}^{t}_{{\mathit{k}}}}{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{t}}{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{t}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}}{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\sum_{t=1}^{{{T}}}{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}^{t}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}}-{\bm{{\mu}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}{\bm{{\mu}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}^{\text{T}}.\end{split}

Finally, we derive the optimal state-transition probabilities:

\begin{split}0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}\frac{\partial{\mathcal{L}}}{\partial{{\mathit{a}_{ij}}}^{\text{T}}}=\eta_{j}-\frac{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\sum_{t=2}^{{{T}}}{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}^{i}_{t}}{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}^{j}_{t-1}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}}{{\mathit{a}_{ij}}}&\implies{\mathit{a}_{ij}}\eta_{j}={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\sum_{t=2}^{{{T}}}{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}^{i}_{t}}{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}^{j}_{t-1}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
&\implies\sum_{i=1}^{\mathit{{K}}}{\mathit{a}_{ij}}\eta_{j}={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\sum_{t=2}^{{{T}}}\sum_{i=1}^{\mathit{{K}}}{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}^{i}_{t}}{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}^{j}_{t-1}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
&\implies\eta_{j}={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\sum_{t=2}^{{{T}}}{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}^{j}_{t-1}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
&\implies{\mathit{a}_{ij}}=\frac{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\sum_{t=2}^{{{T}}}{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}^{i}_{t}}{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}^{j}_{t-1}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}}{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\sum_{t=2}^{{{T}}}{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}^{j}_{t-1}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}}\end{split}

The optimal parameters again have intuitive interpretations.
The class-conditional means and covariance are computed exactly as with the GMM, except that the averages are now across time and independent sequences rather than independent samples.
In the case of EM, there is one other crucial distinction from the GMM:
The recognition means of the HMM are conditioned on samples from
all time
; that is, they are the
smoother means
.
This distinction has no relevance in the GMM, which has no temporal dimension.

Similarly, the optimal mixing proportions for the
initial
state look like the optimal mixing proportions for the latent classes in the GMM, although the sample average for the HMM is over sequences, rather than individual samples.
One upshot is that it would be impossible to estimate the initial mixing proportions properly without access to multiple, independent sequences (this makes sense).
And again, we must be careful in the case of EM: the expectation should be taken under the
smoother distribution over the initial state
.
That is, to estimate the initial mixing proportions, the inference algorithm should first run all the way to the end (filter) and back (smoother)!
This may at first be surprising, but note that future observations should indeed have some (albeit diminishing) influence on our belief about initial state.
(We can imagine, colorfully, an unexpected future observation in light of which we revise our belief about the initial state: “Oh, I guess it must have started in state five, then….”)

We turn to the remaining parameters, the elements of the state-transition matrix,
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}
.
When the states have been fully observed, the optimal
{\mathit{a}_{ij}}
is merely the proportion of times state
j
transitioned to state
i
(rather than some other state).
Under EM, the numerator is the
expected
frequency of state
j
followed by
i
, where the expectation is taken under the smoother.
To transform this into a transition probability, the base frequency of state
j
must be taken into account; under EM, we estimate it with its expected frequency under the smoother (averaged over sequences).

6.4.3
Factor analysis and principal-components analysis

Retaining the Gaussian emissions from the GMM but exchanging the categorical latent variable for a standard normal variate yields “factor analysis” (see Section
2.1.2
).
We also restrict the emission covariance to be diagonal in order to remove a degree of freedom that is not (as we shall see) identifiable from the data.
The model is fully described by Eq.
2.20
.
However, we depart slightly from that formulation by augmenting the latent variable vector
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}
with a “random” scalar that is 1 with probability 1.
This allows us to absorb the bias
{\bm{{\mathit{c}}}}
into a column of the emission matrix
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}
, reducing clutter without reducing generality.

The learning problem starts once again with minimization of the joint cross entropy:

\begin{split}\text{H}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right]}&\approx{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{-\log\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{\mathbf{D}}}}\right)-\log\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{0}},\bm{\mathbf{I}}}}\right)}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\\
\end{split}

(6.8)

The model source distribution does not depend on any parameters, so only the model emission is differentiated.
Starting with the emission matrix
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}
:

\begin{split}0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\text{H}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}}}&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{-\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}}}\log\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{\mathbf{D}}}}\right)}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\\
&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}}}\right)^{\text{T}}\bm{\mathbf{D}}^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}}}\right)}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\\
&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{\mathbf{D}}^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}}}\right)\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\\
\implies\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}^{-1}},\end{split}

the normal equations.
Thus, in a fully observed model, finding
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}
amounts to linear regression.

The emission covariance also takes on a familiar form:

\begin{split}0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\text{H}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mathbf{D}}^{-1}}}&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{-\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mathbf{D}}^{-1}}}\log\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{\mathbf{D}}}}\right)}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{-\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mathbf{D}}^{-1}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{1}{2}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\bm{\mathbf{D}}^{-1}}}\right\rvert-\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}}}\right)^{\text{T}}\bm{\mathbf{D}}^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}}}\right)}}\right]}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{\mathbf{D}}-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}}}\right)^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
\implies\bm{\mathbf{D}}&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}}}\right)^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
\end{split}

The final line can be simplified using our newly acquired formula for
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}
.
First expanding the quadratic and then applying the identity:

\begin{split}\bm{\mathbf{D}}&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}-\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{\text{T}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{\text{T}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\\
&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}-{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}+\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}\\
&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}.\end{split}

Now, we require
\bm{\mathbf{D}}
to be diagonal.
It may be observed that the derivative with respect to any particular entry of
\bm{\mathbf{D}}
is independent of all other entries, so simply setting some components to zero does not change the optimum for the other components.
(This can be made rigorous, albeit pedantic, with a Lagrangian formulation.)
So we merely extract the diagonal from the final equation.

The E step.

In Section
2.1.2
, we derived the recognition distribution for factor analysis:

{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}=\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}\bm{\mathbf{D}}^{-1}{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}\bm{\mathbf{D}}^{-1}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}+\bm{\mathbf{I}}}}\right)^{-1}}}\right).

(6.9)

In the E step, then, the expected sufficient statistics for
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}
and
\bm{\mathbf{D}}
are therefore calculated as

\begin{split}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{\text{T}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right]}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\bm{\mathbf{D}}^{-1}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\bm{\mathbf{D}}^{-1}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}}\end{split}

and

\begin{split}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{\text{T}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right]}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\text{Cov}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right]}+\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right]}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{\text{T}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right]}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\\
&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}\bm{\mathbf{D}}^{-1}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}+\bm{\mathbf{I}}}}\right)^{-1}+{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}\bm{\mathbf{D}}^{-1}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}\bm{\mathbf{D}}^{-1}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\\
&=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}\bm{\mathbf{D}}^{-1}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}+\bm{\mathbf{I}}}}\right)^{-1}+{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}\bm{\mathbf{D}}^{-1}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\bm{\mathbf{D}}^{-1}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}|{}\hat{y}}}.\end{split}

Principal-components analysis

We saw that in the limit of equal and infinite emission precisions, EM for the GMM reduces to
K
-means.
Now we investigate this limit in the case of EM for the factor analyzer.
In this case the only parameter to estimate is
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}
.

From Eq.
6.9
, we see that the recognition covariance goes to zero as
\bm{\mathbf{D}}^{-1}
goes to infinity—recognition becomes deterministic.
With slightly more work, we can also determine the mean,
{\bm{{\mathit{\bar{x}}}}}
, to which each
{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}
is deterministic assigned.
Setting
\bm{\mathbf{D}}=\epsilon\bm{\mathbf{I}}
, we find

\bm{{\mathit{{\bar{X}}}}}:=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{\text{T}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right]}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}\frac{\bm{\mathbf{I}}}{\epsilon}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}+\bm{\mathbf{I}}}}\right)^{-1}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}\frac{\bm{\mathbf{I}}}{\epsilon}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}+\epsilon\bm{\mathbf{I}}}}\right)^{-1}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\xrightarrow[\epsilon\to 0]{}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}}}\right)^{-1}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}.

The final expression is the Moore-Penrose pseudo-inverse of
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}
, i.e., the latent-space projection of
\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}
that yields the smallest reconstruction error under the emission matrix
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}
.

[……]

Iterative Principal-Components Analysis

\bullet\>

E step
:

\bm{{\mathit{{\bar{X}}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}}}\right)^{-1}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}

\bullet\>

M step
:

\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}
{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bar{X}}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}

{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{\bar{X}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bar{X}}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}^{-1}}
,

6.4.4
Linear-Gaussian state-space models

Exactly analogously to the extension of GMMs to HMMs, we extend factor analysis through time (or space) into a dynamical system.
Now the state is a vector of continuous values, and assumed to be normally distributed about a linear function of its predecessor…..

We again forebear to specify the averaging distribution….

\begin{split}\text{H}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right]}&\approx{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{-\log\prod_{t=1}^{{T}}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{-\sum_{t=2}^{{T}}\log\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}},{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}}}}\right)-\sum_{t=1}^{{T}}\log\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}},{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{y}|{}\hat{x}}}}}\right)-\log\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mu}}_{1}},{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{1}}}}\right)}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}.\end{split}

All three summands are Gaussians, so we only consider in detail the differentiation of one.
Optimizing first with respect to
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}
, we find again the familiar normal equations:

\begin{split}0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\text{H}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}}}&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{-\sum_{t=2}^{{T}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}}}\log\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}},{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}}}}\right)}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\sum_{t=2}^{{T}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}}}\frac{1}{2}({\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}})^{\text{T}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}^{-1}}({\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}})}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\sum_{t=2}^{{T}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}^{-1}}({\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}}){\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
\implies\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}&=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{t=2}^{{T}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{t=2}^{{T}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}}}\right)^{-1}\\
&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}^{-1},\end{split}

where in the last line we intepret the average to be under samples from within, as well as across, sequences.
†
†
margin:

implementation note
: Since each sequence contributes only
{{T}}-1
samples, one must remember to subtract
N_{\text{sequences}}
from the total number of samples across sequences before normalizing.

†
†
margin:

implementation note
: Since each sequence contributes only
{{T}}-1
samples, one must remember to subtract
N_{\text{sequences}}
from the total number of samples across sequences before normalizing.

Turning to the covariance matrix,

\begin{split}0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\text{H}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}^{-1}}}}&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{-\sum_{t=2}^{{T}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}^{-1}}}}\log\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}},{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}}}}\right)}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{}}}\\
&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{-\sum_{t=2}^{{T}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}^{-1}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{1}{2}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}^{-1}}}}\right\rvert-\frac{1}{2}({\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}})^{\text{T}}{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}^{-1}}({\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}})}}\right]}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{}}}\\
&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{-\sum_{t=2}^{{T}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}}-({\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}})({\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}})^{\text{T}}}}\right]}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{}}}\\
\implies{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}}&=\frac{1}{{{T}}-1}\sum_{t=2}^{{T}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{({\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}})({\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}})^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{}}}\\
&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{({\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}})({\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}})^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{}}}\\
&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{}}}.\end{split}

where again in the penultimate line we changed the interpretation of the average to be within as well as across sequences.
The final simplification was carried out with the equation for
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}
, exactly the same as with factor analysis (see above).

Derivations precisely analogous lead to formalae for the other cumulants.
Here are all of the equations:

\displaystyle\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}^{-1}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}}={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{}}}

(6.10)

\displaystyle\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{t}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}^{-1}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{y}|{}\hat{x}}}={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\bm{{\mu}}_{1}}={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{1}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{1}}={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}-{\bm{{\mu}}_{1}}{\bm{{\mu}}_{1}}^{\text{T}}.

The E step.

In Eq.
6.10
, the bracketed quantities (including the brackets) are the
sufficient statistics
.
They are all averages of either vectors or outer products of vectors, reflecting the quadratic structure inherent in normal distributions.
When the states
{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}={\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{t}}
are observed, these outer products are simply averaged under the data distribution.
In the context of the EM algorithm, where the states are unobserved, the relevant averaging distributions
{}\check{p}
are the RTS smoothing distributions, multiplied by the data distribution:

\begin{split}{{}\check{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}},{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}}\right)}&={{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};\theta^{\text{old}}}}\right)}{p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)}\\
{{}\check{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}},{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)}&={{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}},{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};\theta^{\text{old}}}}\right)}{p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)}.\end{split}

(We emphasize again that
\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}:={\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{1}},\ldots,{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{{{T}}}}
.)
In EM, Eq.
6.10
corresponds to the M step.
Computing the sufficient statistics belongs to the E step.
That is, the E step requires first running the RTS smoother—more specifically, the Kalman filter followed by the RTS smoother—and then computing the sufficient statistics under them.
To make this extremely concrete, we note that having the smoother distribution in hand means having a mean (vector) and covariance matrix at every time step, since the distribution is normal.
To compute expected outer products, then, we have to combine these together.

For
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{A}}}}}}
and
{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{x}}}
we need

\begin{split}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{}}}&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\frac{1}{{{T}}-1}\sum_{t=2}^{{{T}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\text{Cov}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right]}+\mathbb{E}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right]}\mathbb{E}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}}^{\text{T}}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right]}}}\right]}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{}}}&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\frac{1}{{{T}}-1}\sum_{t=2}^{{{T}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\text{Cov}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}},{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right]}+\mathbb{E}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right]}\mathbb{E}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t-1}}^{\text{T}}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right]}}}\right]}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{}}}&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\frac{1}{{{T}}-1}\sum_{t=2}^{{{T}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\text{Cov}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right]}+\mathbb{E}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right]}\mathbb{E}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}^{\text{T}}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right]}}}\right]}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}.\end{split}

Notice that all sums
start at the second index
, and (consequently)
are normalized by
{{T}}-1
.
That is why the first and third statistics are not identical.
If the sequence is long enough, including all
{{T}}
terms in the first and last statistics will probably have little effect, but both averages can be computed with very little computational cost.

For
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}
and
{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{{}\hat{y}|{}\hat{x}}}
, in contrast, we collect statistics for
all
time.
Thus, even though we also need a statistic we have called

{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}}}
,
in this case the average is over all
t
:

\begin{split}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{}}}&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\frac{1}{{{T}}}\sum_{t=1}^{{{T}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\text{Cov}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right]}+\mathbb{E}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right]}\mathbb{E}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}^{\text{T}}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right]}}}\right]}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{t}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\frac{1}{{{T}}}\sum_{t=1}^{{{T}}}{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{t}}\mathbb{E}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{t}}^{\text{T}}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right]}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{t}}{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{t}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\frac{1}{{{T}}}\sum_{t=1}^{{{T}}}{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{t}}{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{t}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
\end{split}

Finally, the sufficient statistics for the initial cumulants,
{\bm{{\mu}}_{1}}
and
{\bm{\mathbf{{\sigma}}}_{1}}
, require no sums at all, since they rely only on the first time step:

\begin{split}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{1}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{}}}&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\mathbb{E}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{1}}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right]}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{1}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{1}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{}}}&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\mathbb{E}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{1}}{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}^{1}}^{\text{T}}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right]}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
\end{split}

†
†
margin:

implementation note
: The second of these
can yield a low-rank covariance matrix if only a single trajectory has been observed
, and care is sometimes required to avoid inverting a singular matrix in the Kalman filter.

†
†
margin:

implementation note
: The second of these
can yield a low-rank covariance matrix if only a single trajectory has been observed
, and care is sometimes required to avoid inverting a singular matrix in the Kalman filter.

6.5
Parameterized proxy recognition distributions

To widen our view of target models for EM, let us drop the very restrictive assumption that the model recognition distribution,
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
, can be computed.
It will therefore no longer be possible to tighten the bound completely in the E step.
At this price, however, we have bought the ability to work with much more complicated generative models.
The form of the proxy recognition distribution will now be fixed, not by the model recognition distribution, but by our choice.

6.5.1
Gaussian proxy recognition distributions and sparse coding

For continuous random latent variables, the simplest choice is perhaps the (multivariate) normal distribution:

{{}\check{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\right)}=\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\nu}}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}},{\bm{\mathbf{P}}^{-1}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}}}\right).

Note that we are allowing the mean and precision to depend on the observations, although we have not as yet specified how.
To derive an EM algorithm under this proxy recognition distribution, we start with the free energy.
Recall (from the first line of Eq.
6.6
) that the free energy can be written as the difference of two entropies: the cross entropy of the joint distribution, and the entropy of the proxy recognition distribution (averaged under the data).
The latter is now just the entropy of a multivariate normal distribution, averaged under the data:

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log{{}\check{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)}}}\right]}&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\frac{1}{2}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert{\bm{\mathbf{P}}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}}}\right\rvert+\frac{\mathit{{K}}}{2}\log\tau+\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}-{\bm{{\nu}}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}}}\right)^{\text{T}}{\bm{\mathbf{P}}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}-{\bm{{\nu}}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}}}\right)}}\right]}\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathit{{K}}\log\tau+\mathit{{K}}-\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert{\bm{\mathbf{P}}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}}}\right\rvert}}\right]}}}\right).\end{split}

(6.11)

Subtracting this from the cross entropy of the joint, we have

\mathcal{F}({\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\phi}}}})=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right]}+\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{1}{2}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert{\bm{\mathbf{P}}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}}}\right\rvert}}\right]}+C,

(6.12)

lumping constant terms into
C
.
Notice that we have introduced the symbol
{\bm{{\mathit{\phi}}}}
to stand for all the (currently unspecified) parameters of the proxy recognition distributions.

Local parameters

Now let us assume that we will learn (in the E step) one pair of parameters for each observation,
{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}
; that is,

{\bm{{\mathit{\phi}}}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{\bm{{\nu}}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}_{1}}},{\bm{\mathbf{P}}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}_{1}}},\ldots,{\bm{{\nu}}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}_{{N}}}},{\bm{\mathbf{P}}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}_{{N}}}}}}\right\}.

(6.13)

The advantage of this approach is flexibility: the mean vector and precision matrix for each datum can take on arbitrary values.
The (potential) disadvantage is inefficiency:
Unless the parameters can be derived in closed-form, this approach will not scale well to a large number of observations.
Indeed, the recognition distribution for a new (or held-out) observation
{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}_{{N}+1}
cannot be fit any faster, or predicted, on the basis of the previous observations.
We shall explore alternatives shortly.

The E step.

It is instructive
[
21
]
to begin the E-step optimization, even without having specified a generative model.
Replacing the expectation over the data in Eq.
6.12
with a sample average and differentiating with respect to the recognition parameters (
{\bm{{\nu}}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}},{\bm{\mathbf{P}}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}
) associated with a single datum (
{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}
) clearly eliminates all the terms that depend on the parameters for other data.
So we can drop the expectation over the data altogether.
The gradient with respect to a recognition mean vector is then

\begin{split}\frac{\partial{\mathcal{F}}}{\partial{{\bm{{\nu}}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}}}&=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\nu}}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{}|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}{}}}\right]}\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\frac{\partial{\log{}\hat{p}}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}\right]}\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\partial{E_{\text{recog}}}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}\right]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}0.\end{split}

(6.14)

The second line can be derived by exploiting the symmetry of
{\bm{{\nu}}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}
and
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}
in a Gaussian function, and then integrating by parts.In the third line we have put the gradient in terms of the energy of the model recognition distribution, which can easily be seen to have the same gradient as the negative log of the model joint.
Similarly, the gradient with respect to a recognition covariance matrix is

\begin{split}\frac{\partial{\mathcal{F}}}{\partial{{\bm{\mathbf{P}}^{-1}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}}}&=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{\mathbf{P}}^{-1}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{}|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}{}}}\right]}-\frac{1}{2}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert{\bm{\mathbf{P}}^{-1}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}}}\right\rvert}}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}0\\
\implies{\bm{\mathbf{P}}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}&=2\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{\mathbf{P}}^{-1}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{}|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}{}}}\right]}\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\frac{\partial^{2}{\log{}\hat{p}}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}\right]}\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\partial^{2}{E_{\text{recog}}}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}\right]}.\end{split}

(6.15)

The third line can be derived by meticuously differentiating the normal distribution (or, more precisely,
f({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}})\log f({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}})
for normal
f({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}})
) with respect to both
{\bm{\mathbf{P}}^{-1}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}
and
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}
(twice), and then integrating by parts;
or, more elegantly, by expressing the expectations in the Fourier domain (using Plancherel’s theorem) and differentiating there
[
21
]
.

We have written the optimizations for the mean and precision in the form of Eqs.
6.14
and
6.15
in order to highlight the resemblance to the Laplace approximation encountered in Chapter
2
(see especially Eq.
2.32
)
[
21
]
.
In particular, Eqs.
6.14
and
6.15
become the Laplace approximation when the expectations commute with the energy-gradient and energy-Hessian functions of
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}
.
This happens precisely when the order of the energy in
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}
is quadratic or less.

Independent sources and Gaussian emissions: sparse coding and other models

To make the procedure more concrete, let us make a few further assumptions.
Although this is by no means obligatory, the emissions have been Gaussian in all our examples heretofore, so we shall make the same choice here.
For simplicity, we shall even let the noise be isotropic.
The source random variables, on the other hand, we shall assume to be independent, but otherwise distributed generically:

\displaystyle{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}=\frac{1}{Z({\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}\prod_{{\mathit{k}}=1}^{\mathit{{K}}}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{-E_{{\mathit{k}}}(\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}\right\},

\displaystyle{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}=\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}},\frac{1}{\lambda}\bm{\mathbf{I}}}}\right).

(6.16)

Among other models, Eq.
6.16
includes the
sparse-coding
models encountered in Section
2.1.3
, in which each emission
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}}{}
is assembled from a modest number of basis vectors drawn from a large (overcomplete) dictionary—i.e., an emission matrix
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}
that is “fat.”
To enforce sparsity, the energy functions for the source variables can be chosen from leptokurtotic distributions.
For example, we considered energy functions for the Laplace distribution and a smooth approximation thereof:

\begin{split}E_{{\mathit{k}}}(\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}})&:=\alpha_{{\mathit{k}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}\right\rvert\\
&\approx\frac{\alpha_{{\mathit{k}}}}{\beta}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\cosh(\beta\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}})}}\right)\>\>\>\text{for large }\beta.\end{split}

(6.17)

This ensures that the source variables
\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}
take on values close to zero more frequently than normal random variables with similar variance.
Unfortunately, as we saw, such fat-tailed distributions are not conjugate to the likelihood for the basis coefficients specified by Eq.
6.16
, i.e. the Gaussian emission density, so they require approximate inference.

Here we are considering Gaussian proxy recognition distributions.
For the sake of generality, however, we shall refrain at first from assuming any form for the energy functions, deriving our results for the more general class described by Eq.
6.16
.
Now, the cross entropy of this joint distribution is:

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right]}&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\mathit{{K}}}{2}\log\lambda+\frac{\lambda}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}}}\right\rVert^{2}+\log Z({\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})+\sum_{{\mathit{k}}=1}^{\mathit{{K}}}E_{{\mathit{k}}}(\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}\right]}\\
&=\frac{\mathit{{K}}}{2}\log\lambda+\log Z({\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})+\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}}}\right\rVert^{2}+2\sum_{{\mathit{k}}=1}^{\mathit{{K}}}E_{{\mathit{k}}}(\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}\right]}.\end{split}

(6.18)

So far this follows directly from Eq.
6.16
.
The third term can be simplified further, however, by applying our assumption that the proxy recognition distribution is normal, and using Eq.
A.17
from the appendix:

\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}}}\right\rVert^{2}}}\right]}=\lambda\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\text{tr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{\mathbf{P}}^{-1}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{{\nu}}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}}}\right\rVert^{2}}}\right]}.

The M step.

We can now write a more specific expression for the free energy that will allow us to see how to carry out the M step, as well.
Let us simplify even further and treat
\lambda
and the parameters of the prior as fixed.
Then substituting the cross entropy of the joint, Eq.
6.18
, into the free energy for Gaussian proxy recognition distributions, Eq.
6.12
, yields

\mathcal{F}({\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\phi}}}})=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\lambda}{2}\text{tr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{\mathbf{P}}^{-1}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]+\frac{\lambda}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{{\nu}}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}}}\right\rVert^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert{\bm{\mathbf{P}}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}}}\right\rvert}}\right]}+\sum_{{\mathit{k}}=1}^{\mathit{{K}}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[E_{{\mathit{k}}}(\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}\right]}+C.

(6.19)

(All “constant” terms have been lumped into a single scalar
C
.)
The only remaining model parameter to be optimized is the emission matrix,
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}
.
We can compute the gradient of the free energy, Eq.
6.19
, with respect to
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}
using some results from the appendix (Section
A.1
):

\begin{split}\frac{\partial{\mathcal{F}}}{\partial{\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}}}&=\lambda\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{\mathbf{P}}^{-1}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{{\nu}}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}}}\right){{\bm{{\nu}}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}0\\
\implies\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{\bm{{\nu}}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\bm{\mathbf{P}}^{-1}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}+{\bm{{\nu}}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}{\bm{{\nu}}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}^{-1}},\end{split}

(6.20)

Thus, the emission matrix is once again given by some version of the normal equations.
In practice, however, if each
{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}
is (e.g.) an image, and the model is overcomplete, then the matrix inversions will be computationally challenging.
Instead, the gradient in Eq.
6.20
(or the natural gradient
[
17
,
18
]
) can be descended.

The E step.

To carry out the E step, we apply Eqs.
6.14
and
6.15
to the energy of the model recognition or joint distributions (they are equivalent up to constant terms), to wit, the bracketed terms in Eq.
6.18
.
Let us also commit to the source energies specified by Eq.
6.17
, i.e., the energy of the Laplace distribution.
Then the resulting recognition energy,

E_{\text{recog}}({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})=\frac{\lambda}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}}\right\rVert^{2}+\sum_{{\mathit{k}}=1}^{\mathit{{K}}}\alpha_{{\mathit{k}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}\right\rvert,

is quadratic in
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}
everywhere except at points where
{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}
is zero (for any
{\mathit{k}}
).
Therefore the energy-gradient function (of
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}
) and the expectation in Eq.
6.14
“almost” commute with each other, and the free-energy gradient becomes

\begin{split}\frac{\partial{\mathcal{F}}}{\partial{{\bm{{\nu}}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}}}&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\partial{E_{\text{recog}}}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}\right]}\\
&\approx\frac{\partial{E_{\text{recog}}}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}\right]},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)\\
&=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\nu}}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\lambda}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{{\nu}}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}}}\right\rVert^{2}+\bm{{\alpha}}^{\text{T}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert{\bm{{\nu}}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}}}\right\rvert}}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}0\\
\implies{\bm{{\nu}}^{*}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}&=\argmin_{{\bm{{\nu}}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{\lambda}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{{\nu}}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}}}\right\rVert^{2}+\bm{{\alpha}}^{\text{T}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert{\bm{{\nu}}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}}}\right\rvert}}\right\}.\end{split}

(6.21)

Thus
2
2

2

Technically the final line is only a sufficient, not a necessary, condition for the gradient to be zero, but we are assuming the Hessian is locally positive definite.
, the optimal choice for the mean is the solution to the
L^{1}
-penalized least-squares problem.
For “fat” matrices
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}
, this is known as basis-pursuit denoising,
3
3

3

This is also sometimes referred to as the LASSO problem , although typically that term is reserved for “tall” output matrices
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}
.

a convex optimization for which many solvers exist.

That leaves the covariance matrix, given by Eq.
6.15
.
Again we exploit the fact that the Hessian of the energy is a sub-cubic—indeed, constant—function of
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}
, except at points where any
{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}
is zero.
Therefore,

\begin{split}{\bm{\mathbf{P}}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}&=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\partial^{2}{E_{\text{recog}}}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}\right]}\\
&\approx\frac{\partial^{2}{E_{\text{recog}}}}{\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}\partial{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}\right]},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)\\
&\approx\lambda\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}+\text{diag}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\alpha}}\circ\beta\sech^{2}(\beta{\bm{{\nu}}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}})}}\right).\end{split}

(6.22)

The approximation in the final line follows from the approximation to the Laplace energy in Eq.
6.17
.

Let us collect up our optimizations for the complete sparse-coding algorithm:

EM Algorithm for Laplace-Sparse Coding

\bullet\>

E step
:

{\bm{{\nu}}^{(i+1)}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}\argmin_{{\bm{{\nu}}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{\lambda}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}-\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}_{i}{\bm{{\nu}}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}}}\right\rVert^{2}+\bm{{\alpha}}^{\text{T}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert{\bm{{\nu}}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}}}\right\rvert}}\right\}

{\bm{\mathbf{P}}^{(i+1)}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}\lambda\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}_{i}^{\text{T}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}_{i}+\text{diag}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\alpha}}\circ\beta\sech^{2}(\beta{\bm{{\nu}}^{(i+1)}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}})}}\right)

\bullet\>

M step
:

\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{\bm{{\nu}}^{(i+1)}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{{\bm{\mathbf{P}}^{(i+1)}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}}^{-1}+{\bm{{\nu}}^{(i+1)}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}{\bm{{\nu}}^{(i+1)}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}^{-1}}
.

Relationship to classical sparse coding.

We have arrived at a sparse-coding algorithm very similar to that of Lewicki and colleagues
[
17
,
18
]
, but by a somewhat different route.
In those papers, the matrix of basis functions,
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}
, is fit by directly descending along the gradient of the
marginal
cross entropy,
{\text{H}_{p{}\hat{p}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{};\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}}}\right]}}
, rather than descending via the gradient of its upper bound, the free energy.
The price, of course, is the intractability of the marginal cross entropy.
In the classical papers, this price is paid with Laplace’s method, approximating the model marginal,
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}}}\right)}
, with the integral

\int_{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}{}}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}}\right)}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}}}\right)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}{}}
in the vicinity of one of the modes,
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}^{0}}
, of the model joint (Eq.
2.35
).
This in some sense removes the latent variables from the problem, although the modes still need to be estimated.
Consequently, there is no E step, and no fixed parameters
{\bm{{\nu}}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}
and
{\bm{\mathbf{P}}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}
(for any
{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}
).

Nevertheless, the Hessian of the recognition (or joint) energy still shows up in the classical derivation’s loss, this time as a byproduct of Laplace’s method, as a measure of the volume of the recognition distribution.
This is no coincidence: we have allowed the E step to collapse into a Laplace approximation
[
21
]
by using an energy function that is sub-cubic almost everywhere.
However, in the classical method, this precision matrix is not fixed during optimization of
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}
, but rather is interpreted as a function of
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}
and
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}^{0}}
:
f(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}^{0}}):=\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert{\bm{\mathbf{P}}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}^{0}}}}\right)}}\right\rvert
.
On the other hand, the trace term in Eq.
6.19
,

\lambda\text{tr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{\mathbf{P}}^{-1}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]
,
never materializes, because the squared reconstruction error is simply evaluated at the mode, rather than being averaged under a proxy recognition distribution.

Conceptually, these two terms encode the contribution of the recognition precision (
{\bm{\mathbf{P}}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}
) to the two losses, ours and the classical.
Evidently, increasing the precision has opposite effects on the two losses, lowering the free energy (via
\lambda\text{tr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{\mathbf{P}}^{-1}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]
) but raising the approximate marginal cross entropy (via
\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert{\bm{\mathbf{P}}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}^{0}}}}\right)}}\right\rvert
).
But increasing the magnitude of
the emission matrix

\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}
has the
same
effect on these two terms:
It increases the contribution of recognition uncertainty to the average reconstruction error (
\lambda\text{tr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{\mathbf{P}}^{-1}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]
), and it increases the recognition precision itself
(and therefore
\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert{\bm{\mathbf{P}}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}^{0}}}}\right)}}\right\rvert
) via Eq.
6.22
.
Indeed, the partial derivative of

f(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}^{0}}):=\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert{\bm{\mathbf{P}}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}^{0}}}}\right)}}\right\rvert

with respect to
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}
is precisely the same as the derivative of the trace term

\lambda\text{tr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{\mathbf{P}}^{-1}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}^{\text{T}}}}\right]

with respect to
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}
(namely,
\lambda\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}{\bm{\mathbf{P}}^{-1}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}
).

There is one last discrepancy between the loss gradients.
