In the classical derivation, the mode
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}^{0}}
is not fixed, either, and in turns depends on
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}
.
So the
total
derivative of
\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert{\bm{\mathbf{P}}_{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}^{0}}(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}})}}\right)}}\right\rvert
generates extra terms.
On the other hand, in the original papers, these are derived and then promptly dropped, under the conjecture that curvature unrelated to volume is irrelevant to the optimization.

Independent-components analysis

Just as EM becomes
K
-means for the Gaussian mixture model and (iterative) principal-components analysis for the factor analyzer, as the variance of the (Gaussian) emission is shrunk to zero, EM for the sparse coding model becomes independent-components analysis (ICA)…..

6.5.2
Variational Autoencoders

The use of a separate posterior distribution for every observation,
{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}
, will clearly fail if the number of data is too large.
An alternative is to encourage the recognition distribution to use a single set of parameters for all data, by specifying a single, parameter-dependent mapping from the observations to the moments of the proxy recognition distribution.
For example, sticking with Gaussian recognition distributions, we can use

{{}\check{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\right)}=\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\nu}}(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\phi}}}})},{\bm{\mathbf{P}}^{-1}(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\phi}}}})}}}\right).

The moments,
{\bm{{\nu}}(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\phi}}}})}
and
{\bm{\mathbf{P}}^{-1}(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\phi}}}})}
, are now interpreted to be mappings or functions rather than parameters.
For example, the functions can be deep neural networks, and so in some sense arbitrarily flexible.
For sufficiently large
{\bm{{\mathit{\phi}}}}
, this flexibility could include (depending on the ratio of parameters to observations) the ability to store a separate mean and precision for every datum, and therefore to mimic Eq.
6.13
, but ideally it does not.
Limiting the flexibility of the functions allows for the computational cost of learning
{\bm{{\mathit{\phi}}}}
to be “amortized” across observations, and for the model to provide good recognition distributions for new observations
{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}
.

6.5.3
Diffusion Models

6.6
Proxy recognition distributions without parameters: Variational inference

6.7
Non-random “latent” variables.

To reiterate the central theme of this chapter: the fundamental trade-off for generative models is between expressive power and ease of inference.
So far we have explored three different strategies for trading off:
Severely limit expressive power to conjugate or pseudo-conjugate source distributions in order to allow for exact inference (Section
6.4
); allow arbitrarily expressive generative models, but then approximate inference with a recognition distribution that is either arbitrary, but highly parameterized (Section
6.5
); or correct up to simplifying but erroneous independence assumptions (Section
6.6
).
Now we introduce a fourth strategy: let the latent variables of the model be related to the observed variables by an invertible (and therefore deterministic) transformation.
This makes the model marginal
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
computable in closed-form with the standard rules of calculus for changing variables under an integral.
Consequently, the marginal cross entropy,
{\text{H}_{p{}\hat{p}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right]}}
, can be descended directly, rather than indirectly via the free energy, and EM is not needed.

Below we explore two such models in the usual way, starting with simple linear transformations and then moving to more complicated functions.
But let us begin with a more abstract formulation, in order to make contact with the unsupervised, discriminative learning problems of Section
5.2
.
In that section, we related observations
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}
to (hypothetical) latent variables
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}}
via a deterministic, invertible transformation, Eq.
5.25
.
Here we shall make a similar specification, although to emphasize that this is a generative model, we write the observations as a function of the latent variables,
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}
, rather than vice versa.
Still, to exhibit the relationship with the discriminative model, we denote this function as the inverse of some recognition function,
{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}}(\cdot,{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})
:

\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}={\bm{{\mathit{g}}}}^{-1}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}).

(6.23)

For generative models, it is also necessary to specify a distribution over the latent source variables.
Although the discriminative model makes no such specification, its training objective—maximization of the latent (“output”) entropy—will tend to produce independent outputs.
Therefore, we shall
assume
that the generative model’s latent variables are independent—and, for now, nothing else:

{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}=\prod_{{\mathit{k}}=1}^{\mathit{{K}}}{{}\hat{p}_{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}\right)}=\prod_{{\mathit{k}}=1}^{\mathit{{K}}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\mathit{\psi}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}\right).

(6.24)

In the second equality, we have simply expressed the probability distributions in terms of the cumulative distribution functions,
{\mathit{\psi}_{{\mathit{k}}}}(\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}_{{\mathit{k}}})
, or more precisely their derivatives, in anticipation of the results below.

In the discriminative models of Section
5.2
, rather than being specified explicitly, the distribution of latent variables was inherited from the data distribution,
{p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\right)}
, via the change-of-variables formula.
Here something like the reverse obtains:
The distribution of the (model) observations
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
is inherited, via the deterministic transformation Eq.
6.23
and the change-of-variables formula, from the distribution of latent variables, Eq.
6.24
:

\begin{split}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}&={{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{g}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}});{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}\right\rvert\\
&=\prod_{{\mathit{k}}=1}^{\mathit{{K}}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\mathit{\psi}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}_{{\mathit{k}}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathit{g}_{{\mathit{k}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}\right\rvert\\
&=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\psi}}}}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{g}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}\right)}}\right\rvert\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}\right\rvert\\
&=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{f}}}}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}\right\rvert.\end{split}

(6.25)

The third line follows from the fact that
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\psi}}}}}/\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}
is a diagonal matrix, so its determinant is just the product of the diagonal elements.
In the final line, we have combined the two determinants using

{\bm{{\mathit{f}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}}}\right):={\bm{{\mathit{\psi}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{g}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}}}\right)}}\right).

We have recovered Eq.
5.28
, but from a different model
[
5
]
.
In the discriminative model, the Jacobian
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{f}}}}}/\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}}}
results from an explicitly defined input-output relationship,
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}}={\bm{{\mathit{\psi}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})
.
In the generative model defined here,
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\psi}}}}}/\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}}}
is the result of the composition of
two
functions, an input-output relationship
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}={\bm{{\mathit{g}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})
, and the cumulative distribution functions of the latent variables,
{\bm{{\mathit{\psi}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}})
.
Thus the “outputs”
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}}
of the discriminative model are the latent variables of the generative model
passed through their own cdfs
.
If we define
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}}:={\bm{{\mathit{\psi}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}})
for the generative model, then we can say that the generative
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}}
are distributed independently (because
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}
are independent and
{\bm{{\mathit{\psi}}}}(\cdot)
acts elementwise) and uniformly (because the cdfs exactly flatten the distribution of
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}
).
This is consistent with the discriminative objective: maximizing the entropy of
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}}
will likewise tend to distribute them independently and uniformly.

Thus,
in the deterministic, invertible setting, density estimation in a generative model is equivalent to maximizing mutual information through a discriminative model
.
It might seem that this equivalence holds only for a subset of possible discriminative models:
Eq.
6.25
holds for Jacobian determinants
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{f}}}}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}\right\rvert
that can be factored as in the penultimate line,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\psi}}}}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{g}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}\right)}}\right\rvert\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}\right\rvert
, with

\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\psi}}}}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{g}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}}\right)
diagonal; whereas Eq.
5.28
holds for all invertible functions
{\bm{{\mathit{f}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}})
.
But this is a consequence of a non-essential, albeit useful, restriction imposed on the generative model in Eqs.
6.23
and
6.24
: that the sources be independent.
As long as the latent variables
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}
are uniformly distributed,

{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{g}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}});{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
reduces to a constant, and the final line of Eq.
6.25
follows immediately from the first (with
{\bm{{\mathit{g}}}}
in place of
{\bm{{\mathit{f}}}}
).

6.7.1
InfoMax ICA, revisited

This equivalence was noted first historically
[
5
]
for a specific model, InfoMax ICA
[
3
]
, which we first encountered in Section
5.2
.
In this simplest case, the observations are related to the “latent” variables by a square, full-rank matrix:

\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}=\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}=\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}^{-1}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}.

Substituting this relationship (cf. Eq.
6.23
) into Eq.
6.25
, we see that the marginal distribution of the observed variables is

{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\psi}}}}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}}}\right)}}\right\rvert\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}},

where again
{\bm{{\mathit{\psi}}}}
is the array of cdfs for each of the independent source variables.
As a consequence of the discriminative-generative duality, fitting this marginal density follows the same gradient as in InfoMax ICA, Eq.
5.31
,

\begin{split}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}}}\KL\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)}\middle|\middle|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{f}}}}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}})}}\right\rvert}}\right\}&=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\psi}}}}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}})}}\right\rvert}}\right]}-{\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}}^{-\text{T}}.\end{split}

That is, InfoMax ICA can be implemented as density estimation in a generative model with sources distributed independently and cumulatively according to
{\bm{{\mathit{\psi}}}}

[
5
]
; see Fig.
6.1
.

Note that
\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{C}}}}}}=\bm{{\mathit{{\bm{\mathbf{G}}}}}}^{-1}
.

But we haven’t specified
{\bm{{\mathit{\psi}}}}
!
This omission may have seemed minor in the discriminative model—sigmoidal nonlinearities in neural networks are typically selected rather freely—but is striking in a generative model.
And indeed, the choice matters.
Suppose we had let the sigmoidal function be the cdf of a Gaussian.
Then independent outputs
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Z}}}}}
could be achieved merely by decorrelating the inputs, since for jointly Gaussian random variables, uncorrelatedness implies independence.
In contrast, the (standard) logistic function is super-Gaussian (leptokurtotic), so InfoMax ICA with logistic outputs will generally do more than decorrelate its inputs.
This may seem remarkable, given the visually minor discrepancy between the Gaussian cdf and the logistic function (B.A. Olshausen, personal communication).
Now we see the advantage of the generative perspective, from which this difference is more salient—and at long last, (partially) answer the question of how to choose the feedforward nonlinearity,
{\bm{{\mathit{\psi}}}}
, in InfoMax ICA.

6.7.2
Nonlinear independent-component estimation

[[XXX]]

6.7.3
Minimum description length and the “bits-back” argument

The sender-receiver game.

There is another way to think about the free energy
[
14
]
, most intuitively expressed in terms of the Gaussian mixture model (see Section
2.1.1
and again Section
6.4.1
below).

†
†
margin:

P
ut a figure of GMM data here!

†
†
margin:

Put a figure of GMM data here!

Imagine that I (the “sender”) want to communicate an observed datum,
{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}
, to someone else (the “receiver”).
Ideally, I would encode this datum according to the model marginal distribution
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
(or even more ideally, the data marginal
{p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\right)}
), and pay (on average)
{\text{H}_{p{}\hat{p}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right]}}
bits.
But by the hypothesis we have been working under throughout, we cannot get a analytic expression for this quantity.

Alternatively, assuming I have learned a good generative model for the data (
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}={p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\right)}
), I ought to be able to use it to transmit
{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}
efficiently.
Intuitively, I should be able to assign the observation
{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}
to a class,
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}
, and then transmit this class
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}
along with the data “misfit,” i.e. the difference between
{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}
and the class mean.
The receiver can use the class assignment,
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}
, along with the data misfits to reconstruct
{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}
.
(Since the receiver must know my encoding scheme in order to recover these, I also pay a one-time cost to communicate the generative model to her.
We will assume this is small compared with the data to be communicated and ignore it in what follows.)

To work out the costs of such a scheme, we need to consider precisely how we assign the observation (
{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}
) to a class (
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}
).

Prima facie
, the optimal candidate class might seem to be the peak of the recognition distribution, conditioned on the observation:
\argmax_{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right\}
—in a GMM, the cluster to which the observed datum is most likely to belong.
Assuming we design our encoding optimally, i.e. according to the model source distribution,
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
, the average optimal cost of encoding classes assigned in this way is the average of the surprisal,
-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
, under a Dirac delta situated at the mode of the recognition distribution, conditioned on the observation, all averaged under the (data) distribution of observations.
If we call this averaging distribution

{{}\check{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\right)}:=\delta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}-\argmax_{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right\}}}\right){p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\right)}
, then we can call the cost
\text{H}_{{}\check{p}{}\hat{p}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right]}
.
The average optimal cost of encoding the data misfit, in turn, is the surprisal of the emission distribution,
-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
, averaged under the same joint distribution; call it
\text{H}_{{}\check{p}{}\hat{p}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right]}
.
Thus the total cost is
\text{H}_{{}\check{p}{}\hat{p}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right]}+\text{H}_{{}\check{p}{}\hat{p}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right]}
.

The excess cost of hard cluster assignments.

How does this compare to the optimal cost of using the model marginal?
To find out, we rewrite the latter in terms of the model recognition, source, and emission distributions:

\begin{split}{{\text{H}_{p{}\hat{p}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right]}}}&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{-\log\frac{{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}{{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
&=\text{H}_{{}\check{p}{}\hat{p}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\phi}}}}}}\right]}+\text{H}_{{}\check{p}{}\hat{p}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\phi}}}}}}\right]}-\text{H}_{{}\check{p}{}\hat{p}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\phi}}}}}}\right]}.\end{split}

(6.26)

Here we suppose
{{}\check{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\right)}
to be some generic recognition distribution, e.g. optimized during the E step of general EM.
It could, for example, be the actual model recognition distribution,
{{}\check{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\right)}={{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
; we shall consider this shortly.
But suppose (as we have been) that we have no access to
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
.
Accordingly, we try to replace the model-recognition cross entropy,
\text{H}_{{}\check{p}{}\hat{p}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\phi}}}}}}\right]}
, in Eq.
6.26
with the proxy-recognition entropy,
\text{H}_{{}\check{p}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\phi}}}}}}\right]}
.
Adding their relative entropy to both sides, we have

\begin{split}{\text{H}_{p{}\hat{p}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right]}}&+\KL\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{{}\check{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)}\middle|\middle|{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right\}\\
&=\text{H}_{{}\check{p}{}\hat{p}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\phi}}}}}}\right]}+\text{H}_{{}\check{p}{}\hat{p}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\phi}}}}}}\right]}-\text{H}_{{}\check{p}{}\hat{p}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\phi}}}}}}\right]}+\KL\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{{}\check{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)}\middle|\middle|{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right\}\\
&=\text{H}_{{}\check{p}{}\hat{p}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\phi}}}}}}\right]}+\text{H}_{{}\check{p}{}\hat{p}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\phi}}}}}}\right]}-\text{H}_{{}\check{p}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\phi}}}}}}\right]}.\end{split}

(6.27)

Now the right-hand side makes no reference to the model recognition distribution.
And both sides are, of course, the free energy.

Let us consider some choices for the proxy recognition distribution.
In “first-order general EM,” discussed above, we use the Dirac delta, situated at the mode of the model recognition distribution.
Clearly,
\text{H}_{{}\check{p}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\phi}}}}}}\right]}=0
under this choice—and the right-hand side of Eq.
6.27
becomes precisely the cost of communicating the observed data in the sender-receiver game, under hard assignment of data to the posterior mode!
Thus we can say precisely how much we “overpay” in comparison to the mininal cost,
{\text{H}_{p{}\hat{p}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right]}}
: the KL divergence between the Dirac delta and the true model recognition distribution.

An excess cost under the true model recognition distribution?

Alternatively, suppose we
can
compute the model recognition distribution, and set
{{}\check{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\right)}={{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
.
Then the KL divergence vanishes, and the cost reduces to Eq.
6.26
.
How can we interpret the terms on the right-hand side in light of the sender-receiver game?
We have seen that “recognizing” the latent states with Dirac deltas at the posterior modes corresponds to hard assignment in a sender-receiver game, e.g. of cluster identities.
Likewise, the use of a non-deterministic (non-Dirac) recognition distribution corresponds to
soft
assignment of data to latent states.
In particular, the first term in the cost,
\text{H}_{{}\check{p}{}\hat{p}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\phi}}}}}}\right]}
, corresponds to
sampling
from the model recognition distribution; in the GMM, to assigning the observation
{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}
to a cluster randomly, according to
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
.

For concreteness, we can imagine doing so according to the classic precedure: passing a sample from a uniform distribution through the inverse cumulative distribution function.
This might seem like a terrible idea, since sampling appears to be “adding noise”—but we shall see shortly how to recoup our loss.
The second term in the cost,
\text{H}_{{}\check{p}{}\hat{p}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\phi}}}}}}\right]}
, accrues from encoding the “data misfits”—in the GMM, between the observation and the (randomly) chosen cluster mean.

It seems I will pay more than the optimum; to be precise,
\text{H}_{{}\check{p}{}\hat{p}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\phi}}}}}}\right]}
bits more.
This might be surprising, since we have assumed our model to be a good one, but perhaps it is the price of randomly assigning observations to latent states.
Is there a way to recoup these lost bits?

Getting bits back.

There is.
For each “message” I send, the receiver can reconstruct
{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}}
from the sample
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}
and the data misfit.
But she can also recover the number used to sample
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}
—by (in our concrete example) computing the cumulative probability of
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}
.
Furthermore, the uniformly distributed numbers need not have been random; they could be any data we also wish to send to the receiver.
(We might have to transform them first to distribute them uniformly; this is an implementation detail.)
The value of this “refund” will be precisely the cross entropy,
\text{H}_{p{}\hat{p}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right]}
, of the recognition distribution, which we used to draw our samples, so upon getting these bits back, I will indeed have paid the minimal cost given by Eq.
6.26
.

Thus, if the recognition distribution is maximally entropic—if, that is, each of the data are equally well assigned to each cloud of points—then the assignments I actually make communicate lots of information.
At the other pole, if the model assigns data to clouds strictly, then this “extra channel” communicates no extra information.
Most Gaussian mixture models will be somewhere between these extremes, in which case, under the stochastic assignment of latent variables, the receiver will get some bits back.
But in every case the total cost will be
{\text{H}_{p{}\hat{p}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right]}}
.

The encoding costs under proxy recognition distributions.

If I
can’t
compute the model recognition distribution, I can still use this “bits-back” argument, but the more general Eq.
6.27
, rather than the special case of Eq.
6.26
, applies.
As when approximating the recognition distribution with a Dirac delta, the minimal extra cost will always be the relative entropy of the proxy and model recognition distributions,
\KL\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{{}\check{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)}\middle|\middle|{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right\}
.
But unlike the case of the Dirac delta, the receiver will generally have to apply for bits back in order to reach this minimal cost.
Is there an additional cost for sending the proxy recognition distribution, which the receiver needs to acquire these bits?
No: she can simply train her own model on all the
({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}})
pairs she has received or reconstructed.

Practical applications of the bits-back argument to compression.

See Duda 2009, Havasi 2018, Kingma/Abbeel/Ho ICML 2019, Townshend ICRL 2019.

6.8
EFH-like models

Section
6.8.1

We return now to the undirected models of Chapter
3
.
Recall from our introduction to latent-variable density estimation above, Section
6.2
, one of the motivations for introducing latent variables into our density-estimation problem: they can provide flexibility in modeling the observed data.
Directed graphical models, and therefore expectation-maximization, are appropriate when we have furthermore a notion of how these latent variables are (marginally) distributed, and how the observations are conditionally distributed given a setting of the latent variables; that is, when we have some idea of both
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
and
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
.
In moving to models based on the architecture of the exponential-family harmonium (EFH), we trade an assumption about the form of
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
for one about the form of
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
.

In a way, however, this is a misleading description of our assumptions.
For the directed generative models of the kind lately discussed, our construction of
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
(and, perhaps,
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
) is often—though not always—motivated by some problem-specific semantic facts about the latent variables.
In the (admittedly toy) problem introduced above, for example, the source is a Bernoulli distribution
because the hidden variable is taken to be the outcome of a coin flip
.
By contrast, when adopting the harmonium as our latent-variable density estimator, we seldom have concrete ideas about the form of
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
, and its selection is motivated mostly by mathematical convenience.
Perhaps we require the hidden variables to have support in a certain set (e.g., the positive integers), but even this is uncommon.
Most often, the individual elements of the vector of hidden units have no precise semantic content; rather, the vector as a whole represents no more than a kind of flexible set of parameters.
After learning, they can be interpreted as detectors of “features” of the data, but there is still usually no
a priori
reason to assume that these features should be distributed according to
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
.
That is one reason why the RBM is so much more common than other exponential-family harmoniums: a binary vector will generally be just as suitable to represent features as, say, a vector of positive integers.
In this, EFHs have a strong kinship with deterministic neural networks.

In any case, we do specify a form for
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
, at the expense of freedom to choose
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
.
Unfortunately, as discussed in Section
3.1
, this method for making inference tractable (indeed, trivial), renders the joint distribution intractable.
In particular, it is now the joint distribution, as well as the marginals over
both

\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}
and
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}
, that is specifiable only up to the normalizer (“partition function”).
We therefore lose the ability to take (exact) expectations under these distributions, and are left with Monte Carlo techniques.
But we have also undermined our ability to draw samples:
In directed models, we simply sample from the source distribution (which we have) and then use this sample to sample from the emission probability (which we also have).
In the harmonium, it is necessary instead to Gibbs sample—which can at least be performed layer-wise rather than unit-wise, but which nevertheless is painfully slow.
So whereas for directed models, drawing one sample vector from the joint distribution requires one pass through all the variables, it may require (depending on the mixing rate of the Markov chain induced by the sampling procedure) scores to hundreds in the EFH.

Is the price of the trade worthwhile?
Learning in the EFH and related models will require inference, naturally, but it will also require an expectation under the intractable model joint distribution.
This expectation can be replaced with a sample average, but generating samples is, as we have just noted, equally difficult.
However, after deriving the exact learning procedure (Section
6.8.1
), we shall consider an alternative, approximate learning procedure, which obviates (or at least reduces) the need for prolonged Gibbs sampling (Section
6.8.2
), allowing us to have our cake and eat it, too.
The EFH also enjoys an even more remarkable property, which is an ability to be composed hierarchically, along with a guarantee that this improves learning (more precisely, it lowers a bound).
We shall explore such models, called “deep belief networks,” in Section
6.8.3
.

In what follows, we derive learning rules as generally as possible, usually for Boltzmann machines
tout court
, for which they can often be expressed elegantly, if not solved simply.

6.8.1
Minimizing relative entropy for exponential-family harmoniums

Relative-entropy minimization in energy-based models.

We start with joint distributions of a rather general form known as a Boltzmann distribution
4
4

4

The negative energy was originally referred to as the “harmony”
[
25
]
, and omitting the negative sign does indeed frequently make the formulae prettier.
But the base measure has already spoken for
h
, as entropy has for
H
—the capital
\eta
being indistinguishable from a capital
h
.
So we work with energy,
E
, instead.

:

\begin{split}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}&=\frac{1}{Z({\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})}{\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{-E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}\right\}},\\
Z({\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})&=\sum_{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}{}}\sum_{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}}{}}{\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{-E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}\right\}}.\\
\end{split}

(6.28)

This is the generic form of distributions parameterized by undirected graphical models, although in this case we have made no assumptions about the underlying graph structure—indeed, we have not yet even distinguished the roles of
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}
and
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}
.
Critically, without further constraints on the energy, the normalizer,
Z({\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})
, is intractable for sufficiently large
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}
and
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}
, because the number of summands in Eq.
6.28
is exponential in
|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}|+|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}|
.
In some special cases of continuous random variables, the calculation of the normalizer may reduce to tractable integrals, but these cases are exceptional.

The goal, as usual, is to minimize the relative entropy of the distributions of observed data,
{p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\right)}
, and of model observations,
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
.
The method is to follow its gradient in parameter space.
Recalling from Eqs.
4.6
and
6.3
:

{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}\KL\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)}\middle|\middle|{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right\}\approx{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{-{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}},

(6.29)

where the average is under the “hybrid” joint distribution
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}{p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\right)}
.
This is the point at which we opted, above, to use EM.
The problem was that the average under a distribution that invokes parameters
makes the right-hand side, in general, difficult to evaluate.
Sometimes, indeed,
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
is not even available.
Here it certainly is, and we make bold and try to apply Eq.
6.29
directly to the Boltzmann distribution, Eq.
6.28
.
Continuing the progression:

\begin{split}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{-{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\log Z}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}+{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{E}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
&={\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\log Z}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}+{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{E}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
&=\frac{1}{Z}{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}\sum_{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}{}}\sum_{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}}{}}{\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{-E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}\right\}}+{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{E}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
&=-\sum_{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}{}}\sum_{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}}{}}\frac{1}{Z}{\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{-E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}\right\}}{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{E}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}+{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{E}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
&=-\mathbb{E}_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{E}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}}}\right]}+{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{E}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
&\approx{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{E}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}-{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{E}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{}}}\\
\end{split}

(6.30)

where in the last line we note (as always) the move from expectation to sample average, under the model joint, as an approximation.
In words, the gradient is equal to the difference between averaging a quantity under one distribution as opposed to another.
The quantity is the gradient of the energy.
The distributions are the “hybrid joint distribution,” constructed by combining the data distribution (over
\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}
) with the model’s recognition distribution (over
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}
); and the model distribution.

KL-minimization in the harmonium.

Now we make an assumption about the roles of
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}
and
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}
.
As we saw in Section
3.1
, constraining the conditional distributions
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
and
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
only to be in exponential families and to be self-consistent implies that they take the form

\begin{split}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}&=h(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}})\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{b}}+\bm{\mathbf{W}}_{{}\hat{y}{}\hat{x}}\bm{{\mathit{{U}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}})}}\right)^{\text{T}}\bm{{\mathit{{T}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}})-A({\bm{{\mathit{\eta}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}))}}\right\},\\
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}&=k(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}})\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{c}}+\bm{\mathbf{W}}_{{}\hat{y}{}\hat{x}}^{\text{T}}\bm{{\mathit{{T}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}})}}\right)^{\text{T}}\bm{{\mathit{{U}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}})-B({\bm{{\mathit{\zeta}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}))}}\right\}.\end{split}

(6.31)

In this case, the joint distribution will take the form of a Boltzmann distribution, Eq.
6.28
, with energy

E({\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}})=-\bm{{c}}^{\text{T}}\bm{{\mathit{{U}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}})-\bm{{b}}^{\text{T}}\bm{{\mathit{{T}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}})-\bm{{\mathit{{U}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}})^{\text{T}}\bm{\mathbf{W}}_{{}\hat{y}{}\hat{x}}^{\text{T}}\bm{{\mathit{{T}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}})-\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(h(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}})k(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}})}}\right).

(6.32)

The parameter gradients are therefore just the elegant

{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{E}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mathbf{W}}_{{}\hat{y}{}\hat{x}}}}}=-\bm{{\mathit{{T}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{U}}}}^{\text{T}}\hskip 36.135pt{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{E}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{{b}}}}}=-\bm{{\mathit{{T}}}}\hskip 36.135pt{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{E}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{{c}}^{\text{T}}}}}=-\bm{{\mathit{{U}}}}^{\text{T}}.

(See Section
A.1
in the appendices for a refresher on derivatives with respect to matrices.
For brevity, from here on, we omit the arguments of the sufficient statistics.)
Making this substitution into Eq.
6.30
and using Eq.
6.29
yields:

\begin{split}{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\KL\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)}\middle|\middle|{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right\}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{\mathbf{W}}_{{}\hat{y}{}\hat{x}}}}}&\approx{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{T}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{U}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{}}}-{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{T}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{U}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\KL\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)}\middle|\middle|{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right\}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{{b}}}}}&\approx{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{T}}}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{}}}-{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{T}}}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}\\
{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\KL\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)}\middle|\middle|{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right\}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\bm{{c}}^{\text{T}}}}}&\approx{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{U}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{}}}-{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\bm{{\mathit{{U}}}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}{}}}.\end{split}

(6.33)

Evidently, learning is complete when the expected sufficient statistics of the model and data (or, more precisely, the model and the hybrid joint distribution)
match.
However, the elegance of these equations disguises a great difficulty, alluded to at the outset:
The first average in each pair is under the joint distribution, which we can compute only up to the intractable normalizer.
This certainly rules out setting these derivatives to zero and solving for the parameters in closed formed.
Although we can use gradient descent instead, this doesn’t relieve us of the need to evaluate, at least approximately, these averages.
One obvious possibility is Gibbs sampling, and indeed the conditional distributions Eq.
6.31
are generally chosen so as to make block sampling possible—sample the entire vector
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}
given
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}}
and the entire vector
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{y}}}}}
given
{\bm{{\mathit{{}\hat{x}}}}}
.

6.8.2
The method of contrastive divergence

We reiterate the major limitation to the learning algorithm just described.
We can see from Eq.
6.33
that traveling the gradient of the KL divergence between data and model distributions requires expectations under the model joint or marginals (in the positive terms).
Since this integral or sum usually cannot be computed analytically, the only obvious way to take it is via Gibbs sampling from the model.
Although EFHs are designed to facilitate such a sampling procedure—the lack of intralayer connections makes it possible to sample all of the hidden variables conditioned on the visible ones, and vice versa—it will still generally take many steps of sampling to “burn in” the network, and then even more for subsequent thinning to get independent samples.
And then there is a second problem: a procedure based on sampling will introduce variance into our estimate of the gradient; and furthermore, this variance depends on the current value of the parameters (
{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}
).
Hinton likens it to “a horizontal sheet of tin that is resonating in such a way that some parts have strong vertical oscillations and other parts are motionless.
Sand scattered on the tin will accumulate in the motionless areas even though the time-averaged gradient is zero everywhere”

[
11
]
.

Here is an alternative that appears, at first glance, to be very crude
[
12
]
.
We recall the reason that Gibbs sampling requires a long burn-in period: our choice of initializing vector, being arbitrary, may have very low probability under the model.
The burn-in travels down the Markov chain away from it towards the “thick” parts of the distribution we aim to sample.
What if we had a better way to initialize the chain?
In particular, suppose we initialize it at
samples from the data distribution
, and then take only a few steps along the Markov chain.
Certainly, late in training, when the model distribution resembles the data distribution, this is a sensible procedure.
But early in training, data samples are unlikely to have high probability under the model.
Still, perhaps this “bias”—toward the regions of observation space with high probability under the data distribution—is a small price to pay for the reduction in variance that would have been accumulated through many steps of Gibbs sampling.

To try to make this more precise, we write down a mathematical expression for our approximation
[
11
]
.
We replace the averaging under the model distribution in Eq.
6.30
(for Boltzmann machines generally; or, for EFHs in particular, in Eq.
6.33
) with averaging under a distribution we call

{p^{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{1}},{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{1}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
.
This is perhaps best understood in terms of sampling.
Make four successive draws, from: first the data distribution,
{{p}^{0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{Y}^{0}}}}}{}}}\right)}
; then the model recognition distribution,

{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{0}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{Y}^{0}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
;
followed by the model emission,

{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{1}}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{0}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
;
and finally from the recognition distribution again,

{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{1}}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{1}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
.
The first two draws are frow
{p^{0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{0}},{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{0}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
; the second two are from
{p^{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{1}},{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{1}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
.
5
5

5

Note that to draw any sample from
{p^{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{1}},{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{1}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
requires first drawing new samples
{\bm{{\mathit{x}}}^{0}},{\bm{{\mathit{y}}}^{0}}
from
{p^{0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{0}},{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{0}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
; for any single sample
{\bm{{\mathit{x}}}^{0}}
, drawing from the model emission and then the recognition distributions amounts to drawing from the conditional distribution,

{p^{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{1}},{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{1}}\middle|{\bm{{\mathit{x}}}^{\ignorespaces 0}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
.

Under this notational convention, the “hybrid joint distribution,”
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{X}^{0}}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{Y}^{0}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}{{p}^{0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{Y}^{0}}}}}{}}}\right)}
, would be called
{p^{0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{0}},{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{0}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
: the distribution formed by “zero” steps of Gibbs sampling (more precisely, one half step).
And likewise, taking
n
steps yields samples from
{p^{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{n}},{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{n}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
.

At the end of this Markov chain lies the model distribution; hence we can say
{p^{\infty}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{X}^{\infty}}}}}\bm{{\mathit{{{Y}^{\infty}}}}}}}\right)}={{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{X}^{\infty}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{{Y}^{\infty}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}

[
11
]
.
In fact, for the remainder of this section, we shall use
p^{\infty}
for the model joint.

Now substituting
{p^{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{1}},{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{1}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
for
{p^{\infty}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{X}^{\infty}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{{Y}^{\infty}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
in Eq.
6.30
:

\begin{split}{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{L}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{?}}}{{=}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{E}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{0}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{{Y}^{0}}}}}{}}}-{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{E}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{1}}{},{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{1}}{}}}\\
&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{E}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}-{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{E}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{1}}{},{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{1}}{}|{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{0}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{{Y}^{0}}}}}{}}}}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{0}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{{Y}^{0}}}}}{}}},\end{split}

(6.34)

where in the last line we have emphasized that samples from
{p^{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{1}},{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{1}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
would typically be nested inside the sampling from
{p^{0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{0}},{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{0}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
.
We have supposed that the learning rule in Eq.
6.34
is the gradient of some (unknown) loss function
\mathcal{L}
.
This is clearly an attractive learning rule from the perspective of number of required samples, but will it produce good density estimators?

Contrastive divergence for energy-based models.

From here we try to work backwards: what loss function might yield this gradient?
Hinton proposes “contrastive divergence,” i.e., the difference between the original KL divergence and a different one:

\mathcal{L}({\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}):=\KL\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{{p}^{0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{Y}^{0}}}}}}}\right)}\middle|\middle|{p^{\infty}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{Y}^{0}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right\}-\KL\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{p^{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{n}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}\middle|\middle|{p^{\infty}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{n}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}}}\right\}.

(6.35)

This is an interesting quantity.
First of all, it is positive.
Why?
Because the
n
-step distribution is “trapped” between
p^{0}
and
p^{\infty}
.
That is, Gibbs sampling down the Markov chain brings distributions closer to
p^{\infty}
, which will consequently diverge less from “later” than “earlier” distributions.
So the second term is always less then the first, and their difference always positive.
Second, the contrastive divergence is zero at precisely the same points as the first term—i.e., the classic KL penalty (as in Eq.
6.29
).
This can be seen by noting that the contrastive divergence can be zero only when the divergences are equal, i.e. when
p^{n}\approx p^{0}
.
But this implies that
p^{0}
is an equilibrium distribution for a Markov chain with the transition operator given by a single step of Gibbs sampling under the model.
Further applications of this transition operator—additional steps of sampling from the model—cannot change this equilibrium distribution.
So in this case,
p^{0}
would be equal to
p^{m}
for all
m
, and both divergences would vanish.

We might take this as a specific instance of a more general procedure: if the gradient of a function is difficult, replace it with a different function with the same minimum but an easier gradient.
But
is
the gradient of the contrastive divergence easier to evaluate than the gradient of the KL divergence?

We have already worked out the gradient of the first KL divergence, Eqs.
6.29
and
6.30
, but we break the computation into steps to reuse portions for the derivative of the second divergence.
For brevity, we suppress the arguments:

\begin{split}{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{L}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}&=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\KL\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{p^{0}\middle|\middle|p^{\infty}}}\right\}-\KL\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{p^{n}\middle|\middle|p^{\infty}}}\right\}}}\right]\\
&=\frac{\partial{\KL\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{p^{0}\middle|\middle|p^{\infty}}}\right\}}}{\partial{p^{0}}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{p^{0}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}+\frac{\partial{\KL\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{p^{0}\middle|\middle|p^{\infty}}}\right\}}}{\partial{p^{\infty}}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{p^{\infty}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}-\frac{\partial{\KL\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{p^{n}\middle|\middle|p^{\infty}}}\right\}}}{\partial{p^{n}}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{p^{n}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}-\frac{\partial{\KL\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{p^{n}\middle|\middle|p^{\infty}}}\right\}}}{\partial{p^{\infty}}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{p^{\infty}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\\
&=0+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{E}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{0}}{},{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{0}}{}}}-{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{E}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{X}^{\infty}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{{Y}^{\infty}}}}}{}}}}}\right)-\frac{\partial{\KL\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{p^{n}\middle|\middle|p^{\infty}}}\right\}}}{\partial{p^{n}}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{p^{n}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{E}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{n}}{},{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{n}}{}}}-{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{E}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{X}^{\infty}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{{Y}^{\infty}}}}}{}}}}}\right)\\
&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{E}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{0}}{},{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{0}}{}}}-{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{E}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}}}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{n}}{},{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{n}}{}}}-\frac{\partial{\KL\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{p^{n}\middle|\middle|p^{\infty}}}\right\}}}{\partial{p^{n}}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{p^{n}}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}.\end{split}

(6.36)

On the third line we have substituted for the fourth term by exploting the analogy with the second term (which we worked out in Eq.
6.30
above).

The final line is seemingly quite close to the desired gradient, Eq.
6.34
.
Is this close enough?
Hinton reports that extensive simulation seems to indicate that this last term is small in practice, and that in any case its inclusion rarely changes the approximate version (Eq.
6.34
) by more than 90 degrees; that is, neglecting it seldom makes the gradient point in the wrong direction
[
11
]
.
And inefficiencies introduced by ignoring this term can perhaps be alleviated by choosing an
n
that balances the cost of these inaccurate gradients against the computational costs of protracted Gibbs sampling.
After all, since the final term must disappear as the number of these “contrastive-divergence steps” approaches infinity, as long as there is no reason to suspect a discontinuity in the Markov chain, there will be some finite number
n
of CD steps that will shrink the nuisance term to an arbitrarily small size.

Contrastive divergence for the harmonium.

The derivation in Eq.
6.36
is general to models based on the Boltzmann distribution.
Applying the contrastive-divergence approximate learning rule, Eq.
6.34
, to exponential-family harmoniums just means substituting
{p^{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{n}},{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{n}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
for
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}{},\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}{};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
in Eq.
6.33
.
Here we write the one-step version, emphasizing the source of the samples of
{p^{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{1}},{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{1}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
by rearranging the averaging brackets as we did in Eq.
6.34
.
We also let the sufficient statistics be the vector random variables themselves,
\bm{{\mathit{{U}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}})=\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}},\bm{{\mathit{{T}}}}(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}})=\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}
(what we will later have occasion to call “linear sufficient statistics”) in order to facilitate a biological interpretation:

\begin{split}\Delta\bm{\mathbf{W}}_{{}\hat{y}{}\hat{x}}&\propto{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{0}}{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{0}}^{\text{T}}-{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{1}}{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{1}}^{\text{T}}}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{1}}{},{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{1}}{}|{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{0}}{},{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{0}}{}}}}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{0}}{},{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{0}}{}}},\\
\Delta\bm{{b}}&\propto{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{0}}-{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{1}}}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{1}}{}|{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{0}}{},{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{0}}{}}}}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{0}}{},{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{0}}{}}},\\
\Delta\bm{{c}}&\propto{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{0}}-{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{1}}}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{1}}{}|{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{0}}{},{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{0}}{}}}}}}\right\rangle_{{\bm{{\mathit{{X}}}}^{0}}{},{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}^{0}}{}}}.\end{split}

(6.37)

Thought of as a neural network, we can say that each sample vector drives activity in the layer above, which reciprocally drives activity in the layer below, which drives activity in the layer above; after which “synaptic strengths” change proportional to pairwise correlations (average products) between the pre- and post-synaptic units, with the initial contributions being Hebbian and the final contributions being anti-Hebbian.
For CD
n
,
n>1
, the reciprocal activity simply continues for longer before synaptic changes are made.

Some examples.

6.8.3
Learning in deep belief networks

We summarize our progress in learning in EFH-like models.
Since we will shortly need to introduce subscripts to the random variables, we avoid clutter from here on by setting aside contrastive divergence and working out the results in terms of the original loss function (see Eq.
6.29
)—allowing us to dispense with superscripts.
Here, then, we return to using
{}\hat{p}
rather than
p^{\infty}
for the model distribution.

To recapitulate: the EFH represents, in some sense, a generative model more “agnostic” than the directed ones learned with EM: rather than committing to a source distribution of latent variables,
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
, it licenses a certain inference procedure to them,
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
.
The price we pay for the ease of inference is difficulty in generating samples from, or taking expectations under, the unconditional (joint or marginal) model distributions.
Computing the gradients for latent-variable density estimation (Eq.
6.30
) requires just such operations, so the price may appear to be steep.
However, an alternative procedure which requires very few samples, the method of contrastive divergence (Eq.
6.34
), works well in practice.
It can be justified either as a crude approximation to the original learning procedure, or as the approximate descent of an objective function slightly different from, but having the same minimum as, the original.

Supposing even that our learning procedure does minimize the KL divergence between data
{p\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}}}\right)}
and model
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}}}\right)}
distributions, that divergence may not be zero.
The statistics of the data may be too rich to be representable by an EFH, or (say) one with a given number of hidden units.
It would be nice to have a procedure for augmenting this trained EFH that is guaranteed to improve performance, i.e., to decrease KL divergence.

From incomplete cross entropy to free energy.

Our derivation of just such a procedure begins with an idea: perhaps only certain parts of the trained, but insufficient, EFH want improvement.
Specifically, we shall commit ourselves to using the generative distribution it has learned,
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}_{0}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{0};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{0}}}\right)}
, but try to replace the source distribution,
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{0};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{0}}}\right)}
.
(Here we have numbered the parameters—and the corresponding random variables—in anticipation of the introduction of more.)
Of course, the whole point of the EFH is that the source distribution is learned only implicitly, so it may seem an infelicitous candidate for replacement.
In particular, if we substitute a new source distribution,
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{1};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{1}}}\right)}
, we shall seemingly be unable to avail ourselves of the EFH learning procedures—e.g., the contrastive-divergence parameter updates, Eq.
6.37
.
These rules require the recognition distribution, and although we have one consistent with
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{0};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{0}}}\right)}
and
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}_{0}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{0};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{0}}}\right)}
, to wit
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{0}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}_{0};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{0}}}\right)}
, we do not have in hand one consistent with
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{1};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{1}}}\right)}
and
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}_{0}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{0};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{0}}}\right)}
—call it
{\ignorespaces{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{1}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}_{0};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{0},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{1}}}\right)}
.
Nor is it clear how we should get one (although the reader is invited to think about this).

So we turn for inspiration to expectation-maximization (EM), the learning procedure for directed graphical models, which is in some sense what our problem has become, since we now have a parameterized generative distribution,
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}_{0}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{0};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{0}}}\right)}
, and source distribution,
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{1};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{1}}}\right)}
, even if the latter has yet to be specified.
Recall from Section
6.3
that our problem may be simplified if we trade descent in the cross entropy of the model under the data for descent in an upper bound, the “free energy.”
The slack in the bound is taken up by the KL divergence between a proxy recognition distribution and its true, but possibly uncomputable, counterpart.
In the present case, let us select the true recognition distribution under the
original
EFH, i.e.,
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{0}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}_{0};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{0}}}\right)}
, as the proxy.
As we have lately noted, abandoning this model’s source distribution presumably spoils the validity of its recognition distribution in the augmented model—that is, in general,

{\ignorespaces{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}_{0};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{0},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{1}}}\right)}\neq{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}_{0};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{0}}}\right)}
.
To emphasize that the right-hand side of the inequality (the original model recognition distribution) is a proxy for the left-hand side, we will subsequently refer to it as
{{}\check{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}_{\ignorespaces 1}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}_{0}}}\right)}
.

Let us rewrite the free energy so as to separate out the pieces that are dependent on
{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{0}
and on
{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{1}
:

\begin{split}F(\{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{0},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{1}\},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{0})&={\text{H}_{p{}\hat{p}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}_{0};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{0},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{1}}}\right]}}+\KL\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{{{}\check{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}_{1}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}_{0}}}\right)}\middle|\middle|{\ignorespaces{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}_{1}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}_{0};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{0},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{1}}}\right)}}}\right\}\\
&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}_{0};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{0},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{1}}}\right)}+\sum_{{\bm{{\mathit{{}\check{x}}}}_{1}}{}}{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{}\check{x}}}}_{1}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}_{0};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{0}}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\log{{}\check{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{}\check{x}}}}_{1}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}_{0}}}\right)}-\log{\ignorespaces{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\bm{{\mathit{{}\check{x}}}}_{1}}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}_{0};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{0},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{1}}}\right)}}}\right]}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}_{0}{}}}\\
&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\log{{}\check{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}_{1}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}_{0}}}\right)}-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}_{1},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}_{0};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{0},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{1}}}\right)}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}_{1}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}_{0}{}}}\\
&={\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\log{{}\check{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}_{1}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}_{0}}}\right)}-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}_{0}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}_{1};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{0}}}\right)}-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}_{1};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{1}}}\right)}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}_{1}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}_{0}{}}}.\end{split}

(6.38)

Therefore:

{\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{1}}}}F(\{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{0},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{1}\},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{0})={\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{1}}}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{-\log{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}_{1};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{1}}}\right)}}}}\right\rangle_{\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}_{1}{},\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}_{0}{}}}.

(6.39)

Does this help?

Unrolling the EFH.

Now we make a very clever observation
[
11
]
.
Consider the “hybrid” graphical model in Fig. 6.5a.
†
†
margin:

I
nsert figure of (a) EFH, and (b) inverted EFH with one directed connection “hanging” off.

†
†
margin:

Insert figure of (a) EFH, and (b) inverted EFH with one directed connection “hanging” off.

Samples from this model can be generated by, first, prolonged Gibbs sampling from the “inverted” EFH (notice the labels), followed by a single sample of the bottommost
layer via the directed connections.
But if the weight matrix defining these connections is the transpose of the EFH’s weight matrix, then samples from this model are identical to samples from the visible layer of a standard EFH, Fig. 6.5a!
Likewise, the recognition distributions from the models in Figs. 6.5a,b,
{{}\check{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}_{\ignorespaces 1}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}_{0}}}\right)}
and
{\ignorespaces{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{1}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}_{0};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{0},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{0}}}\right)}
, are identical.

Suppose then we let the source distribution over
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{1}
in the model we seek to learn,
{{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{1};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{1}}}\right)}
, be the marginal distribution over
\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{1}
in a second, inverted EFH, as in Fig. 6.5b.
Moreover, let us initialize the parameters of this EFH at those of the first, fully trained EFH:
{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{set}}}{{=}}{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{0}
.
Then at this point in training, the KL divergence in the first line of Eq.
6.38
vanishes, and following the gradient in Eq.
6.39
decreases—at least at first—the cross entropy of interest,
{\text{H}_{p{}\hat{p}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}_{0};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{0},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{1}}}\right]}}
.
This is like a single M step of standard EM.
There is no subsequent E step because there is no obvious way to bring the proxy recognition distribution,
{{}\check{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\check{X}}}}}_{\ignorespaces 1}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{Y}}}}_{0}}}\right)}
, back into agreement with the true recognition distribution,
{\ignorespaces{}\hat{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{X}}}}}_{1}\middle|\bm{{\mathit{{{}\hat{Y}}}}}_{0};{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{0},{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{1}}}\right)}
.
We shall employ instead a different iterative improvement scheme (see below).

As soon as
{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{2}
is changed, the bound on this cross entropy can loosen: the KL divergence between proxy and model recognition distributions in the first line of Eq.
6.38
may increase.
But, as in EM, so far from being problematic, this loosening would correspond to an even greater decrease in the cross entropy, since by hypothesis we are decreasing the free energy.
That is, if this KL divergence grows, it does so at the expense of cross entropy; and if it doesn’t (i.e., it stays at zero), then cross entropy still decreases.
It is true that subsequent movement along this gradient can
decrease
the KL divergence between proxy and model recognition distributions, and therefore allow the cross entropy to increase, all while lowering the free energy.
But the cross entropy can never increase past its value at the beginning of the changes of
{\bm{{\mathit{\theta}}}}_{1}
, since here the bound is tight, and all changes lower this bound.
